===== PDF page 3 of 215 =====

Résumé

    La plasticité cristalline est gouvernée par le mouvement, l’interaction et l’organisation
collective des dislocations. Aux grandes échelles, ces mécanismes microscopiques sont généralement
représentés par des lois constitutives continues. À l’échelle atomique, ils peuvent être résolus
explicitement, mais seulement sur des échelles de longueur et de temps limitées. Entre ces
deux limites se trouve un régime mésoscopique dans lequel la plasticité reste discrète, collective,
intermittente et dépendante de l’histoire. Cette thèse étudie ce régime à l’aide du Modèle
Tensoriel Mésoscopique (MTM), un modèle énergétique construit à partir d’une densité d’énergie
invariante par les symétries du réseau cristallin et implémenté ici dans un cadre éléments finis.
    Dans une formulation purement lagrangienne, le maillage peut subir un verrouillage topologique
lorsque les éléments deviennent fortement distordus. Nous développons une procédure de recon-
nexion, fondée sur des basculements locaux d’arêtes, afin de maintenir une topologie adaptée à
la configuration déformée tout en préservant l’information plastique pertinente. Cette approche
supprime les artefacts associés à un maillage fixe et donne accès, aux grandes déformations
plastiques, à un régime intermittent quasi stationnaire marqué par la formation de grains.
    La deuxième contribution de la thèse est une construction unique par plus court chemin
permettant de sélectionner la décomposition élastoplastique à valeurs entières du MTM pour un
réseau carré. La branche plastique est sélectionnée comme la plus courte séquence admissible de
cisaillements simples de SL(2, Z) reliant la métrique courante au domaine élastique fondamental.
Nous montrons que cette sélection est unique, sauf sur les frontières entre puits, où apparaît la
dégénérescence attendue entre branches voisines. Cette construction fournit une règle cohérente
pour transporter l’histoire plastique lors des changements de représentation du maillage.
    Enfin, ce travail analyse les avalanches plastiques produites sous chargement quasi statique.
Les statistiques des chutes d’énergie sont utilisées pour caractériser leur intermittence et leur
comportement critique, avec une attention particulière portée à l’extraction des exposants
critiques. Nous montrons que les statistiques d’avalanches ne sont que faiblement affectées par le
choix de l’algorithme de minimisation et par la reconnexion du maillage. Cette faible sensibilité
suggère un comportement critique robuste.




Mots clés : plasticité cristalline ; dislocations ; modèle tensoriel mésoscopique ; décomposition
élastoplastique ; histoire plastique ; reconnexion de maillage ; avalanches plastiques ; criticalité.

                                                 i

===== PDF page 4 of 215 =====

Abstract

    Crystal plasticity is governed by the motion, interaction and collective organization of
dislocations. At large scales, these microscopic mechanisms are usually represented by continuum
constitutive laws. At the atomic scale, they can be resolved explicitly, but only over limited length
and time scales. Between these two limits lies a mesoscopic regime in which plasticity remains
discrete, collective, intermittent and history-dependent. This thesis studies this regime using
the Mesoscopic Tensorial Model (MTM), an energy-based model built from a lattice-symmetry-
invariant energy density and implemented here within a finite-element framework.
    In a purely Lagrangian formulation, the mesh can undergo topological locking when elements
become strongly distorted. We develop a reconnection procedure, based on local edge flips, to
maintain a topology adapted to the deformed configuration while preserving the relevant plastic
information. This approach removes artifacts associated with a fixed mesh and gives access,
at large plastic deformation, to a quasi-stationary intermittent regime marked by grain-like
organization.
    The second contribution of the thesis is a unique shortest-path construction for selecting the
integer-valued elastoplastic decomposition of the square MTM. The plastic branch is selected as
the shortest admissible sequence of SL(2, Z) simple shears connecting the current metric to the
fundamental elastic domain. We show that this selection is unique, except on well boundaries,
where the expected degeneracy between neighboring branches appears. This construction provides
a consistent rule for transporting plastic history during changes of mesh representation.
    Finally, this work analyzes plastic avalanches produced under quasistatic loading. The
statistics of energy drops are used to characterize their intermittency and critical behavior, with
particular attention paid to the extraction of critical exponents. We show that avalanche statistics
are only weakly affected by the choice of minimization algorithm and by mesh reconnection. This
weak sensitivity suggests a robust critical behavior.




Keywords : crystal plasticity; dislocations; Mesoscopic Tensorial Model; elastoplastic decompo-
sition; plastic history; mesh reconnection; plastic avalanches; criticality.

===== PDF page 5 of 215 =====

Preface


Metals remain central to modern engineering because they combine strength, ductility, durability
and manufacturability. Their properties, however, are not fixed. Small changes in composition,
heat treatment, microstructure or sample size can produce large changes in mechanical response.
Understanding this sensitivity is one of the central motivations of metallurgy and crystal plasticity.
    The modern understanding of crystalline solids began with the discovery of X-ray diffraction
and the identification of crystal structures in the early twentieth century [1]. A crystalline solid
is a material whose atomic arrangement has long-range periodic order: a small unit is repeated
in space to form a lattice. Familiar examples include minerals like diamond, ruby and quartz,
but also metals like iron, copper and aluminum.
    Once the crystalline structure of materials had been identified, a natural next question was
how such materials deform. In 1926, Frenkel estimated the shear stress required to plastically
deform a perfect crystal by assuming that one atomic plane slips coherently over another [2]. The
predicted stress was of order µ/(2π), where µ is the shear modulus, several orders of magnitude
larger than typical experimental yield stresses [3]. The resolution came in 1934, when Polanyi,
Orowan and Taylor independently introduced dislocations as the lattice defects responsible for
plastic flow in crystals [4–6]. This insight made it possible to understand plastic deformation
not as the simultaneous sliding of whole atomic planes, but as the motion, multiplication and
interaction of line defects.
    Dislocations explain why plastic flow can occur at stresses far below the ideal strength. They
can glide along crystallographic slip planes, so plastic deformation can proceed through the
motion and multiplication of defects that are already present. It does not require an entire
atomic plane to slip coherently. However, predicting the collective response of many interacting
dislocations remains difficult. In a short essay on work hardening, Cottrell argued that work
hardening is a more difficult problem than turbulence because the relevant defects are neither
atomic-scale point objects nor smooth continuum fields, but extended, discrete dislocation lines
whose collective organization remains mechanically important across many scales [7]. The
same difficulty remains visible in modern theories of crystal plasticity. Dislocation mechanisms
are microscopic, while the quantities of practical interest are often macroscopic: yield stress,
hardening, ductility, localization and failure. Even today, a fully predictive theory of metal
hardening directly from the collective behavior of dislocations remains incomplete [8].
    Different modelling approaches therefore emphasize different scales. Continuum crystal-
plasticity models and their finite-element implementations are effective for polycrystals and
engineering-scale boundary-value problems, but they usually incorporate microscopic mechanisms
through phenomenological constitutive laws and internal variables [9, 10]. Atomistic simulations
resolve lattice defects directly and allow many relevant mechanisms to emerge, but they are
limited in accessible length and time scales [8, 11]. Discrete dislocation dynamics represents
dislocations explicitly and reaches larger scales than atomistic simulations, but still relies on

                                                 iii

===== PDF page 6 of 215 =====

mobility laws and local rules for core-scale interactions [12]. Between these descriptions lies the
mesoscopic regime, where plastic flow is too discrete and intermittent to be treated as a smooth
average, but too collective and extended to be followed atom by atom.
     This thesis is concerned with this mesoscopic regime. More specifically, it studies the
Mesoscopic Tensorial Model (MTM) proposed by Salman et al. [13]. The MTM is based on
two simple ingredients: an energy functional respecting the crystalographic symmetries of the
underlying crystal lattice, and a Cauchy–Born kinematic description of the atomic deformation
inside each mesoscopic element. On this basis, the model describes nonlinear elasticity in a
finite strain framework and, through its non-convex lattice-invariant energy landscape, accounts
for plastic events and the nucleation of dislocations [13, 14]. Its main advantage is that elastic
interactions, lattice symmetry and plastic rearrangements are encoded within a consistent
energetic formulation, without the need to impose ad hoc interaction rules for individual defects.
     The MTM has already been successfuly used to study scaling properties in crystal plastic-
ity [15]. It has also been extended to describe crack nucleation and the interaction between
cracks and dislocation fields, making it a useful framework for investigating the ductile-to-brittle
transition [16]. However, despite these advantages, the method has so far mostly relied on a purely
Lagrangian description. At large deformation, a fixed mesh can become severely distorted and
may produce artifacts, in particular when dislocations cross [16]. Moreover, studying deformation
paths and relating plasticity to history requires a consistent way to track the accumulated plastic
deformation during large plastic rearrangements.
     The work presented here addresses these issues through three contributions. First, it develops
a mesh-reconnection strategy that allows large-deformation simulations to proceed without the
purely Lagrangian mesh becoming locked by highly distorted elements. Second, it introduces
an elastoplastic decomposition procedure that separates elastic deformation from the plastic
history carried by lattice transformations. This makes it possible to track deformation paths
under severe plastic deformation. Third, it studies plastic avalanches in the model and examines
how the extracted statistical properties depend on modelling and numerical choices.
     The manuscript is organized as follows. Chapter 1 reviews the background needed for the the-
sis: crystal plasticity across scales, plastic intermittency, criticality, and modelling strategies from
atomistic to continuum descriptions. Chapter 2 introduces the theoretical framework of the
Mesoscopic Tensorial Model, including its lattice-invariant energy density and finite-element im-
plementation. Chapter 3 discusses mesh locking in the MTM and develops the mesh-reconnection
procedure used to overcome it. Chapter 4 presents the elastoplastic decomposition introduced
in this thesis, together with the transport of plastic history across changes of representation.
Chapter 5 analyzes plastic avalanches in the model, with particular attention to power-law
classification, exponent extraction, and the influence of mesh reconnection and minimization
algorithms on avalanche statistics. The manuscript is completed by appendices on technical
proofs and calculations supporting the MTM formulation.
     The work carried out during this thesis has resulted in several scientific communications,
including one journal article submitted for publication [17] and two oral presentations at inter-
national conferences: the GDR IDE annual meeting, held in Grenoble in 2024, and the ESMC
2025, held in Lyon.

General conclusions                                                                               160

1       Crystal plasticity across scales
The plastic response of crystalline solids is intrinsically multiscale. Dislocations, line defects in the
crystal lattice, carry irreversible deformation at the microscopic level, while their collective motion
produces macroscopic yield, hardening and permanent strain [3]. The difficulty in modeling this
response is that explicitly resolving dislocations and their interactions can be computationally
expensive at the scales of interest. Replacing their activity by a smooth constitutive response,

                                                        1

===== PDF page 13 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


however, can miss important fluctuations: even micrometer-scale samples may deform through
distinct strain bursts rather than continuous plastic flow [18].
    At large scales, the contributions of many dislocations, grains and local events can combine
into an approximately smooth stress–strain curve. Continuum models describe this response by
separating its reversible and irreversible parts. Classical elasticity describes the reversible response,
including the long-range fields of defects. Continuum plasticity relates the averaged irreversible
response to the evolving defect structure through internal variables such as accumulated plastic
strain and dislocation density [9, 10].
    At smaller scales, this averaging can break down. In micropillar experiments, plastic strain
accumulates through distinct bursts of dislocation motion that remain visible in the measured
stress–strain curve [18]. A single burst, also referred to as a dislocation avalanche, can contribute
substantially to the deformation of a small specimen. This is the mesoscopic modeling problem:
individual bursts contribute too large a fraction of the deformation to be averaged out, while
resolving their microscopic mechanisms remains computationally expensive.
    Existing modeling approaches address this problem through different compromises. Atomistic
simulations resolve defects but are limited in length and time [8, 11]. Discrete dislocation
dynamics (DDD) retains interacting lines but requires mobility laws and rules for short-range
reactions [12]. Continuum crystal plasticity reaches larger scales by averaging much of the
intermittent activity [9]. These trade-offs motivate the search for descriptions that retain
collective plastic events at a manageable computational cost.
    This chapter develops the argument from physical mechanisms to event statistics and modeling
approaches. The present section introduces elasticity and plasticity through complementary
macroscopic and microscopic examples, then discusses dislocations, obstacles and size effects.
The remaining sections of this chapter follow that progression: Section 2 introduces plastic
avalanches, their statistics and the interpretation of criticality. Section 3 reviews how different
models retain or average the relevant physics, leading to the Mesoscopic Tensorial Model (MTM).
Finally, Section 4 brings these points together to explain the questions and organization of the
thesis.

1.1    Elasticity and plasticity
Bending a metallic spoon provides a familiar example of the distinction between elasticity and
plasticity (Fig. 1.1a). A thin spoon bends slightly under a small force and returns to its original
shape when the force is removed. This recoverable deformation is elastic. If the spoon is bent
further, some deformation remains after unloading; this permanent part is plastic. The spoon
thus illustrates how elastic and plastic deformation can contribute to the mechanical response,
with unloading revealing which part is recoverable.
    The tensile test in Figure 1.1b expresses the same distinction through a stress–strain curve.
A small tensile load produces elastic strain. The onset of irreversible deformation defines the
yield stress σy . Beyond point E, plastic strain accumulates; unloading from P brings the stress
back to zero but leaves a permanent extension. The two examples therefore identify plasticity
through the part of the deformation that does not recover.

                                                   2

===== PDF page 14 of 215 =====

1. Crystal plasticity across scales



                                                                  Elastic           Plastic deformation


                                                                  Linear




                                                     Stress
                                                                            E   P
                                                                    H                              Fracture
                                                                                                    point



                                                              0      < 1%                 Strain

                          (a)                                                       (b)




                          (c)                                                       (d)

Figure 1.1: Illustrations of plastic deformation and crystal plasticity across scales. (a) Bent
spoon: macroscopic irreversible plastic deformation. (b) Typical stress–strain curve of a ductile
material, showing elastic deformation, yielding and plastic flow. The hypothetical sample shows
a linear response up to H, and remains elastic up to E. Beyond E, unloading from point P leaves
the sample permanently deformed. Further loading eventually leads to fracture. (c) Grains and
dislocations in a square two-dimensional MTM simulation. The color denotes the energy density;
short blue lines mark dislocation cores. (d) Slip steps, surface offsets left by slip in a deformed
cadmium single crystal, illustrating the crystallographic nature of plastic slip [19].


     The distinction between recoverable and permanent deformation also has a microscopic energy-
based interpretation. In a crystal, small elastic distortions perturb the atomistic configuration
about a stable crystal configuration, raising the energy; after unloading, the crystal can return to
that configuration. Slip can instead move a region of the crystal into another lattice-equivalent
configuration, leaving a permanent relative displacement. These collective rearrangements
connect the energy landscape to the macroscopic deformation. Their classification still depends
on unloading: crossing an energy barrier does not by itself establish that the resulting deformation
is irreversible. Chapter 5 returns to the distinction between recoverable rearrangements and
irreversible deformation when classifying simulated events.
     Connecting lattice rearrangements to the measured yield stress requires an account of how
slip proceeds through a crystal. In 1926, Frenkel estimated the stress required to slide one atomic
plane coherently past another in an ideal crystal [2]. This coherent-slip estimate was far above the
yield stresses of many real crystals [3], showing that the idealized mechanism could not account


                                                 3

===== PDF page 15 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


for their low-stress plastic deformation. In 1934, Orowan, Taylor and Polanyi independently
proposed dislocation-based explanations of plastic slip [4–6]. A moving localized defect allows slip
to advance progressively through the crystal, rather than requiring a simultaneous translation of
the entire plane. Dislocations therefore provide a mechanism for low-stress plastic deformation
that Frenkel’s coherent-slip calculation omits.

1.2    Crystal dislocations
In three dimensions, a dislocation is a line defect in a crystal lattice whose motion can produce
plastic slip. It is characterized by its local line direction and its Burgers vector b, which measures
the lattice mismatch associated with the defect [20]. The Burgers vector has a geometric definition
through a Burgers circuit: one compares the same sequence of lattice steps in a perfect crystal
and around a dislocation. Steps that close in the perfect crystal leave a closure failure around
the defect; that mismatch defines b, with its sign fixed by the circuit convention.
    Screw and edge character are the two limiting cases, distinguished by the orientation of the
Burgers vector relative to the local line direction. For a screw dislocation, the two are parallel,
and the atomic planes form a helical arrangement around the line. For an edge dislocation, they
are perpendicular; the line marks the termination of an extra half-plane of atoms. A general
dislocation can have mixed character, which can vary along a curved line [20].
    Figure 1.2 illustrates how edge-dislocation motion produces progressive slip. In panels (b)–(d),
the defect forms (nucleates) and moves along the slip plane A by glide. After it exits in panel
(e), the material above that plane has been translated relative to the material below it by b.
The Burgers vector therefore specifies both the lattice mismatch detected by the circuit and the
magnitude and direction of the slip left by dislocation passage [21].
    The idealized Volterra construction represents this slip by cutting the body along a surface
bounded internally by the dislocation line, displacing the opposite faces by b, and rejoining them.
In a real crystal, the lattice mismatch is spread over a finite dislocation core. Outside that core,
continuum elasticity describes the long-range fields. For an isolated straight dislocation in an
infinite linear-elastic medium, the stress and strain fields decay as 1/r, where r is the distance
from the line, and become formally singular as r → 0. This singularity marks the breakdown of
continuum elasticity at the core [20].

1.3    Microstructural obstacles and hardening
The stress required for dislocation motion depends on the obstacles encountered during glide.
In polycrystalline metals, one source of obstacles is the boundary between grains: regions with
different lattice orientations, as illustrated in Figure 1.1c. These grain boundaries can impede
dislocation motion and increase the stress required for plastic flow. Reducing the grain size
brings these barriers closer together and can promote dislocation storage near the boundaries. In
many polycrystalline metals, this raises the flow stress, an effect commonly called Hall–Petch
strengthening [22, 23].
    Solute atoms and impurities provide another obstacle to glide. Their local lattice distortions


                                                  4

===== PDF page 16 of 215 =====

1. Crystal plasticity across scales


                       Lx                                Tx

                                       Lz

          Ly

      y

  z            x      (a)                            (b)                          (c)



                                                         b
                                                     A



                                 (d)                            (e)
Figure 1.2: Illustration of a dislocation, adapted from [21]. (a) A perfect simple cubic crystal
with dimensions Lx × Ly × Lz . (b) A traction Tx is applied to the top surface, while the bottom
surface is fixed. An edge dislocation nucleates from the left surface. (c)–(d) The dislocation
glides across the crystal. (e) Final state after the dislocation exits through the right surface.
The slip plane is indicated by A, and the region above the slip plane has been displaced relative
to the lower part by the Burgers vector b.


can impede dislocation motion, so increasing their concentration can increase the stress required
for plastic flow [24–28]. In ferrite, the body-centered cubic phase of iron, dissolved carbon
occupies interstitial sites between iron atoms. The resulting lattice distortions impede dislocation
motion and provide one contribution to steel strengthening [29].
    Dislocations can themselves provide obstacles to further glide. In three dimensions, disloca-
tions whose lines pass through a given glide plane are called forest dislocations. Their interactions
with mobile dislocations can form dislocation junctions, where intersecting lines join and resist
further motion [30–32]. The resulting resistance to glide is called forest hardening. As deforma-
tion multiplies dislocations, their density—the total line length per unit volume—can increase,
reducing the average spacing between obstacles. Continued deformation can then require higher
applied stress: this is strain hardening, also called work hardening. Plastic deformation thus
generates an evolving obstacle structure even in pure single crystals, without requiring grain
boundaries or solute atoms [33, 34].
    These mechanisms describe plasticity in three-dimensional crystals. To study collective
plastic events in a simpler setting, the simulations developed in this thesis restrict displacements
and deformation to a plane. In this two-dimensional model, edge dislocations represent lines


                                                 5

===== PDF page 17 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


normal to the simulated plane. Screw dislocations would require out-of-plane displacement, and
the three-dimensional junction formation involved in forest hardening is also absent. Defect
interactions and rearrangements can still change the internal stress and resistance to later motion,
but any hardening must be interpreted through the mechanisms actually represented.

1.4    The mesoscopic gap in crystal plasticity modeling
Plastic deformation is mediated by dislocations, but their collective motion is difficult to coarse-
grain. At the atomic scale, bonding, intrinsic lattice resistance to glide (lattice friction), and defect
interactions are identifiable ingredients [21]. At the macroscopic scale, constitutive laws describe
many averaged responses [9]. Between these limits, local events interact through long-range
elastic stress fields, and past deformation changes the defect structure through which later events
move [32]. The response is therefore nonlocal and history-dependent; the modeling question is
how much of that evolving structure an averaged description must retain.
    An overall dislocation density does not fully describe the evolving defect structure [35]. Within
the same volume, two populations with the same total line length can differ in their spatial
arrangement, sources able to generate new dislocations, network connectivity and obstacles.
They can therefore respond differently to the same loading [12]. A continuum theory must retain
enough information about the defect structure to predict the behavior of interest, while reducing
the number of microscopic degrees of freedom.
    For intermittent flow, the region of material and time interval used for averaging also matter.
Combining events over a larger region or longer interval can make individual bursts less visible.
When a single burst accounts for a substantial part of the deformation within that region or
interval, replacing the burst by a smooth strain rate hides a visible feature of the response [36,
37]. Averaging can still be useful if the aim is to predict only the mean deformation, but a model
designed to retain the fluctuations must resolve individual bursts. The choice of averaging region
and interval therefore depends on what the model is meant to predict, as well as microstructure,
boundaries and loading conditions.
    The mesoscopic modeling problem is therefore to reduce the microscopic description without
losing the defect information or plastic fluctuations needed for the response of interest. DDD
retains individual dislocation lines [12], whereas continuum-dislocation theories replace the
lines by evolving density fields [35, 38]. The model survey later in this chapter explains the
different details retained by these approaches. Experiments provide observations against which
the models can be assessed. Figure 1.3 collects complementary examples: size-dependent strength
in micropillars, distinct acoustic-emission bursts and broad distributions of event sizes [18,
39]. Models intended to reproduce these observations must account for both the specimen-size
dependence and the intermittent deformation.

1.5    Size effects and the mesoscopic regime
The micropillar experiments in Figure 1.3a show two striking effects [18]. First, smaller samples
generally require higher stresses to deform than bulk samples. Second, the plastic response


                                                   6

===== PDF page 18 of 215 =====

1. Crystal plasticity across scales




                                                                                     (b)




                                (a)
                                                                                     (c)

Figure 1.3: Size effects, intermittency and scale-free plastic fluctuations in crystal plasticity. (a)
Compression of metallic micropillars of different diameters, showing intermittent stress–strain
curves and the increase of flow stress at small scales, adapted from [18]. The bulk sample is
2.6 × 2.6 × 7.4 mm. (b) Acoustic-emission activity during plastic deformation of a cadmium
single-crystal sample, illustrating the intermittent character of plastic flow, adapted from [39].
(c) Probability distributions of avalanche energies in different crystalline materials, showing
broad power-law-like regimes, adapted from [39].


becomes intermittent, with discrete strain bursts or stress drops. In a small volume, fewer
dislocation sources and collective events contribute to the response, so an individual localized
burst need not be hidden by the activity of many others.
    These size effects depend on what limits dislocation-mediated plasticity: the availability of
mobile dislocations, or their ability to move once they are present. In small samples, dislocations
may escape through free surfaces faster than they can multiply or be stored. This dislocation
starvation can raise the flow stress and make deformation depend on intermittent source activation.
With strong obstacles or lattice friction, dislocations may instead remain available but difficult
to move, producing obstacle-controlled plasticity and hardening. The relative importance and
interplay of these mechanisms remain actively discussed [40–42].
    Irradiated micropillars help distinguish these two limiting mechanisms. Irradiation introduces
defects such as dislocation loops, clusters and cavities that obstruct glide and reduce the distance
dislocations can travel before meeting an obstacle [43]. If those internal obstacles dominate the
response, changing the specimen diameter should have less influence on strength. Experiments
show this weakening or suppression of the usual size effect: unirradiated pillars strengthen as
their diameter decreases, while irradiated pillars can have nearly size-independent strengths over
the same range [44, 45]. These observations support a shift from dislocation-availability-limited
plasticity toward an obstacle-controlled regime.
    One primary practical motivation for understanding these size effects is that small-scale


                                                  7

===== PDF page 19 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


metallic crystals can be much stronger than their bulk counterparts. If reducing the relevant
structural length scale can raise strength, then size effects offer a possible route to stronger
materials by design. This idea is central to metallic metamaterials and nanolattices, where the
goal is to embed nanoscale load-bearing elements inside a larger architected structure. In such
materials, strength is not expected to arise from size reduction alone, but from coupling between
nanoscale strengthening, structural hierarchy and load redistribution across many interacting
elements [46, 47]. Recent work on architected materials has therefore emphasized the need to
bridge length scales from nanoscale features to macroscopic components, since many of the most
attractive properties of micro- and nanoarchitectures remain difficult to transfer to application-
relevant dimensions and loading conditions [48]. More broadly, nanolattices, nanoarchitected
materials, and ultra-strong nanostructured metals all point toward the same design opportunity:
size-dependent strengthening may become a useful design principle if it can be made reliable
beyond isolated small-volume tests [49–52].
    Transferring small-specimen strengthening to a macroscopic architecture is not automatic.
The same reduction in length scale that increases strength also changes the mechanisms of
deformation and failure. Nearly defect-free nanoscale crystals may sustain very high stresses,
but plasticity can then be controlled by dislocation nucleation and may occur through abrupt
strain bursts, stochastic avalanches, or catastrophic collapse rather than smooth, distributed
flow [37, 52]. Similarly, architected materials can achieve unusual combinations of stiffness,
strength, and energy dissipation, but often only by managing trade-offs between strength,
ductility, stretchability, damage tolerance, and failure localization [53]. The relevant design
problem is therefore mesoscopic: one must understand how local mechanisms such as dislocation
source activation, storage, surface escape, and obstacle-controlled glide combine with collective
load sharing, localization, and failure across the architecture.


2     Criticality and intermittency in plastic flow
Plastic flow is often treated as a smooth continuum process, but this description can hide large
fluctuations [3, 54]. Under slow driving, deformation may proceed through intermittent plastic
events separated by periods of mostly elastic loading [36, 39, 55]. This section introduces these
events as plastic avalanches, explains how their statistics are characterized, and discusses why
their apparent scale-free behavior has been interpreted in terms of criticality and universality.
The central question is when plastic flow becomes scale-free and how this depends on material
structure, system size, disorder, boundaries, and modeling assumptions.

2.1   From smooth plasticity to intermittent flow
At sufficiently large scales, many microscopic rearrangements can be averaged into a continuous
strain rate, making a smooth description of irreversible flow useful. However, this continuum
picture can hide the discrete and fluctuating nature of crystal plasticity. Under slow loading, a
crystal may deform through a sequence of abrupt plastic events separated by periods of mostly
elastic loading. In this regime, plastic flow is intermittent rather than smooth.

                                                8

===== PDF page 20 of 215 =====

2. Criticality and intermittency in plastic flow


    Such intermittent dynamics is not unique to crystals. Many slowly driven dissipative systems,
in which elastic interactions compete with disorder, respond through avalanche-like events
spanning a broad range of sizes, a phenomenon often referred to as crackling noise [56, 57].
Examples include granular piles [58, 59], tectonic fault networks [60], Barkhausen noise in
ferromagnets [61], and the motion of imbibition fronts in disordered media [62]. Similar avalanche
phenomenology appears in driven systems moving through pinning landscapes (depinning), in
yielding, and in martensitic transformations. In the latter, coordinated atomic displacements
change the crystal structure without long-range atomic diffusion. In these and other driven
systems with disorder, metastable configurations, local instabilities, and redistribution of stresses
or driving forces govern the dynamics [57, 63].
    In crystals, the arrangement of dislocations, obstacles and interfaces creates a complex
energy landscape with many metastable configurations: locally stable states separated from
other arrangements by energy barriers. Under slow deformation, the system can remain trapped
until a local instability triggers a rapid collective rearrangement, producing intermittent plastic
activity observed as acoustic-emission bursts, strain bursts, stress drops, or slip steps. The
central question is therefore not only whether plastic flow can become scale-free, but also under
what material, size, loading, and modeling conditions such behavior emerges.

2.2   Plastic avalanches
A plastic avalanche is a cascade of plastic rearrangements triggered during slow loading. A
local rearrangement changes the elastic stress in the surrounding material and can destabilize
other regions, triggering further rearrangements. The cascade ends when the system reaches a
mechanically stable state [64]. In a dislocation-mediated picture, it involves coupled motion of
dislocation segments, rather than independent responses to further loading [55, 65]. An avalanche
may be localized or spatially extended, and can be characterized by its released energy, plastic
strain increment, stress drop, duration or spatial extent.
    In the quasistatic limit, loading is slow enough that each avalanche finishes before appreciable
further deformation is imposed. Numerical athermal quasistatic protocols implement this
separation by alternating small loading increments with energy minimization at fixed imposed
deformation, neglecting thermal activation [64].
    Figure 1.3b shows the acoustic-emission signature of intermittent flow in a cadmium sample.
The signal consists of separated bursts with strongly varying acoustic power. Each burst is
interpreted as elastic waves emitted by a rapid plastic rearrangement [37, 39, 55]. The same
underlying activity can be observed through its mechanical consequences: strain jumps in a
stress–strain curve (Fig. 1.3a) or slip steps at the sample surface (Fig. 1.1d). These measurements
provide complementary views of intermittent deformation [36].
    The measured event size depends on the technique. Acoustic-emission studies may analyze
burst energies or peak amplitudes, compression experiments may measure strain or displacement
bursts [36], and simulations may track stress drops, energy drops, or plastic increments [64–66].
These quantities measure different consequences of a rearrangement and need not have the same
statistical exponent [39]. Comparisons must therefore specify both the observable and how events

                                                 9

===== PDF page 21 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


are detected. In Chapter 5, we define the simulated drops and test irreversibility before selecting
the events used for avalanche statistics.

2.3   Scale-free statistics
A common way to characterize plastic avalanches is to collect many events and construct a
probability density p(x) for an event size x. Here x may denote, for example, an acoustic-emission
energy, a strain burst, a stress drop, or a displacement burst. A commonly used model for this
density over a finite scaling range is a power law,

                               p(x) = Cx−α ,       xmin < x < xmax .                           (1.1)

For 0 < xmin < xmax < ∞, normalization over this interval gives

                                         1−α
                                     
                                     
                                      1−α
                                              1−α ,       α ̸= 1,
                                  C = xmax −1 xmin
                                                     ,     α = 1.
                                     
                                     
                                         ln(xmax /xmin )
                                     


The exponent α determines the decay rate: the slope of log p(x) versus log x is −α. The quantities
xmin and xmax mark the bounds of the fitted scaling regime. Figure 1.3c illustrates such behavior
for acoustic-emission energies [39].
    The physical meaning of a power law is that there is no single characteristic avalanche size
within the fitted range. Small events are more common than large events, but events occur over
many scales. In a physical sample, however, this scale-free regime is bounded at both small and
large event sizes. At small sizes, the scaling regime may be limited by microscopic length scales
as well as by the finite resolution and detection threshold of the measurement. At large sizes,
events are limited by the system size, microstructure, boundary conditions, or hardening state,
producing an upper cutoff [37, 56, 67].
    At the lower end of the distribution, the apparent scaling regime is affected by detection limits
and event definitions. Small avalanches may fall below the experimental resolution, while limited
temporal resolution can cause nearby events to be merged. High driving rates introduce a related
problem by causing distinct relaxation events to overlap, shifting the measured distribution away
from the quasi-static avalanche limit [56, 67]. For this reason, the fitted exponent depends not
only on the underlying physics, but also on the choice of lower cutoff xmin , the fitted range, and
the statistical method used to estimate the exponent [68].
    An apparently straight segment on a log-log plot can suggest a power law, but a power
law over a limited range is not, by itself, conclusive evidence of criticality. Here, criticality
refers to collective dynamics in which plastic activity is correlated across many length scales:
rearrangements in one region influence the activity of other regions, rather than all regions
responding independently. A broad event-size distribution alone does not establish these spatial
correlations.
    Stronger evidence comes from additional scaling tests. Finite-size scaling examines how
event statistics, including their upper cutoff, change with system size. A scaling collapse tests

                                                 10

===== PDF page 22 of 215 =====

2. Criticality and intermittency in plastic flow


whether distributions from different sizes or conditions follow a common curve after the predicted
rescaling. Avalanche-duration statistics and avalanche-shape analysis examine how long events
last and how their activity varies during an event. Together, these tests constrain the scaling
description and help assess whether systems share a universality class [56, 61, 69].

2.4   Universality
Power-law exponents are often discussed through the language of universality classes. In this
framework, systems with different microscopic details may nevertheless share the same large-scale
behavior if they have the same relevant symmetries, dimensionality, conservation laws, and
interaction range [70]. The exponent is then not merely a fitting parameter, but a possible
signature of the large-scale dynamics governing the avalanches [56, 71].
    Early work on intermittent dislocation flow in ice interpreted scale-free acoustic-emission
statistics within a criticality and universality framework. Miguel et al. reported power-law-
distributed acoustic-emission energies during creep of ice single crystals and argued that the
relevant universality class should depend on effective dimensionality, basic symmetries, and
deformation mechanism [55]. In this view, simplified models may reproduce the observed scaling
when they retain the relevant coarse-grained ingredients, even if they omit much of the microscopic
detail.
    Later studies have examined how avalanche statistics change with material properties and
loading conditions. Systematic studies of intermittent plastic flow show that while an incipient
power-law regime may be robust, the measured exponent, cutoff, and crossover to large avalanches
can depend on crystal orientation, sample size, geometry, driving mode, hardening, applied stress,
strain rate, and machine stiffness [67, 72–75]. In some cases, nominally similar systems exhibit
comparable small-avalanche exponents but different cutoffs or large-event statistics, indicating
that the exponent alone does not fully characterize the avalanche dynamics. The exponent is
therefore best interpreted as one part of a broader scaling description, rather than as a fixed
label for a material class.
    A related proposal is that plastic intermittency is controlled by the ratio of an external
system size L to an internal length scale ℓ: R = L/ℓ. Weiss et al. [37] define this internal length
as ℓ = Gb/τpin , where G is the shear modulus, b is the Burgers-vector magnitude, and τpin is
the effective stress resisting dislocation motion, including lattice friction and obstacles such as
forest dislocations and solute atoms. At a separation of order ℓ, the elastic interaction stress
between dislocations, of order Gb/ℓ, is comparable to this resistance. The ratio R therefore
compares specimen size with the length set by the competition between dislocation interactions
and pinning. Weiss et al. propose that the ratio R organizes the crossover from mild fluctuations,
with a more characteristic event scale, to wild, scale-free avalanches [37]. The proposed relation
between R and intermittency links microstructure to sample size; it does not by itself fix the
exponent for every material or loading protocol.
    The resulting picture is more nuanced than a simple universality-class assignment. Broad
event distributions alone do not determine a universality class, and the exponent is not a fixed
material label. Agreement between models under comparable conditions can support a common

                                                11

===== PDF page 23 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


statistical picture, but differences in observables, cutoffs and loading must also be considered.
The next section reviews which degrees of freedom and assumptions each model retains, then
returns to their ability to generate and sample collective events.


3    Modeling approaches
Crystal-plasticity models differ in the degrees of freedom they retain and the length and time
scales they can reach. Figure 1.4 compares electronic-structure, atomistic, lattice-based, discrete-
defect, continuum and energy-based descriptions. Their scale ranges overlap because they simplify
the microscopic description in different ways. For example, the quasicontinuum (QC) method
combines atomistic and continuum regions, while phase-field crystal (PFC) is a continuum field
model with lattice-scale structure. The following subsections explain the variables, advantages
and limitations of these approaches before comparing their suitability for avalanche studies.
This comparison requires care because time has different meanings in the different descriptions:
quasistatic loading, physical dynamics, and diffusive evolution do not define equivalent time
scales.

Chapter 1. Introduction


3.1    Atomistic and lattice-based models
Molecular dynamics (MD) and molecular statics (MS) provide two different atomistic descriptions
of crystal plasticity. In classical MD, atomic trajectories are obtained by integrating Newton’s
equations of motion using forces derived from an empirical or semi-empirical interatomic po-
tential [21]. This contrasts with electronic-structure methods such as density functional theory
(DFT) [82–84] and ab initio molecular dynamics (AIMD) [85], where the interatomic forces are
obtained from an underlying quantum-mechanical description of the electronic structure.
    The central distinction between MD and MS is how they represent evolution. MD integrates
the equations of motion to generate an atomic trajectory. An isolated simulation conserves total
energy, while a simulation at prescribed temperature commonly uses a thermostat to exchange
heat with the atomic system. This heat exchange is part of the deformation protocol and can
influence the dynamics, particularly at high strain rates or during intermittent events. MS
instead relaxes the atomic positions to a local minimum of the potential energy, usually after each
increment of boundary loading. It therefore describes a zero-temperature or athermal quasistatic
response: successive mechanically stable configurations replace a physical trajectory. Without
thermal fluctuations, this description cannot represent thermally activated crossings of energy
barriers or their rates.
    In both MD and MS, the quality of the atomistic description depends on the interatomic
potential. Classical empirical potentials typically prescribe physically motivated functional forms
whose parameters are fitted to experimental data, first-principles reference data, or both, including
DFT calculations and AIMD trajectories [11, 86]. Machine-learning interatomic potentials provide
a more flexible alternative, fitting potential-energy surfaces from reference configurations while
retaining reasonable computational cost for atomistic simulations [87–89].
    One advantage of atomistic simulations is that dislocations are not prescribed as separate line
objects, but emerge from the atomic dynamics or from the energy landscape being minimized. In
MD, dislocation nucleation, glide, reactions and annihilation can therefore be governed directly
by the interatomic potential, rather than by an imposed mobility law [79, 90]. MS can identify
corresponding mechanically stable defect configurations, but it does not describe their thermal
activation or time-dependent motion. The energy-minimization procedures used in the MTM are
closer in spirit to MS than to finite-temperature MD.
    The primary challenge of atomistic simulations for crystal plasticity is scale. Modeling
every atom comes at a considerable computational cost. Even with neighbor lists, domain
decomposition, and parallel processing, the accessible system size depends strongly on the
interatomic potential, simulation duration and computational resources. DFT and AIMD are
generally better suited to small systems where an explicit electronic-structure description is
needed, while classical potentials allow larger systems at lower cost [21]. Large supercomputer
simulations can span hundreds of nanometers and, in some cases, reach characteristic lengths on
the micrometer scale [90, 91]. Because atomic vibrations require femtosecond-scale integration
steps, conventional direct MD is also limited in physical time, often to nanoseconds or, in
favorable cases, sub-microsecond trajectories [92–94]. MS avoids this time-step limitation, but it
has no physical time scale and cannot describe thermally activated kinetics.

                                                 14

===== PDF page 26 of 215 =====

3. Modeling approaches


    These scale limitations motivate reduced descriptions that retain selected consequences of
crystal periodicity without necessarily resolving a full atomistic trajectory. In lattice-based
models, crystallographic discreteness or periodicity is introduced explicitly at the level of the
model energy, geometry, or response function. This can be done through a periodic substrate
potential, as in Frenkel–Kontorova models [95, 96]; through a misfit energy on a slip plane,
as in Peierls–Nabarro models [97–99]; or through lattice sites, force constants, and the lattice
Green’s function of an explicitly discrete crystal, as in lattice-statics models of defects [100, 101].
Depending on the formulation, the reduction may come from restricting the geometry, replacing
many-body atomistic interactions by effective periodic potentials, focusing on collective variables
such as the local slip across a plane, or targeting equilibrium and driven-defect response rather
than resolving the full inertial trajectory of all atoms. In slip-plane models, this local relative
displacement is called the disregistry; it measures how far the material immediately above the
plane has slid relative to the material immediately below it. It varies along the plane through a
dislocation core, connecting regions whose relative slip differs by a Burgers vector.
    Lattice-based models therefore occupy an intermediate role: they retain selected consequences
of the crystal lattice while simplifying the atomistic description. They are useful for isolating
mechanisms associated with lattice discreteness, core spreading, lattice friction, and slip-plane
energetics. Many of the individual-defect formulations in this group are not designed to describe
the collective evolution of a large interacting dislocation population over mesoscopic volumes.
DDD addresses this problem by treating dislocations themselves as the primary objects of the
simulation.

3.2   Discrete dislocation dynamics
Discrete dislocation dynamics (DDD) represents dislocations explicitly as line defects, typically
discretized into connected segments. The degrees of freedom are therefore no longer atomic
positions, but the geometry, Burgers vectors, slip systems (combinations of slip plane and slip
direction), and connectivity of the dislocation network. The motion of this network is governed
by the stresses acting on the lines, mobility laws, boundary conditions, and rules for short-range
reactions such as junction formation and annihilation [12, 79]. These simplifications allow DDD
simulations with characteristic lengths of roughly 0.1–10 µm and simulated durations of 1 µs–1 ms.
Large three-dimensional examples include 10 × 10 × 10 µm3 work-hardening simulations [12]; see
Figure 1.4.
    The local stress exerts a Peach–Koehler force on each segment, depending on its Burgers
vector and line orientation. The stress includes contributions from the applied loading and
elastic interactions within the network, with boundary corrections used to enforce the boundary
conditions. The elastic stress fields depend on the segment geometry, elastic anisotropy and the
chosen treatment of the dislocation core [12].
    The line motion is commonly treated as overdamped: inertia is neglected, and a mobility law
maps the driving force directly to velocity. A simple mobility gives velocity proportional to force,
while more general laws account for anisotropy or thermal activation. Once the line moves, the
area it sweeps determines its contribution to plastic slip: at fixed Burgers vector, sweeping a

                                                 15

===== PDF page 27 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


larger area gives a larger plastic strain increment. As the network evolves, remeshing updates its
numerical segments, while reaction rules describe changes of physical connectivity.
    The DDD representation replaces most atomic degrees of freedom by an elastic medium
and focuses computational effort on the defect network. It therefore retains discrete dislocation
motion at scales difficult to access with MD, while relying on additional descriptions of unresolved
core processes.
    One limitation is that several processes in DDD must be supplied through constitutive
or algorithmic rules rather than emerging directly from atomistic dynamics. Nucleation and
short-range reactions may require additional modeling assumptions. For example, local rules
may specify that oppositely signed segments annihilate when they approach within a capture
distance, or that intersecting segments form a junction when their geometry and Burgers vectors
permit it. Such rules represent processes that are not resolved explicitly at the DDD scale and
are usually calibrated using experiments, theory, or lower-scale simulations such as MD [12, 79].
    Another limitation is computational. Each segment can interact elastically with many
others, so increasing the number of dislocations increases the number of interactions to be
evaluated. The fields are long-ranged compared with the short-range interactions used in many
atomistic potentials; they cannot simply be discarded using the same local cutoff argument.
Direct evaluation of all segment pairs costs O(N 2 ) for N segments, making dense networks and
large-strain simulations demanding [12]. The O(N 2 ) estimate concerns direct pairwise evaluation;
other DDD implementations can use different algorithms.
    Discrete lines can also be coupled to a continuum calculation without averaging away
individual dislocations. One such hybrid is the Discrete–Continuous Model (DCM), which
couples dislocation dynamics to the finite-element method (FEM). The discrete network supplies
the plastic flow, while the finite-element model represents the surrounding elastic medium and
enforces the boundary conditions. Slip generated by dislocation glide is transferred to the mesh
as an eigenstrain: a prescribed inelastic strain acting as a source in the elastic calculation. The
resulting stress field then drives further dislocation motion [102].
    A further reduction in the number of defect degrees of freedom requires coarse graining
the dislocation network itself. The collective effect of line motion is then represented through
continuum slip, plastic strain or dislocation-density fields, rather than by tracking each line
individually.

3.3   Continuum crystal plasticity
Continuum crystal plasticity (CP) takes the next step in coarse graining. Instead of tracking
individual dislocation lines, it describes plastic deformation using smooth fields and constitutive
laws for slip on crystallographic systems. At each material point, the local state is described by
internal variables such as accumulated slip, hardening, lattice orientation, and, in some models,
dislocation density [9, 10, 103]. Across a sample, these variables form spatial fields that evolve
with deformation. Averaging dislocation activity into continuum fields allows calculations on
polycrystalline aggregates, engineering-scale geometries and complex boundary-value problems,
especially through finite-element or spectral full-field implementations. The CP range in Figure 1.4

                                                16

===== PDF page 28 of 215 =====

3. Modeling approaches


extends from full-field simulations of grains and representative volume elements to engineering-
scale boundary-value problems. Full-field methods resolve spatial variations of the mechanical
fields; representative volume elements are sample volumes used to estimate an averaged material
response. These applications include finite-element and spectral implementations reported in [9,
23, 103, 104].
     The deformation gradient F describes the local stretches, shears and rotations. At finite
strain, elastic and plastic contributions are commonly separated through the multiplicative
decomposition F = Fe Fp . The plastic factor Fp records deformation accumulated through
crystallographic slip, while the elastic factor Fe describes the recoverable lattice distortion and
lattice rotation. The elastic distortion determines the stress, which drives further slip [9, 103].
Chapter 4 uses this kinematic structure to interpret the deformation history in the MTM.
     On each slip system, the driving quantity is the resolved shear stress: the shear component
acting along the slip direction on the slip plane. A constitutive law relates this stress to the
slip rate and the current resistance to slip [9]. Phenomenological models prescribe this relation
and how the resistance increases with accumulated slip. Slip on one system can harden both
that system and others through their interactions, giving self-hardening and latent hardening,
respectively [9].
     Dislocation-based models instead relate slip rates and hardening to the evolving defect
population. The Orowan relation links the shear rate to the mobile dislocation density, Burgers-
vector magnitude and average velocity [32, 105]. Velocity laws and density-evolution equations
are still needed to describe the response. Such models can use information from DDD to represent
slip-system interactions and hardening without resolving individual lines [106].
     These local laws are solved together with mechanical equilibrium and the boundary conditions,
commonly using finite-element or spectral methods, including FFT-based algorithms. In CPFEM,
the nodal displacements determine the local deformation gradients. The slip and hardening laws
update the internal state and supply the stress, from which the element forces are calculated.
Equilibrium requires these forces to balance the applied loading [9]. Section 4 of Chapter 2
develops the finite-element formulation used here, with the MTM energy replacing conventional
slip-based constitutive laws.
     By representing plastic deformation through smooth continuum fields rather than through
discrete objects such as atoms or individual dislocations, continuum crystal plasticity can access
a broad range of length and time scales [9, 104]. It is used in engineering applications such as
sheet-forming anisotropy, fatigue-crack initiation, and component-scale fatigue assessment [107–
110], as well as in fundamental studies of grain-scale deformation, size effects, and dislocation-
density-based hardening [22, 111, 112].
     The price of this coarse-grained description is that discrete defect events, intermittent dislo-
cation avalanches, and local core-scale mechanisms are usually averaged into a smooth response.
Active research seeks to reintroduce the fluctuations expected at smaller scales [113–118]. Con-
tinuum crystal plasticity is therefore a central modeling level in multiscale plasticity: it sacrifices
explicit defect resolution, but provides a practical framework for connecting crystallographic
deformation mechanisms to macroscopic stress-strain behavior.


                                                  17

===== PDF page 29 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


    In summary, the models considered here introduce successive levels of coarse graining: from
atoms in MD, to explicit dislocation motion in DDD, to continuum slip-system plasticity in
CPFEM. In this perspective, one can also mention homogenized polycrystal models in which
individual grain-scale fields are no longer fully resolved [119–121].
    These successive reductions are not the only ways to simplify a microscopic description.
Before turning to energy-based field models in Section 3.4 of this chapter, we consider two
alternatives: QC interpolates selected atomic degrees of freedom, while continuum dislocation
dynamics (CDD) averages dislocation geometry into fields.

3.3.1   Quasicontinuum

The quasicontinuum (QC) method retains atomistic resolution near defects and reduces degrees
of freedom in smoothly deforming regions [122]. Selected representative atoms form the nodes
of a finite-element mesh. Their displacements are the independent variables, and interpolation
determines the displacements of the remaining atoms. At full resolution, every atom is retained.
Elsewhere, the displacements of many atoms follow from those of fewer representative atoms,
which explains the computational saving.
    In the local QC formulation, the Cauchy–Born rule maps the deformation gradient in each
finite element to a corresponding homogeneous deformation of the underlying lattice. The
atomistic energy is then approximated from this local deformation, and equilibrium is found
by minimizing the resulting energy with respect to the positions of the representative atoms.
The homogeneous-lattice approximation is efficient in smoothly deforming regions, but it cannot
accurately represent the non-uniform atomic environments of dislocation cores, surfaces, and
interfaces. Mixed local–nonlocal QC formulations retain explicit atomistic interactions in such
regions and couple them to the local continuum approximation in the surrounding material. The
classical QC formulation is based on lattice statics [122], although dynamic and finite-temperature
extensions also exist [92].

3.3.2   Continuum dislocation dynamics

Continuum dislocation dynamics (CDD) is a coarse-grained counterpart of discrete dislocation
dynamics (DDD) [35]. Individual dislocation lines are no longer represented explicitly. Instead,
their local density, orientation, curvature, and transport are represented by continuous fields.
    The evolution equations account for transport of dislocation content and changes of line
length as curved dislocations move. In more detailed theories, density and curvature depend on
line orientation. Keeping only a few averaged fields leaves some of that information unspecified,
so closure approximations express the omitted quantities in terms of the retained ones. This
gives a finite set of equations that can be solved, at the cost of assumptions about the unresolved
orientation distribution [35].
    CDD is therefore distinct from conventional crystal plasticity. Crystal plasticity uses dis-
location information primarily to formulate slip and hardening laws, whereas CDD evolves
an averaged dislocation microstructure itself. The dislocation velocity is determined from a


                                                18

===== PDF page 30 of 215 =====

3. Modeling approaches


constitutive relation involving the applied and internal stresses, and the evolving CDD fields
are used to calculate dislocation fluxes, plastic slip, and internal stress. CDD thus links the
transport of averaged dislocation content to continuum plastic slip [35, 38].

3.4     Energy-based phase-field models
Energy-based field models take a different route from conventional continuum plasticity and
dislocation-density approaches. Instead of prescribing plastic flow primarily through slip-system
kinetics, mobility laws, or evolution equations for averaged defect densities, they formulate the
problem around a free-energy or elastic-energy landscape whose symmetries encode aspects of
the underlying crystal lattice. The field evolution can then give rise to structures and events that
are naturally interpreted as defects, slip, and plastic rearrangements, rather than requiring each
of them to be prescribed by separate rules. This broad group includes phase-field descriptions of
dislocations on slip planes [123, 124], phase-field crystal (PFC) models using a periodic density
field [78], and phase-field descriptions of structural transformations [125, 126].
    More recently, the Mesoscopic Tensorial Model (MTM) was introduced, in which plasticity is
associated with transitions between energy minima representing equivalent lattice states [14, 127,
128].

3.4.1    Phase-field crystal

The phase-field crystal (PFC) model describes a crystal through a continuous periodic density-like
field n(r, t), usually interpreted as a dimensionless deviation of the atomic number density from
a reference average value [78, 129]. In the crystalline state, this field is spatially periodic, with
maxima associated with lattice sites, while in the liquid state it is approximately uniform [129].
Rather than tracking individual atomic trajectories, PFC represents the lattice through a smooth
periodic density modulation. In standard PFC dynamics, the density redistributes to lower the
free energy on diffusive time scales [78, 130].
     Elasticity enters through distortions of the periodic density field. Changes in the local spacing
of density peaks and orientation mismatches between neighboring regions can increase the free
energy. For slowly varying elastic distortions, the model recovers continuum elasticity [78]. More
localized disruptions of the periodic pattern can be interpreted as defects such as dislocations
and grain boundaries.
     Evolving the periodic density field allows dislocation nucleation, interaction and annihilation
to emerge without separate line-reaction rules [78, 131]. PFC thus retains microscopic lattice
information while accessing diffusive times, providing a bridge between atomistic and continuum
descriptions.
     The main spatial limitation of PFC is that its periodic density field must still be resolved
on the scale of the lattice spacing. PFC therefore avoids atomistic trajectories and accesses
diffusive time scales, but it does not fully escape atomistic spatial resolution. The required spatial
resolution makes large three-dimensional simulations of developed plastic flow computationally
demanding. In addition, quantitative predictions for material-specific dislocation cores, Peierls


                                                 19

===== PDF page 31 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


barriers (the lattice energy barriers opposing glide), mobilities, and defect energies depend on
the chosen free-energy functional and parametrization [78].
    Amplitude phase-field crystal (APFC) formulations address the spatial cost by describing
slowly varying amplitudes of the periodic density waves, reducing the need to resolve every lattice
oscillation [78]. The combined PFC/APFC range in Figure 1.4 extends from lattice-resolved
lengths to roughly a few times 10−7 m. To keep the comparison consistent with the other
three-dimensional crystal-plasticity methods, its upper range is based on three-dimensional
simulations of defected and polycrystalline systems [78, 130]. Larger two-dimensional examples,
including micrometer-scale graphene/Moiré calculations, are excluded from that range because
they describe thin-film systems [78].

3.4.2    Phase-field reaction-pathway model

The phase-field reaction-pathway (PF-RP) model describes displacive structural transformations,
in which coordinated atomic motion changes the crystal structure. Unlike PFC, it describes the
local state through finite-strain deformation measures rather than a periodic density field. The
local deformation is represented by the deformation gradient F. A transformational internal
variable Ft describes the local change of crystal structure, while the elastic response measures
reversible distortion relative to the transformed state.
    The free energy combines reversible elasticity around the transformed state with a transfor-
mational contribution defining its stable states and connecting paths [125, 126]. This separation
keeps the elastic response reversible while Ft evolves dissipatively; the model can also retain
coupling to acoustic waves during rapid transformations.
    The transformational part of the energy describes how one crystal structure changes con-
tinuously into another. Each transformation path is a sequence of local states described by Ft ,
connecting the initial and final structures. The same transformation can produce the new struc-
ture in different orientations, called variants. The point-group symmetries of the participating
lattices generate the corresponding equivalent paths. These paths are organized into a branching
pathway tree, allowing the model to represent the variants and successive transformations through
Ft without assigning a separate scalar variable to each variant. For example, PF-RP has been
used to model the pressure-induced transformation of iron from a body-centered cubic (bcc) to a
hexagonal close-packed (hcp) structure [132].
    Martensitic transformations can occur through avalanches, with broad acoustic-emission
statistics observed experimentally [63]. PF-RP has been used to study variant selection, hier-
archical nucleation and transformations coupled to elastic waves [125, 126, 132]. The studies
cited here do not establish critical avalanche scaling for PF-RP. Its use to study transformation
criticality therefore requires further statistical investigation.

3.5     Mesoscopic Tensorial Model
The Mesoscopic Tensorial Model (MTM)[13, 127, 128, 133, 134] shares PFC’s aim of retaining
lattice periodicity and PF-RP’s finite-strain, energy-based description. In its traditional for-


                                                20

===== PDF page 32 of 215 =====

3. Modeling approaches


mulation, however, the MTM does not decompose F into elastic, plastic, or transformational
parts. Instead, the total right Cauchy–Green tensor C = FT F acts as the local state variable.
It describes changes in lengths and angles and is unchanged by a superposed rigid rotation.
For the square reference lattice used here, a primitive basis is a pair of vectors whose integer
combinations generate the lattice. The local energy density Φ is unchanged by an integer change
of this basis,
                            Φ(C) = Φ(MT CM),         M ∈ GL(2, Z).                          (1.2)

In two dimensions, the group GL(2, Z) consists of two-by-two integer matrices with determinant
±1; these change the primitive basis without changing the lattice it generates. The Lagrange
reduction algorithm selects a reduced basis of the same lattice and places its metric in a
fundamental domain, a region containing representative lattice metrics. The energy can therefore
be defined in this reduced domain and evaluated elsewhere through the reduction, generating
an infinite set of equivalent energy minima, or wells, without a separate internal variable [127].
Plastic rearrangements are represented by transitions between these wells. An illustration of this
energy landscape is shown in Figure 1.5a.
    The tensorial MTM generalizes the scalar construction proposed in [13, 133] to finite rotations,
multiple slip pathways and full lattice-basis invariance, while retaining elastic stress redistribution
between slip events.
    The main advantage of the MTM is that lattice symmetry, elasticity and plastic rearrangements
enter a single energy description. Its field is the deformation map, rather than the periodic atomic-
density field of PFC. Local gradients F are obtained from that map, so neighboring element
deformations must fit together into a continuous body; this is the compatibility constraint.
Minimizing the total energy subject to boundary conditions couples these local states and
produces long-range elastic stress redistribution, while retaining finite rotations and nonlinear
elasticity [14, 127, 134].
    Compared with DDD, the MTM does not track dislocations as explicit line objects with
prescribed mobility laws and reaction rules. Instead, dislocation-like structures appear near
regions where neighboring elements occupy different lattice-equivalent wells, with plastic events
corresponding to elements crossing barriers in the energy landscape. Defect nucleation and
annihilation can therefore emerge from the evolving deformation field without prescribing each
reaction individually [14, 127, 134].
    The tensorial state can describe several shear directions and finite rotations within the
same landscape. Different slip mechanisms correspond to different paths between wells; their
interaction depends on the wells, barriers and saddle points along those paths. The model thus
couples the mechanisms through one energy, rather than prescribing a separate phenomenological
interaction between every pair of scalar slip variables [14, 127].
    Studies of the MTM and its precursor automata linked intermittent flow to marginal stability—
states close to losing mechanical stability—as well as to avalanche statistics, finite-size scaling,
and scale-free dislocation patterns [13, 133]. Tensorial studies then examined collective nucleation
and discontinuous yielding in pristine crystals, showing how symmetry, orientation, sample
geometry and size affect the instability [14, 127, 128]. These studies establish the framework’s

                                                 21

===== PDF page 33 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


ability to generate collective plastic events.
    Later applications addressed the deformation and microstructure produced by those events.
Coordinated microslip can produce large inelastic rotations and texture formation (the develop-
ment of preferential lattice orientations), with power-law spatial correlations [134]. A formulation
using an explicit integer matrix to describe plastic distortion reproduced intermittent avalanches
with statistics comparable to theoretical predictions and DDD results [135]. Salman et al. studied
self-induced marginality in plastically deformed crystals. They interpreted avalanche statistics
before and after yield as evidence that plastic deformation generates states close to mechanical
instability [15].
    Other applications extend the questions beyond plastic-flow statistics: Ghosh et al. showed
that a transformation from a square to a triangular lattice can proceed mainly through organized
plastic slip, revealing alternating slip patterns not apparent from the final structure alone [136],
while coupled crack–dislocation models address brittle–ductile competition [16]. Figure 1.5
illustrates MTM plasticity in panels (a) and (b), a slip-plane phase-field description in panel (c),
and an MTM plasticity–fracture application in panel (d). The MTM’s applications to structural
transitions and fracture illustrate its broader scope; both topics lie outside the simulations of
this thesis.
    The compact field description does not by itself provide an independently adjustable physical
coarse-graining scale. In the formulation used in this thesis, the reference mesh spacing h
equals the elementary Burgers-vector magnitude b. The same spacing therefore limits spatial
resolution and sets the amount of elementary slip: increasing h while keeping b = h also changes
the effective Burgers vector. Chapter 2 explains this identification and its consequences for
system-size comparisons. Separating numerical resolution from crystallographic slip requires an
additional modeling construction, explored in recent work by Engrand [16]. More generally, the
correspondence between the MTM mesh and the underlying atomistic lattice remains an open
modeling question. We therefore do not assign the MTM a physical scale range in Figure 1.4.
    Finally, in a Lagrangian mesh the nodes move with the material. Keeping their connectivity
fixed retains the original node connections as the body deforms. Large slip can distort the
elements, making the mechanical calculation poorly conditioned. The retained connections can
also link nodes that no longer form appropriate neighbors, artificially obstructing later dislocation
passage. We refer to this mesh-induced blocking as topological locking. Distortion and artificial
blocking are related consequences of the fixed mesh, but they are distinct from material obstacles
such as solutes or physical dislocation junctions [16]. Chapter 3 examines element distortion and
mesh-induced blocking, and develops reconnection to update the obsolete connections.
    The MTM therefore combines a compact description of collective plastic events with unresolved
questions of scale, crystal structure and discretization. This thesis addresses the fixed-connectivity
limitation: reconnection updates the mesh neighborhoods so that earlier slip is not retained as
an artificial obstacle to later motion. Reconnection does not by itself decouple the mesh and
Burgers scales or extend the energy to crystals with a multi-atom basis.

===== PDF page 34 of 215 =====

4. Motivation
