                   (b)                                                  (c)


Figure 1.5: Examples of energy-based field models of crystal plasticity. (a) and (b) Mesoscopic
Tensorial Model simulations illustrating plastic deformation and the organization of plastic
activity in a lattice-invariant energy landscape, adapted from [14]. (c) A recent MTM simulation
of plasticity coupled to damage and fracture, showing void nucleation, crack propagation, and
surrounding dislocation structures, adapted from [16].


4    Motivation
This chapter has traced crystal plasticity from familiar permanent deformation to collective
dislocation motion and intermittent flow. The spoon and tensile test identify the macroscopic
effect; lattice rearrangements and moving defects explain its microscopic origin. Obstacles
and finite sample sizes alter how those mechanisms combine, making the passage to a smooth
constitutive response a mesoscopic coarse-graining problem.
    The modeling survey showed different ways to address that problem. Atomistic methods
resolve local mechanisms at high computational cost. DDD retains discrete interacting defects
but prescribes their mobility and short-range reactions. Continuum plasticity reaches larger
scales by averaging these processes [9, 12]. For avalanche studies, the useful compromise must
retain collective events and allow enough sampling to examine their distributions, cutoffs and

                                              23

===== PDF page 35 of 215 =====

Chapter 1. Introduction


dependence on protocol.
    The MTM supplies a compact energy-based description combining lattice symmetry, finite
rotations, nonlinear elasticity and mechanical equilibrium. Defect-like structures and plastic
rearrangements emerge from transitions between equivalent wells [14, 127, 137]. Generating
collective plastic events from the energy description makes the MTM a candidate for studying
avalanche statistics. Using it for that purpose requires a numerical formulation that permits
sustained plastic flow without introducing unintended constraints.
    The numerical limitation addressed here is the fixed Lagrangian connectivity. Large slip can
both distort the elements and leave connections that artificially block later defect motion. Such
locking can contaminate the large-strain response used to study sustained flow and avalanches [16].
The aim of this work is therefore to update the connectivity while retaining the lattice-invariant
energy description, then assess the mechanical and statistical consequences of those updates.
    Reconnection creates a second issue when newly connected elements require new reference
configurations. Updating an element’s reference configuration changes the deformation gradient
used to describe its current state, so its accumulated plastic history cannot simply be inferred
from that gradient alone. Reconnection and plastic-history transport therefore require separate
constructions. The thesis is organized around three tasks: removing artificial locking, transporting
plastic history, and determining how reconnection and relaxation choices affect avalanche statistics.
All three are studied in the two-dimensional MTM setting.
    The third task is to assess how allowing mesh connectivity to change affects avalanche statistics.
Reconnection gives access to larger deformations and different microstructural organization, but
those changes need not produce different avalanche exponents. We therefore compare both fitted
exponents and cutoffs using consistent observables, event classification and statistical tests.


5    Organization of the Thesis
Chapter 2 introduces the MTM formulation and the theoretical background needed for its
implementation. We first recall the finite-deformation framework and the geometry of two-
dimensional crystal lattices. These ingredients allow us to define the lattice-invariant energy and
explain how it describes elastic deformation and plastic rearrangements. We then present the
finite-element implementation, followed by the boundary conditions, sample preparation and
quasistatic loading procedure used in the simulations.
    In this formulation, the mesh connectivity remains fixed as the material deforms. Large
plastic deformation can therefore distort the elements and leave connections that artificially
block further dislocation motion. Chapter 3 examines this limitation and introduces mesh
reconnection to update these connections. We develop a local edge-flip method and compare it
with global Delaunay triangulation. Since the new elements also require reference configurations,
we introduce canonical reference triangles and examine the energy and stress changes associated
with reconnection. We then use numerical tests and large-strain simulations to assess the removal
of locking and study the resulting plastic flow and grain-like organization.
    The use of new reference configurations creates a second difficulty: the deformation gradient


                                                 24

===== PDF page 36 of 215 =====

5. Organization of the Thesis


of a reconnected element no longer directly records its previous plastic deformation. Chapter 4
addresses this problem by separating the elastic state from the accumulated plastic history. We
introduce an integer-valued multiplicative elastoplastic decomposition and construct it using a
plastic reduction algorithm. This decomposition provides a way to transport plastic history to
the new elements while obtaining their elastic distortion from the new geometry. We then use
these quantities to examine elastic rotations, plastic increments and local mechanical stability in
the reconnected simulations.
    Chapter 5 turns to the statistics of the collective plastic events. We first define the energy-
drop observables and explain how reversible rearrangements are distinguished from irreversible
events. This classification determines the events retained for the statistical analysis. We then
describe the fitting procedure used to estimate avalanche exponents and cutoffs, and examine
their dependence on system size. Finally, we compare different minimization algorithms and
simulations with and without reconnection to determine how these choices affect the measured
avalanche statistics.
    The general conclusions bring together the main results and discuss the limitations of the
present formulation. We then outline possible extensions to other lattice symmetries, a more
explicit treatment of coarse graining, and three-dimensional plasticity.

   In Chapter 1, we motivated the Mesoscopic Tensorial Model (MTM) as a mesoscopic, energy-
based framework for crystal plasticity. We now turn to its formulation. Throughout the thesis,

                                                    26

===== PDF page 38 of 215 =====

1. Continuum mechanics


the discussion and simulations are restricted to two-dimensional lattices. This chapter introduces
the mechanical and crystallographic notation needed to define the MTM and to implement it
numerically.
    We first recall the finite-deformation framework used in the model. We introduce the
deformation map, the deformation gradient F, and the right Cauchy–Green tensor C = FT F.
This tensor defines the kinematic metric used in the MTM. It measures changes in lengths
and angles relative to the reference configuration while being insensitive to the local rigid-body
rotation.
    We then introduce the geometry of two-dimensional crystal lattices. We describe lattice bases,
GL(2, Z) changes of basis, the Gram matrix G, and lattice reduction. These ingredients are used
to define the lattice-invariant energy density of the MTM [13, 14, 127].
    Finally, we present the finite-element framework used in the simulations. We explain how the
deformation gradient is reconstructed from shape functions and nodal positions, how element
energies, nodal forces, and stiffness matrices are assembled, and how the quasistatic boundary-
value problem is solved by energy minimization. We then describe the periodic boundary
conditions, loading procedure and sample preparation used in the collective simulations.


1     Continuum mechanics
This section introduces the continuum-mechanical notation used in the MTM. The presentation
is intentionally selective: we only introduce the objects needed later in the chapter and in the
numerical implementation. For further reading, see [138–140].

1.1   Notation and conventions
Throughout the thesis, the discussion and simulations are restricted to two-dimensional lattices.
Unless stated otherwise, all vectors have two components, and all second-order tensors and
matrices are 2 × 2.
    Unless explicitly stated otherwise, all lengths in the MTM formulation and simulations are
expressed in units of the reference square-grid spacing h. Positions, displacements and lattice
vectors therefore denote their physical values divided by h, and areas are measured in units of h2 ;
we omit separate symbols for these dimensionless quantities. The reference grid spacing is thus
unity. Scaling both reference and current lengths by the same factor leaves F and C unchanged.
The physical meaning of h and its relation to the underlying atomic lattice are discussed in
Section 4.1.
    Scalars are written in italic or Greek letters, for example Φ for the energy density. Vector
and tensor quantities are written in bold. The reference and current positions of a material
point are denoted by X and x. Second-order tensors and matrices are usually written with bold
upper-case letters, such as the deformation gradient F and the identity I.
    Upper-case indices I, J, K, L refer to Cartesian components in the reference configuration,
while lower-case indices i, j, k, l refer to Cartesian components in the current configuration.
Indices a, b, c select vectors from a specified basis, whether Cartesian or crystallographic. For

                                                27

===== PDF page 39 of 215 =====

Chapter 2. The MTM


a labeled vector, we put its label first and its Cartesian component index second: for example,
aai := (aa )i . Basis matrices collect vectors as columns, so the corresponding matrix entry is
(A)ia = aai . Superscripts in parentheses, such as X(p) , are labels and not powers.
    Repeated indices are summed unless otherwise stated. Basis-vector labels and Cartesian
component indices range over 1, 2 unless another range is specified. When the meaning is clear,
we write the basis {ea } without explicitly stating a = 1, 2.
    Square brackets specify a representation in chosen bases. For a second-order tensor T acting
in one Euclidean space, [T]A denotes the matrix of its associated bilinear form in the basis with
columns aa :
                             ([T]A )ab = aa · Tab ,   [T]A = AT TA.

These are covariant components. They differ, in a non-orthonormal basis, from the coordinate
matrix of the linear map, A−1 TA. For a linear-map representation, we instead write [T]A←B ,
where B specifies the domain basis and A the codomain basis; its matrix is A−1 TB. The
bilinear-form and linear-map matrices coincide when the same orthonormal basis is used on both
sides. When the bases and the intended representation are unambiguous, the tensor symbol
alone may denote its matrix representation.

1.2    Deformation map and deformation gradient
We consider a body in a reference configuration at time t0 and in a current configuration at
a later time t. The vectors X and x denote the positions of the same material point in the
two configurations. A deformation map φ maps the reference configuration to the current
configuration,
                                        x = φ(X).                                       (2.1)

In practice, an explicit algebraic expression for φ is rarely used. While φ describes the deformation
globally, kinematic information is typically obtained from its local variation. This local variation
is described by the deformation gradient

                                                  ∂x
                                             F=      .                                          (2.2)
                                                  ∂X

The deformation gradient determines how infinitesimal material elements are mapped from the
reference configuration to the current configuration. Consequently, it characterizes local stretches,
rotations, changes in angle, and changes in area.
    To interpret the mathematical expression of F, it can be helpful to introduce the displacement
field u(X) such that
                                          x = X + u(X).                                         (2.3)

Then
                                      ∂x   ∂X   ∂u     ∂u
                                F=       =    +    =I+    .                                     (2.4)
                                      ∂X   ∂X ∂X       ∂X
Thus, F measures how the displacement varies with infinitesimal changes in the reference position.
This interpretation is illustrated in Figure 2.1.

1.2.1    The deformation gradient F as a two-point tensor

Let {Ea } and {ea }, with a = 1, 2, be fixed orthonormal Cartesian bases for reference and current
vectors, respectively. The label a selects a basis vector; its reference or current role is identified
by E or e. We denote their Cartesian components by

                                  EaI := (Ea )I ,          eai := (ea )i .                       (2.5)

Throughout this thesis, both frames are aligned with the same laboratory axes and remain
fixed during deformation. With the basis vectors ordered along the coordinate axes, EaI = δaI
and eai = δai . These Kronecker symbols identify the basis-vector label with the corresponding
coordinate direction.
    The deformation gradient is a two-point tensor: it maps reference vectors to current vectors.
For a reference vector V and its image v = FV, the coordinate columns satisfy

                                    [v]{e} = [F]{e}←{E} [V]{E} .                                 (2.6)

Here [V]{E} and [v]{e} collect the reference and current Cartesian components, respectively.
The matrix representing F has scalar entries FiI , with the first index identifying the output
component and the second the input component. Thus,

                                             vi = FiI VI .                                       (2.7)

For the deformation map introduced above,

                                          ∂xi
                                  FiI =       ,          dxi = FiI dXI .                         (2.8)
                                          ∂XI

When these fixed Cartesian bases are understood, we also use F to denote its matrix representation.
Section 2.1 applies this convention to lattice vectors.

1.3     Objectivity and the right Cauchy–Green tensor
A constitutive description should not depend on the observer’s frame of reference. In particular,
a rigid rotation of the current configuration should not change the stored elastic energy. For
every proper rotation R ∈ SO(2), objectivity requires an energy density written in terms of F to
satisfy
                                        Φ(RF) = Φ(F).                                       (2.9)

This invariance means that F contains redundant information for the purpose of defining an
objective energy.
   The redundant rotation can be removed using the polar decomposition

                                              F = RU,                                          (2.10)

where R is a rotation and U is the right stretch tensor. We can also remove the rotation without

In the fixed Cartesian bases introduced above, its components are

Because rotation matrices are orthogonal, RT R = I, the rigid rotation cancels. The tensor C
therefore describes changes of lengths and angles between material directions while removing the
arbitrary orientation of the current configuration.
   Note that finite-deformation kinematics is essential for the MTM. Plastic rearrangements are
not infinitesimal corrections around a single reference state, but finite lattice-invariant changes
between equivalent descriptions of a crystal. This information is lost in a small-strain measure
such as

2.2    Lattices, bases, and GL(2, Z)
Crystals, lattices and group theory are closely related, but operate at different levels of abstraction.
A crystal is a physical or mathematical object with a periodic structure described by a lattice.


                                                    31

===== PDF page 43 of 215 =====

                      Reference Configuration                             Current Configuration

                                                                                                       (0)
Figure 2.2: The fixed Cartesian bases {Eb } and {eb }, together with the lattice bases {aa } and
{aa }, in the reference and current configurations. Both Cartesian frames are fixed and aligned
with the same laboratory axes; their distinct symbols identify reference and current roles.


A lattice is a discrete set of points generated by a set of vectors called a lattice basis. We
are interested in basis transformations that leave the lattice unchanged. The set of all such
transformations forms a group.
   Given a set of two linearly independent lattice vectors {aa } = {a1 , a2 }, we define the
two-dimensional lattice
                                L({aa }) = {v = na aa , na ∈ Z}.                          (2.22)

The lattice is the set of all integer linear combinations of the basis vectors, or equivalently the
Z-span of {aa }.
   The same lattice can be generated by different bases. For example, let

                      a1 = (1, 0),       a2 = (0, 1),    ā1 = (1, −1),            ā2 = (0, 1).             (2.23)

As illustrated in Figure 2.3, these bases generate the same lattice. Whenever two bases can be
written as integer linear combinations of one another, they generate the same lattice. In this
example, ā1 = a1 − a2 , ā2 = a2 , a1 = ā1 + ā2 , and a2 = ā2 .
   To formalize this idea, we represent a lattice basis by the matrix

                                                A = (a1 ; a2 ),                                              (2.24)

   whose columns are the lattice vectors. A change of lattice basis is then represented by

                                        A′ = AM,         M ∈ GL(2, Z).                                       (2.25)

   Consider the following basis change matrices:

                                              1 n                                  1 0
                                                 !                                        !
                         M     (12)
                                      (n) =       ,          M    (21)
                                                                         (n) =         .                     (2.26)
                                              0 1                                  n 1

                                                        32

===== PDF page 44 of 215 =====

2. Geometry of two-dimensional crystal lattices


Matrix M(12) (n) adds column 1 to column 2, n times. Likewise, M(21) (n) adds column 2 to
column 1. For example, applying M(12) (n) to A gives

                      A′ = A M(12) (n),           a′1i = a1i ,       a′2i = a2i + na1i .       (2.27)

Since these transformations produce integer linear combinations of the original basis vectors,
they preserve the generated lattice. The matrices M(12) (1) and M(21) (1) generate the special
linear group SL(2, Z), and any lattice is invariant under these transformations.
    However, SL(2, Z) does not contain all transformations that preserve a lattice. The basis â
in Figure 2.3 also generates the same lattice as A, but cannot be obtained from A using only
SL(2, Z) transformations. All products of M(12) (±1) and M(21) (±1) have determinant 1, and
therefore preserve the determinant of the basis. Since det A = 1, and det A b = −1, we need to
look outside SL(2, Z).
    To include transformations with determinant −1, introduce

                                                        1 0
                                                                 !
                                        M   (1)
                                                  =          ,                                 (2.28)
                                                        0 −1

which flips the sign of the second basis vector. The set {M(1) , M(12) (1), M(21) (1)} generates the
general linear group GL(2, Z). This group relates any two bases if and only if they generate the
same lattice; see Appendix B.1. This fact is important for lattice reduction, where we need to
identify all equivalent bases of the same lattice.




                          a2              ā2                                   â2
                                   a1
                                                  ā1                     â1

all generate the same lattice: L({aa }) = L({āa }) = L({âa }). The vectors are drawn from different
origins for clarity.

    A change of basis and a physical deformation have different meanings. The right action
A′ = AM, with M ∈ GL(2, Z), relabels the basis of a fixed lattice.    A physical simple shear
instead acts on the lattice vectors from the left, as A = Fγ A for horizontal shear. The same
                                                       ′   (h)

numerical matrix can occur in either construction, but its role and the side on which it acts
distinguish a change of lattice representation from an imposed deformation.

2.3   The lattice Gram matrix G
A basis matrix contains more information than is needed to describe the intrinsic geometry of
the lattice. It contains the lengths and relative angle of the basis vectors, but also the orientation
of the basis in space. If the energy density is invariant under rigid rotations, the orientation of
the basis is redundant. See Figure 2.4. This motivates the Gram matrix of the lattice basis,
                                                                                      !
                                                                        |a1 |2 a1 · a2
            Gab := aa · ab ,     or, equivalently,        G=A A=
                                                              T
                                                                                       .        (2.29)
                                                                       a1 · a2 |a2 |2

We call the Gram matrix G the lattice metric. Its indices label the chosen lattice basis vectors,
and its entries are their scalar inner products. This description does not require a reference
configuration. The matrix contains the squared lengths of the basis vectors and their mutual dot
product, but no information about the orientation of the basis. In standard tensor terminology,
G is the matrix of covariant components of the Euclidean metric in the current lattice basis
{aa }.
    Besides removing the rigid orientation of the basis, the Gram matrix also loses its handedness.
The bases ā and â in Figure 2.3 have the same Gram matrix G while having determinants of
opposite sign. The dot product determines cos(θ), where θ is the angle between the basis vectors,
but cos(θ) = cos(−θ). The Gram matrix therefore cannot distinguish bases related by a proper
rotation from bases related by a reflection. This loss of information will become relevant in
Chapter 4.

                           A                          B




Figure 2.4: Four lattices are shown: a black reference lattice in both (A) and (B), a blue
translated lattice in (A), and a red rotated lattice in (B). In the absence of external constraints
or fields, the energy density associated with the lattice is invariant under rigid translations (A)
and rigid rotations (B); therefore, neither the position nor the orientation of the lattice affects
the energy density. See Appendix B.2.



2.4   Relation between G and C
The Gram matrix G and the right Cauchy–Green tensor C are closely related but have different
interpretations. The matrix G describes the geometry of a selected lattice basis, while C describes
the deformation of material line elements relative to a reference configuration.


                                                 34

===== PDF page 46 of 215 =====

2. Geometry of two-dimensional crystal lattices

                                                         (0)
    When the current lattice vectors satisfy aa = Faa , as in Eq. (2.19), the lattice metric is
also the matrix of covariant components of C in the reference lattice basis. For vectors labeled a
and b,                                         
                                              (0)               (0)
                 Gab = aa · ab = Faa(0) · Fab       = aa(0) · Cab = ([C]A(0) )ab .          (2.30)

   The matrix of C in the reference Cartesian basis is

                                          [C]E = ET CE.                                        (2.31)

Its entries are ([C]E )ab = Ea · CEb = EaI CIJ EbJ ; they coincide numerically with the Cartesian
array CIJ for the chosen coordinate axes. Using A(0) = EQ, the matrix form of Eq. (2.30) is
therefore                                       
                                 G = QT ET CE Q = QT [C]E Q.                               (2.32)

This relation holds for any reference lattice basis under the stated correspondence. Thus, for a
specified reference basis and correspondence, G and C contain equivalent metric information.
For the normalized square reference, Q = I, G is numerically equal to [C]E .

2.5   Configurational and kinematic representations
Figure 2.5 summarizes the configurational and kinematic representations introduced in this
chapter. The indicated reduction in degrees of freedom is a change of representation, not a
physical loss of degrees of freedom. On the configurational side, a two-dimensional lattice
including its position in space can be described by three points (a, b, c). Passing to a basis
A = (a1 ; a2 ) removes translations, and passing to the Gram matrix G = AT A removes the rigid
orientation when the energy is rotation-invariant.
    The same hierarchy appears in kinematics: φ → F → C = FT F. The deformation gradient
removes the translation contained in the affine map, while C removes the rigid rotation.
    The lattice metric G describes the geometry of a current lattice basis, whereas the right
Cauchy–Green tensor C describes current inner products of material vectors relative to a chosen
reference configuration. The configurational description does not require a material reference,
but is related to the kinematic description once the reference lattice basis and its deformation
correspondence are specified.

2.6   Lattice reduction and reduced metric space
Figure 2.3 shows several bases that describe the same lattice. Since the basis is not unique,
it is useful to choose a preferred representation. Bases consisting of short, nearly orthogonal
vectors tend to provide a clearer description of the lattice geometry. For example, it is difficult
to recognize the lattice spanned by â1 = (5, 3) and â2 = (13, 8), but it is immediate if the same
lattice is represented by a1 = (1, 0) and a2 = (0, 1).
     In two dimensions, a reduced basis can be characterized by successive minima: its first vector is
a shortest nonzero lattice vector, and its second is a shortest vector independent of the first [141].
A reduction algorithm RL selects a representative among these bases. The reduced basis need

Figure 2.5: A schematic illustration of different representations of a lattice. The top row shows
(from left to right) three points (a, b, c), two vectors a1 , a2 , and the lengths and dot product
between the vectors a1 and a2 . The second row indicates the dimensionality of each representation
and the reduction in degrees of freedom. The asterisk on −1 reflects an additional missing bit of
information: one cannot discern the solid or dashed configuration. See Section 2.3 for further
discussion. The third row shows configurational properties: a triple of points (a, b, c), a basis
A, and its Gram matrix G = AT A. The fourth row shows corresponding kinematic properties:
affine deformation φ, deformation gradient F and right Cauchy–Green tensor C = FT F.


not be unique, especially at symmetric lattices; a canonical choice requires additional sign and
tie-breaking conventions. For the energy construction in Section 3, the essential requirement is
that lattice-equivalent inputs receive the same energy, including when reduction selects different
representatives on identified boundaries.
    Geometrically, the idea behind the lattice-invariant energy landscape is the following: consider
the space of all lattice bases. For two-dimensional lattices, this space has four dimensions. Passing
to the metric G removes the orientation of the basis and leaves three independent entries: two
squared lengths and a dot product. We call the resulting space Q+     2 , the space of positive-definite
symmetric 2 × 2 matrices. Different points in this space can still represent the same lattice, up
to an orthogonal transformation, because changing the lattice basis gives G 7→ MT GM with
M ∈ GL(2, Z). A lattice-invariant energy must assign the same value to all these equivalent
metrics. Lagrange reduction [141] lets us choose one representative from each such family, in a
region called a fundamental domain.
    For the Lagrange reduction used here, the fundamental domain is
                                   n                                    o
                            DL = G ∈ Q+
                                      2 : 0 ≤ 2G12 ≤ G11 ≤ G22 .                                 (2.33)

Before describing the reduction algorithm, we use the Poincaré disk to visualize this domain and
the regions obtained by joining its symmetry-related copies.

    To understand how these representations fit together near a particular lattice, choose a
reference metric G0 . Some integer basis changes leave this metric unchanged: they preserve the
lengths and mutual angle of its basis vectors. These transformations form the finite lattice group
                                                  n                                          o
                                         L(G0 ) = M ∈ GL(2, Z) : MT G0 M = G0 ,                          (2.40)

which represents the point-group symmetries in lattice coordinates. An Ericksen–Pitteri neigh-
borhood (EPN) is an open region around G0 that these reference symmetries map onto itself,
while every other integer basis transformation maps it entirely outside itself. Such a region
exists around every positive-definite metric. Within it, equivalent metrics are related only by the


                                                                 38

===== PDF page 50 of 215 =====

2. Geometry of two-dimensional crystal lattices


reference lattice group, so the local energy description needs only this finite symmetry [137].
    A maximal EPN is one that cannot be enlarged without losing these properties for the chosen
reference metric. To construct one, start from a Lagrange domain containing G0 , combine its
images under the transformations in the lattice group L(G0 ), and take the interior of their
union [137]. The colored points in Figure 2.7 illustrate four cases. For the black point inside
DL , the reference lattice is oblique and a maximal EPN is simply the interior of that one sector.
For the orange point on a shared edge, the reference lattice has rectangular symmetry and the
neighborhood contains the two adjacent sectors, including their shared edge but excluding the
outer boundary. At the blue square point, four sectors join to form De ; at the green triangular
point, six join to form DT . These two regions are
                                n                                   o
                          De = G ∈ Q+
                                    2 : 2|G12 | ≤ min(G11 , G22 ) ,
                                n                                       o                    (2.41)
                          DT = G ∈ Q+
                                    2 : 0 ≤ G12 ≤ min(G11 , G22 ) .


We include the boundaries in these domain definitions, while the maximal EPNs are their open
interiors. In particular, the blue square point lies on the excluded boundary of the triangular
EPN, and the green triangular point lies on the excluded boundary of the square EPN.
    For the normalized square material reference used later, Q = I and G = [C]E by Eq. (2.32).
The same normalization and projection therefore give the same disk in G and C coordinates. We
use the same labels DL , De and DT for the corresponding domains in these two representations
and for their images in the disk. For a general material reference, the conversion through Q must
be retained.1
    For each EPN constructed this way, it is only after including its boundary that the region
contains a representative of every lattice class. Equivalent copies then cover Q+ 2 , meeting along
their boundaries. This gives a way to build the energy landscape: define the energy on a
fundamental domain and copy it to equivalent metrics throughout Q+      2 . One can also start from
one of these maximal EPNs with a potential that respects the reference symmetries. In either
case, the energy values must agree where the copies meet, and a smooth energy requires the
appropriate derivatives to match there as well [137].

2.8   Lagrange reduction algorithm
The Lagrange reduction scheme reduces a two-dimensional basis A by repeatedly subtracting the
shorter basis vector from the longer. In terms of the metric G = AT A, the reduction terminates
when
                                     0 ≤ 2G12 ≤ G11 ≤ G22 ,                              (2.42)

where Gab are the components of G and G11 denotes the squared length of the shortest basis
vector.

                                                                       !

The squared length of the second basis vector changes by ∆G22 = G11 − 2G12 . After the sign and
ordering steps ensure G12 ≥ 0 and G11 ≤ G22 , subtraction shortens the second vector only when
2G12 > G11 . If it does not, all the inequalities in Eq. (2.42) hold and the algorithm terminates.
The sign and ordering conventions select the Lagrange domain but do not remove every basis
degeneracy at its symmetry boundaries. The reduced metrics therefore lie in the fundamental
domain DL defined in Eq. (2.33) and shown in Figure 2.7. Algorithm 1 gives the reduction
directly in terms of the metric.

Algorithm 1 Lagrange reduction
 1: repeat
 2:    if G12 < 0 then
 3:       G ← M(1)T GM(1)                                                               ▷ Flip the sign of a2 .
 4:    if G22 < G11 then
 5:       G ← M(2)T GM(2)                                                                  ▷ Swap a1 and a2 .
 6:    if G12 > G11 /2 then
 7:       G ← M(3)T GM(3)                                                              ▷ Subtract a1 from a2 .
 8: until G does not change

2.9      Reduction of the metric strain tensor
We now use Lagrange reduction to select a lattice-equivalent strain representative relative to
the fixed material reference. For the normalized square reference used in the implementation,
Q = I and G = [C]E . Algorithm 1 can therefore be applied directly to the Cartesian matrix
of C, replacing Gab by CIJ . With the fixed Cartesian bases understood, the resulting integer
matrix Mf defines the reduced metric strain tensor

                                                       e =M
                                                       C  f T CM.
                                                               f                                             (2.50)
  2

The reference basis remains fixed in this construction: C  e is a generally different tensor, not a
new coordinate representation of C. The corresponding deformation-gradient matrix is F    e = FM;
                                                                                                f
its images of the reference lattice basis generate the same current lattice as those of F. Thus,
the reduced tensor selects a representative in the Lagrange domain from the lattice-equivalent
strains. The lattice-invariant energy introduced below assigns them the same energy; reduction
does not prescribe an additional physical deformation.
    The square and triangular symmetry points are therefore the same as in Figure 2.7. A
triangular sample referred to its own undeformed configuration instead has C(0) = I; its Gram
matrix is still triangular because its reference basis has Q ̸= I. Appendix B.3 explains how to
convert that sample-relative tensor to the square-reference tensor used by the energy.


3     Lattice-invariant energy density and the MTM
The MTM is built from an energy density that is objective with respect to rigid rotations and
invariant under lattice transformations. Objectivity motivates using C rather than F, while
lattice invariance motivates reducing C modulo GL(2, Z).

3.1   Lattice-invariant shear and periodic energy wells
The periodic structure of the MTM energy follows from three distinct symmetry requirements.
Material frame indifference removes superposed rigid rotations and motivates the use of C = FT F.
Point-group symmetry accounts for the orthogonal symmetries of a given lattice. However, this
local crystallographic symmetry is not sufficient for plasticity, because lattice-invariant shears
associated with slip are generally non-orthogonal transformations. The energy must therefore
also respect the global lattice symmetry that identifies primitive bases related by GL(2, Z) [127,
139]. A simple illustration is homogeneous shear of a square reference lattice,

                                                  1 γ
                                                      !

                                          γ =
                                         F(h)         .
                                                  0 1

When γ is an integer, the original square lattice is recovered. The energy should therefore have
equivalent minima at integer shear values, as expressed by the invariance condition in Eq. (2.51).
Plastic rearrangements correspond to changes between these lattice-equivalent wells.
    In this picture, an edge dislocation can be interpreted as an interface between two regions
occupying different but lattice-equivalent energy wells, as illustrated in Figure 2.9. For example,
one region may be unsheared, while the other is shifted by one lattice-invariant shear. The
dislocation core then forms the transition zone between these two states, where the configuration
crosses the energy barrier separating the wells.

3.2   Energy density as a function of C
Let Φ(C) = Φ(FT F) denote the energy density associated with a deformation gradient F. We
choose the stress-free square reference C = I as a local minimum and set its energy to zero,


                                                43

===== PDF page 55 of 215 =====

Chapter 2. The MTM




Figure 2.9: Schematic illustration of lattice-invariant plasticity in the MTM. Left: the energy
density Φ is periodic in the homogeneous shear parameter γ, with wells at integer values
corresponding to equivalent primitive bases of the same lattice. Right: an edge dislocation
is represented as a transition between regions occupying neighboring lattice-equivalent shear
states. The horizontal dashed line indicates the glide plane. The colored elements correspond to
equivalent wells, whereas the element near the core samples higher-energy configurations between
wells.


Φ(I) = 0. This zero is a normalization convention, not a condition for equilibrium: subtracting
a constant changes neither stresses nor elastic moduli. The reference subtraction used for the
Conti potential is specified below. Lattice symmetry also requires equivalent primitive bases to
have the same energy, expressed by

                            Φ(C) = Φ(MT CM)          ∀ M ∈ GL(2, Z).                         (2.51)

   We construct the lattice-invariant density using Lagrange reduction. Let RL (C) denote the
procedure in Section 2.9, which maps C to a reduced metric tensor C. e Introducing an inner
density Φ0 on the reduced domain, we define

                                  Φ(C) = Φ0 (RL (C)) = Φ0 (C).
                                                           e                                 (2.52)

Because C  e lies in the fundamental domain D , Φ0 only needs to be defined there. Lattice
                                                 L
invariance requires consistent boundary choices or equal values at all identified boundary repre-
sentatives. Smoothness of the extended density additionally requires matching derivatives across
those boundaries. With these compatibility conditions, Eq. (2.51) holds; see Appendix B.4.
    In the following, we adopt the expression for Φ0 given in [137]. Its strain argument is referred
to a normalized square lattice reference configuration, regardless of the reference configuration
chosen for the sample. To retain this expression while measuring C from a general sample
reference, we introduce a fixed linear map H from the normalized square lattice reference to
the sample reference. The composite gradient FH then gives Csq = HT CH, which is reduced
before evaluation in Φ0 . In this work, our sample reference configuration is the normalized square
lattice, thus H = I and Csq = C, so we use C directly. Appendix B.3 develops this change of


                                                44

reference for non-square lattices.

3.3      Smooth periodic polynomial energy density
It remains to specify the inner density Φ0 on the reduced domain. In this thesis, Φ0 is not
treated as a fitted atomistic potential, but as a controlled model energy. Its purpose is to encode
elastic stiffness near stable lattice states, finite barriers between competing states, and the local
symmetry structure of the square-to-hexagonal transformation.
    Following [137], we separate area change from shape change. Since det C is invariant under
changes of lattice basis, we write the reduced metric in normalized form as

                                             b = p    C
                                                      e

penalize deviations from unit area. This keeps elements close to area-preserving deformation and
discourages strongly dilatational mechanisms associated with vacancy formation, void nucleation,
and fracture, which are not the focus of this work. This direction is discussed in more detail by
Engrand [16].
    The deviatoric part is constructed from polynomial invariants of the hexagonal lattice group.

where β is a control parameter. Varying β changes the relative stability of the square and
hexagonal states. For β = −1/4, the potential has a local minimum in C = I, corresponding
to a square crystal. All simulations shown in this thesis use β = −1/4. Using β = 4 gives the
potential a local minimum in C = Q△ , corresponding to a hexagonal crystal. See [14, 128] for
studies on this triangular potential.
    The volumetric part is chosen as a simple logarithmic penalty,

                         Φvol (det C) = Kbulk (det C − log(det C) − 1) ,                              (2.59)

where Kbulk > 0 is a volumetric stiffness parameter controlling the energetic cost of area change.
All simulations shown in this thesis use Kbulk = 4. This value is large enough to prevent crack
formation and small enough to avoid dominating the deviatoric contribution in small volumetric
deformations.

    As a perspective, one may also imagine calibrating such potentials using methods analogous
to those used for machine-learning interatomic potentials [142]. In this thesis, however, the
energy density is used as a controlled mesoscopic model rather than as a quantitatively fitted
material potential.

3.4   Elastic wells and stress
The periodic energy landscape contains many equivalent wells related by GL(2, Z) transformations.
Within one well, the response is elastic: small changes in the deformation gradient F produce
smooth changes in the stored energy and in the corresponding stress. A plastic event corresponds
to a transition between such elastic wells. These energy wells are illustrated in Figure 2.11.
For a square crystal, the well in the center of the disk is described by De , which was shown in
Figure 2.7. In square-reference, right Cauchy–Green strain coordinates, the closed fundamental
elastic domain and its open interior are
                                    n                                   o
                             De = C ∈ Q+
                                       2 : 2|C12 | ≤ min(C11 , C22 ) ,
                                    n                                   o                    (2.63)
                          int De = C ∈ Q+
                                        2 : 2|C12 | < min(C11 , C22 ) .


This retains the closed-domain convention introduced in Eq. (2.41); the maximal EPN is the open
interior. For a general reference lattice, a maximal EPN supplies the corresponding geometric
neighborhood. Interpreting the reference metric as an elastic-well center assumes that it is a local
energy minimum; the EPN boundaries do not by themselves specify mechanical stability limits.

the contours of the energy landscape align with the reduction domains. Square and triangular
markers have been added to indicate the symmetry of the lattice at those points.

    In the finite-element implementation, the same derivative determines the nodal forces. Each
element is assigned a local deformation gradient, from which its metric C is computed and reduced.
As long as the associated reduction matrix M   f remains unchanged, the element remains inside
an elastic well. When the local deformation crosses a Lagrange-sector boundary, the reduction
matrix changes. The physical metric may vary continuously, but the reduced representation
switches to a different equivalent basis. Such a switch need not change the elastic well: plastic well
changes correspond to crossing the outer boundary of a well, rather than an internal boundary
between its Lagrange sectors.


4     Finite-element formulation and numerical implementation
4.1   Physical interpretation of the discretization
To implement the continuum MTM numerically, we discretize the reference domain with linear
triangular finite elements and use the nodal positions as degrees of freedom. Linear shape
functions interpolate the deformation within each triangle, giving a constant local deformation
gradient F and hence a constant metric C = FT F and energy density. Triangular elements are
chosen because they keep the element kinematics simple and because they are well suited to the
reconnection procedure introduced in Chapter 3.
     The role of the finite-element mesh in the MTM is illustrated in Figure 2.12. Each element
supplies a local deformation gradient F, which is interpreted through the Cauchy–Born rule as
a homogeneous deformation of the underlying lattice. Equivalently, the deformation induces
the lattice metric G = QT [C]E Q, as in Eq. (2.32). This connects continuum kinematics to the
lattice-based MTM energy density. To give the mesh a physical interpretation, we must also relate
its spacing h to an atomic length scale a, which in this section will denote the nearest-neighbor
atomic spacing.
     Under the coarse-grained interpretation illustrated in Figure 2.12, each element represents an
aggregate of atoms. We want to determine how many atoms are represented by each element.
Here, the MTM framework can be approached from two slightly different starting points. The
first is to specify a square sample of side length L and discretize it with N mesh intervals along
each side. Denoting the spacing between adjacent nodes by h, we have h := L/N . The number
of atoms represented by each element then depends on the ratio between the element area and
the atomic area. If the element size must remain within an acceptable physical range, a larger
sample requires a larger number of elements. The second approach is to fix the number of atoms
represented by each element, thus fixing h for a given underlying lattice. The sample size is then
determined by the mesh: L := hN .
     In the first approach, L and N are chosen parameters, from which h follows directly. One
can therefore assign a ≪ h ≪ L, corresponding to substantial coarse-graining. However, in the
absence of an independently specified atomic scale in the constitutive formulation, uniformly
rescaling L and h at fixed N leaves the normalized response unchanged under correspondingly
rescaled loading. Different physical sample sizes can therefore be assigned to the same numerical

                                                 49

===== PDF page 61 of 215 =====

Chapter 2. The MTM


response. This freedom to rescale the sample without changing its normalized response prevents
the model from capturing the dependence of mechanical behavior on physical sample size observed
experimentally [18]. Introducing an explicit dependence on the ratio between mesh and atomic
scales could provide a more detailed physical calibration of the first approach, but this lies outside
the scope of this thesis. We therefore adopt the second approach and hold h fixed. Changing N
then changes the physical sample size, allowing the study of finite-size effects.
    The challenge for the second approach is to determine h. One argument starts from the lattice
periodicity imposed on the MTM energy. Baggio considers a finite box of atoms interacting
through a Lennard–Jones potential and subjects it to homogeneous simple shear. As the number
of atoms increases, the resulting energy density becomes approximately periodic. This suggests
choosing the element size as the smallest atomic domain for which periodicity is a reasonable
approximation over the strain range of interest [143]. The element size h therefore typically
scales with the interatomic cutoff. Using the same argument, Salman et al. report a working
scale of h ∼ 10a [15].
    Another possibility is to choose the mesh spacing equal to the interatomic spacing. This
interpretation requires care for small samples with fixed or free boundaries since the MTM imposes
a perfectly periodic bulk energy, whereas the energy of a finite atomic sample can be affected by
its boundaries. For sufficiently large samples, or with compatible periodic boundary conditions,
these boundary effects are reduced, making perfect periodicity a reasonable approximation. The
finite-element formulation even offers some potential computational advantages over molecular
dynamics (MS). While MS simulations with short-range pair potentials evaluate interactions
within a cutoff radius, the MTM computes nodal forces from the surrounding elements. An
illustrative cutoff radius rc = 2.15a includes 12 neighboring atoms in an undeformed 2D square
lattice. By comparison, an interior node in a regular triangular mesh belongs to only six elements.
Furthermore, each triangular element contributes energy derivatives to three nodes, whereas a
pair interaction contributes forces to two atoms. These features suggest a possible computational
saving, although the overall cost also depends on the constitutive energy, its derivatives, and the
implementation.
    Choosing an atomic mesh spacing does not by itself make the MTM an accurate or efficient
substitute for molecular statics or molecular dynamics. We therefore stress that the goal of
the MTM is not direct atomistic modeling. Instead, the framework provides a promising basis
for further development, with additional parameters or constitutive features needed to connect
the mesh scale explicitly to the underlying atomic scale. One such extension has recently been
proposed [16], but further development is required.
    Our findings and contributions concern the discretized model and do not require a particular
assignment of h to an atomic length scale. We therefore leave relationship between the atomic
spacing a and the mesh spacing h in the MTM open, and it is not examined further in this thesis.
    Note that under the coarse-grained interpretation, mesh defects represent effective mesoscopic
dislocations, grain boundaries, and other crystal structures. For simplicity, we use the corre-
sponding crystal terminology throughout this thesis, omitting the “effective mesoscopic” qualifier
while retaining the understanding that these structures are represented at the mesh scale.


                                                 50

===== PDF page 62 of 215 =====

4. Finite-element formulation and numerical implementation




Figure 2.12: Schematic view of the two constitutive ingredients of the MTM and of its finite-
element discretization. The central triangular mesh represents the continuum body used in the
numerical problem. For each element, the Cauchy–Born rule identifies the element deformation
with a homogeneous deformation of an underlying lattice, illustrated by the magnified lattice
cells. The lattice energy is then evaluated from a shear-periodic density, so that lattice states
related by an integer shear correspond to equivalent wells. The global problem is solved by
assembling these element contributions within the finite-element method.

4.4   Boundary conditions, minimization and numerical checks
The simulations in this thesis are quasistatic. At each loading step, selected nodal positions,
displacements or boundary conditions are prescribed, and the internal energy is minimized over
the remaining nodal degrees of freedom. At a perfect local minimum, every free node satisfies

                             ∂Π                                         (p)
                             (p)
                                     = 0,        or equivalently       fi     = 0.          (2.85)
                           ∂xi

Numerically, we minimize the energy until a stopping criterion is met. Let f collect the force
components over the independent free nodal degrees of freedom, and let ∆u collect their
displacement increments during a minimization step. Depending on the minimizer and protocol,



                                                      54

===== PDF page 66 of 215 =====

4. Finite-element formulation and numerical implementation


we use the criteria
                           ∥f ∥2 < ϵg ,   ∥∆u∥2 < ϵx ,       ∥f ∥∞ < ϵr .

The infinity norm is the largest absolute force component. The criteria and tolerance values used
in each calculation, together with any variations of the loading protocol, are specified in the
relevant chapters.
    We have primarily used periodic boundary conditions (PBC). These and the loading procedure
are described in Section 5.
    The finite-element implementation can therefore be summarized as follows: the nodal positions
determine a constant deformation gradient in each element, the deformation gradient determines
the metric C, the metric is reduced before evaluating the lattice-invariant energy, and differentia-
tion of the assembled energy gives the nodal forces and stiffness. The quasistatic problem is then
a finite-dimensional energy minimization under the prescribed boundary conditions.

4.5   Terminology
In the following chapters, we will use some nomenclature that we define here for convenience.

Sample In this text, a sample will always be an initially pristine square crystal, typically with
periodic boundary conditions.

Loading Loading refers to placing the sample under strain. In this text, unless otherwise
specified, all samples are loaded using positive horizontal simple shear:

                                                  1 γ
                                                       !

                                           γ =
                                          F(h)        .                                      (2.86)
                                                  0 1

The loading procedure is described in Section 5.2.

Elastic branch and plastic events The total internal energy is assembled from the element
contributions, as in Eq. (2.79). As the mesh is loaded, the metric C(e) of each element traces a
path through the local energy landscape illustrated in Figures 2.10 and 2.11. These elemental
contributions are not independent, because neighboring elements share nodes and must satisfy
deformation compatibility. Their assembly therefore defines a coupled, high-dimensional energy
landscape in terms of the free nodal coordinates.
    Under quasistatic loading, the mesh follows a sequence of local minima of this total energy.
While elements remain within their current elastic well, the energy varies smoothly. We say that
the system is following an elastic branch. When one or more elements becomes unstable and
changes well, the mesh relaxes toward a new local minimum, marking a plastic event and the
start of a new elastic branch. The resulting redistribution of stress can trigger further plastic
changes in neighboring elements, producing a collective plastic event, often in the form of a
dislocation avalanche. At the macroscopic level, this intermittent evolution appears as sudden
drops in the total energy and measured stress, separated by smooth elastic loading segments.


                                                 55

===== PDF page 67 of 215 =====

Chapter 2. The MTM


Pre- and post-yield regimes We divide the loading trajectory of a sample into two regimes:
pre-yield and post-yield.3 For each sample, the yield strain γc is the loading parameter at which
its spatially averaged first Piola–Kirchhoff shear stress reaches a global maximum:

                                           γc = arg max ⟨P12 ⟩γ ,
                                                     γ


where ⟨P12 ⟩γ is the reference-area-weighted average of the element shear stresses in that sample
at imposed strain γ.


5       Numerical protocol
This section describes the protocol used for the collective simulations in the following chapters.
Different boundary conditions or loading procedures used in individual tests are specified where
they are introduced.

5.1     Boundary conditions
The boundary conditions are periodic unless otherwise specified. Figure 2.15 illustrates the
construction for a 3 × 3 mesh and the ghost nodes needed to complete its periodic tiling. Each
ghost node is linked to a physical node and represents one of its periodic images. For a rectangular
mesh, an additional row and column of ghost nodes are introduced, as shown in Figure 2.15(a).
The top-right ghost node is linked to the bottom-left physical node; the remaining nodes in the
top row and rightmost column are linked to the bottom row and leftmost column, respectively.
The cell boundary marks where adjacent periodic images meet. Figure 2.15(b) isolates the
effect of updating the periodic cell while holding the physical-node positions fixed. This is an
explanatory intermediate, rather than a separately relaxed simulation state.
    The node at (0, 0) in the bottom-left corner is linked to three ghost nodes with reference
shifts s1 = (0, L), s2 = (L, 0), and s3 = (L, L), where L = N h is the reference side length (L = 3
for a 3 × 3 mesh in units where h = 1). When the periodic cell is deformed by FPBC , a ghost
node with reference shift s has position

                                             xs = x + FPBC s.                                           (2.87)

    After updating the periodic cell, we also apply the affine loading increment to the physical
nodes and use the resulting positions as the initial guess for minimization. Figure 2.15(c) shows
this affine configuration for the pristine lattice. In this simple example it is also the relaxed state;
a general sample can undergo further non-affine motion during minimization.
    3
   We use “yielding” to describe the transition marked by a drop in stress and energy. The terminology does not
imply that the underlying mechanisms are the same as those responsible for yielding in experiments. As shown in
Chapter 3, the transition separates a dislocation-dominated regime from a grain-like regime.

===== PDF page 68 of 215 =====

5. Numerical protocol


                                             real nodes   ghost nodes   periodic cell




                                 load cell                                     relax




             (a)                                           (b)                                     (c)


Figure 2.15: Quasistatic loading protocol illustrated for a 3 × 3 mesh: (a) relaxed state from the
previous step; (b) periodic-cell update under a simple-shear increment, with physical nodes held
fixed to illustrate the change in periodic images; and (c) affinely updated physical and ghost
nodes, used as the minimization initial guess and also representing the relaxed state for this
pristine example. General samples can relax non-affinely.


5.2    Quasistatic loading
The collective simulations are performed on a periodic pristine square crystal with N lattice sites
in each direction. The initial triangulation consists of right-angled triangles, as in Fig. 2.15(a).
We impose simple shear by increasing the macroscopic strain γ in steps of ∆γ. After each step,
the system is relaxed to the chosen minimization tolerance. This athermal quasistatic (AQS)
procedure approximates the zero-temperature, zero-strain-rate limit as ∆γ and the minimization
tolerances tend to zero [64].
    From the pristine state, the first collective nucleation event occurs at γ ≃ 0.138. This
instability marks the end of the homogeneous branch [127]. The periodic cell contains no surfaces,
defects, or imposed nucleation sites, so the dislocation pattern emerges from the MTM energy
landscape itself [14].

5.3    Sample preparation
A pristine crystal under simple shear reaches the predicted loss of strong ellipticity of Φ at
approximately γ = 0.1322214; see Appendix B.6. In the noiseless loading test considered here,
dislocations nucleate at γ = 0.13243. Figure 2.16 shows the homogeneous loading branch from
the pristine configuration at γ = 0 (A) to the last state before this drop, at γ = 0.13242 (B).4
    To generate distinct samples with the same system size and loading parameters, we introduce
independent, zero-mean Gaussian perturbations to the nodal coordinates. The illustrated
preparation path adds these perturbations, with standard deviation 0.05, at γ = 0.13243 (B’),
then follows the resulting relaxation through C–G. Different noise realizations lead to different
relaxed configurations. In routine sample preparation, we skip the homogeneous loading branch
   4
     For this illustration, we load without the affine loading increment described above in Section 5.1. Instead, we
start minimization directly from the previous equilibrium state, but with updated boundary conditions. If we use
the affine initial guess, the energy landscape which used to be a local minimum is still very flat. The minimization
algorithms we used are therefore unable to detect the instability without extra noise or perturbation.



                                                           57

===== PDF page 69 of 215 =====

Chapter 2. The MTM


and initialize a pristine crystal affinely sheared to γ = 0.15. No further stochastic perturbations
are added after initialization.5

5.4    Example simulations
Figure 2.17 shows the reference-volume-normalized energy E/V (0) , averaged over eight samples
for each of several system sizes. Because the loading increment containing γc often includes a
large energy drop, we define the post-yield regime by γ ≥ γc + 0.01.
    The continued post-yield energy increase in Figure 2.17 also points to a limitation of fixed
connectivity. Mesh connections retained after slip can artificially obstruct subsequent dislocation
motion, forming topological locks. Chapter 3 demonstrates this mechanism in controlled slip
tests and compares simulations with and without reconnection to identify the associated energy
accumulation. This mesh-induced contribution should not be interpreted as physical strain
hardening.




  5
     Running the simulation with multiple threads may change the order of floating-point operations. Because
floating-point addition is not associative, this can produce small trajectory differences between runs.

samples. Dashed vertical lines show the average yield ⟨γc ⟩ for each system size. The × markers
show the individual yielding loads γc .




                                                     60

===== PDF page 72 of 215 =====

5. Numerical protocol


Conclusion
This chapter established the kinematic, crystallographic and numerical ingredients of the MTM.
The model is formulated in a finite-deformation framework. The deformation gradient F describes
the local mapping of material directions, while the right Cauchy–Green tensor C = FT F removes
rigid rotations and provides the objective metric entering the energy density. This finite-strain
measure is required because distinct finite shear transformations can have the same linearized
strain, as demonstrated by the horizontal and vertical simple shears above.
    The crystallographic part of the construction was expressed through the Gram matrix G.
This matrix records the lengths and angles of a chosen lattice basis, while Eq. (2.30) connects it to
the continuum metric C. Since the choice of primitive basis is not unique, equivalent descriptions
are related by G ∼ MT GM. Lagrange reduction removes this redundancy by mapping metrics
to the fundamental domain DL , whose geometry can be visualized in the Poincaré disk.
    On this reduced space, the MTM defines a lattice-invariant nonlinear energy density, Φ(C) =
Φ (RL (C)). This energy encodes the crystal symmetries, the elastic wells and the branch changes
  0

associated with plastic events. By differentiation, the same structure gives the stresses and the
tangent operators used in the finite-element formulation.
    We then formulated the numerical implementation. Through the Cauchy–Born interpretation,
each linear triangular element reconstructs a local F and C, contributes to the total energy, and
provides nodal forces and stiffness matrices. We also described the periodic loading protocol and
the randomized preparation of samples used in the collective simulations. In the standard MTM,
however, the mesh is not only a numerical discretization. Its spacing also fixes a mesoscopic
regularization length, and its connectivity determines which nodes are used together to reconstruct
element deformation gradients.
    This fixed-connectivity Lagrangian description is natural for moderate deformations, defect
nucleation and history tracking. In a plastic-flow regime, however, large lattice-invariant shears
and dislocation motion can make the reference mesh connections obsolete. These connections
then no longer reflect the local neighborhood of nodes in the deformed configuration, leading
to mesh distortion and topological locking. This limitation motivates the reconnection strategy
developed in Chapter 3.

       4.5     Simulation results: grain-structure evolution under 500% shear . . . . . . . . . .        85




    In Chapter 2, we introduced the finite-deformation MTM and its finite-element implementation.
In this implementation, the mesh is not only a numerical discretization: it also fixes the mesoscopic
lattice size and defines the mesh connections used to reconstruct F. This dual role becomes
problematic in the plastic-flow regime, where large lattice-invariant shears can make the fixed
Lagrangian connectivity obsolete. This chapter introduces mesh reconnection as a way to update
those connections without moving, adding, or removing nodes.
    The first part of the chapter examines how obsolete connectivity can artificially restrict
dislocation motion. We call this artifact topological locking. This mesh-locking phenomenon has
also been observed in other MTM-like implementations [16]. A sequential slip test, shown in


                                                   62

===== PDF page 74 of 215 =====

1. Motivation: topological locking in the MTM


Fig. 3.1, is used to isolate this mechanism: after one lattice-invariant shear, the retained mesh
connectivity can block a later dislocation passage that should remain admissible.
    The second part of the chapter establishes when reconnection is needed. We express the
local neighborhood condition in terms of the current mesh-edge geometry. We then introduce
edge flipping as a way to update the connectivity [144, 145], before comparing this local method
with global Delaunay triangulation [141]. Both approaches preserve the nodal positions and the
number of lattice sites while changing which nodes are connected when the element deformation
gradients are reconstructed.
    The chapter then addresses the reference-state issue created by a change of connectivity. The
original material positions of newly connected nodes need not form a nondegenerate reference
triangle. Using those positions can therefore make the reference Jacobian singular. To avoid
this, we assign a canonical lattice reference triangle to every element. For affine deformation,
reconnection leaves the energy, Cauchy stress and nodal forces unchanged. For non-affine fields,
it can produce the interpolation jumps discussed in Section 3.3.
    The last part of the chapter combines the edge-flip algorithm with mechanical relaxation
into a procedure for simulations under continued loading. We first return to the sequential
slip test to assess whether reconnection allows dislocations to pass through previously slipped
regions. We then examine element conditioning and the energy and stress changes introduced by
topology updates. Finally, we turn to collective plastic flow under simple shear, examining the
grain-like organization of the deformed crystal and whether reconnection prevents the continued
accumulation of energy observed with a fixed mesh.


1    Motivation: topological locking in the MTM
Fixed connectivity produces two related but distinct artifacts. First, strongly distorted elements
reduce interpolation accuracy and degrade numerical conditioning. This effect is discussed further
in Appendix A. Second, an obsolete neighborhood relation can constrain the admissible motion
of defects; we refer to this physical artifact as topological locking. Element collapse toward a
collinear configuration is the limiting geometric manifestation of the problem, but locking can
affect dislocation motion before an element becomes exactly degenerate.
    Figure 3.1 presents a controlled test designed to isolate the topological locking produced
by a fixed mesh connectivity. The test is deliberately simple: a small initially perfect square
crystal is deformed quasistatically, using imposed boundary displacements followed by energy
minimization after each loading step. This setting makes it possible to follow the motion of
individual dislocations and to compare directly the behavior of the model with and without mesh
reconnection.
    The protocol has two successive stages, with displacements ux and uy measured in units of
the mesh spacing h = b. First, the upper half of the left edge is displaced horizontally from
ux = 0 to ux = 1, while uy = 0. Then ux = 1 is held fixed and the right half of the bottom edge
is displaced vertically from uy = 0 to uy = 3. The lower half of the left edge and the left half
of the bottom edge remain fixed; the remaining boundaries are free. The first stage drives a

loading protocol. Black markers indicate fixed nodes, arrows indicate imposed displacements,
and green dashed lines indicate free boundaries. First, the upper half of the left edge is displaced
horizontally to ux = 1, with uy = 0. Then ux = 1 is held fixed while the right half of the bottom

fixed. (b) Configurations A, B, C, and D at selected displacements, colored by the element
