horizontal slip event through the sample, and the second drives vertical dislocations across the
previously slipped region.
    The snapshots A, B, C, and D in Fig. 3.1(b) show the mesh configurations during this
sequential slip protocol. The elements are colored by their potential energy E (e) . Without
reconnection, the second, vertically gliding dislocation becomes trapped when it reaches the
previously slipped region, illustrated in snapshot D. This produces a strong localized increase
of energy, visible as the high-energy zone near the locking point. By contrast, when edge
reconnection is allowed, the mesh connectivity adapts to the deformed configuration and the
second dislocation can cross the horizontally slipped region. The comparison shows that the
blocking observed with fixed connectivity is not a physical obstruction to dislocation motion, but
a numerical artifact caused by the obsolete mesh topology.
    After the first dislocation has crossed the sample at ux = 1, the crystal is again defect-
free according to its energy, stress and lattice state. The sheared band retained by the fixed
triangulation is therefore numerical memory rather than a physical obstacle or intentional model
feature. Our aim is consequently to remove this mesh-induced constraint.

1.1   Remeshing in Lagrangian meshes
In finite-element simulations, maintaining acceptable mesh quality is important for the accuracy
and robustness of the numerical solution [146–148]. In Lagrangian formulations, the mesh
deforms with the material, so initially well-shaped elements may progressively become distorted
during large deformation. Conventional mesh-adaptation methods address this through vertex
relocation or smoothing (r-adaptation), as well as local remeshing operations such as point

                                                                                64

===== PDF page 76 of 215 =====

1. Motivation: topological locking in the MTM


insertion and deletion and local edge or face removal [149]. Mesh connectivity may also be
modified through edge flips in two dimensions and face or edge swaps in three-dimensional
tetrahedral meshes [144]. For large deformations, node repositioning can be combined with edge
splitting, edge collapse, and edge swapping before elements become severely distorted [150]; local
refinement, coarsening, and anisotropic remeshing provide further means of controlling resolution
and element quality [151]. The following discussion considers which of these otherwise standard
operations remain admissible when the mesh itself carries physical meaning, as in the MTM.
    As discussed in Section 4.1 of Chapter 2, the mesh spacing h = b fixes the elementary
Burgers-vector magnitude and the associated regularization length in the present formulation.
Changing h therefore changes the physical model, rather than simply refining a discretization
at fixed physical parameters. This constrains the use of conventional remeshing methods: their
effect on the represented material state must be considered alongside any improvement in element
quality.
    We therefore favor an approach that updates the mesh without moving, adding, or removing
nodes. The mesh connectivity specifies which nodes are joined by edges and which groups of three
nodes form triangular elements. By reconnection, we mean changing these connections while
keeping the nodal positions fixed. Nodes that have moved apart during deformation can thus
cease to be edge-connected, while nodes that have become close can be connected. This allows
the mesh to follow the evolving local neighborhood without imposing an additional displacement
on the material.
    In two dimensions, local reconnection can be performed through edge flips [144, 145]. We
describe this operation in Section 2.2. Before choosing which connections to change, however, we
need a geometric condition that identifies when the current connectivity has become obsolete.

1.2   Topological locking and mesh dependence
In this chapter, we define the local neighborhood of a node as the other nodes whose closed
Voronoi cells intersect its own, including contact at a single vertex. A Voronoi cell consists of the
points at least as close to its node as to any other node. This definition applies to irregular point
sets and includes the eight surrounding nodes of a rectangular lattice. This convention is broader
than Voronoi adjacency restricted to cells sharing an edge. Neighborhood is a geometric relation,
independent of mesh connectivity; nodes joined by a mesh edge are called edge-connected. A
triangle or mesh satisfies the neighborhood condition when every edge joins neighboring nodes.
    Figure 3.2 illustrates this condition on an irregular point set. The green triangle satisfies
it, whereas the long edge of the red triangle joins nodes with disjoint Voronoi cells. Later, in
Section 2.1.3, we derive a local metric test that is equivalent to this condition for a primitive
triangle of a Bravais lattice, without constructing the Voronoi cells.
    Figure 3.1 demonstrates locking in a numerical simulation, but the geometric origin of
the obstruction deserves a closer look. The locking mechanism is illustrated schematically in
Figure 3.3. Consider an initially perfect square lattice, and disregard boundary effects (A). Let
the top half of the sample be displaced horizontally to the left (B). Some diagonal mesh edges now
extend beyond the local neighborhood of their endpoints. A subsequent upward displacement of

                                                 65

===== PDF page 77 of 215 =====

Chapter 3. Reconnecting in the MTM


                             Valid                             Invalid




Figure 3.2: Neighborhood condition on an irregular point set. Gray lines delimit Voronoi cells.
All edges of the green triangle join Voronoi neighbors. The long edge of the red triangle does not.


the right half of the sample would progressively flatten the element pair highlighted in red (C).
Completing this displacement by one lattice spacing would restore the square-lattice arrangement
of the nodes but produce degenerate elements (D), whose vertices are collinear and whose area is
zero. Note that here, displacements are imposed directly on halves of the sample. This differs
from Figure 3.1, where imposed boundary displacements nucleate dislocations that carry slip
through the interior.
    These red highlighted elements collapse in the current configuration while their reference
triangles remain nondegenerate. Consequently, det F = 0, and the represented two-dimensional
lattice is mapped onto a line. The element therefore no longer represents a two-dimensional
crystal, even though the surrounding nodes have recovered a valid square-lattice arrangement.
The complete ABCD sequence is thus impossible with fixed connectivity if the elements are
to retain a physically admissible lattice representation. Additionally, any energy density that
penalizes volume changes makes the approach to element collapse prohibitively expensive; for
the potential used here, the energy diverges as the element area approaches zero. In contrast,
the sequence AEFG accommodates vertical slip without collapsing elements. The obstruction
therefore comes from the retained mesh connectivity, rather than from the lattice arrangement
itself.
    The top row of Figure 3.1 illustrates the corresponding obstruction in a mesh whose diagonals
have the opposite orientation to those in Figure 3.3. Its loading is similar to the AEFG sequence
in Figure 3.3, but the second dislocation becomes trapped before completing its passage.
    Let a major diagonal run from top left to bottom right, as in Figure 3.3(A), and a minor
diagonal run from top right to bottom left. The initial diagonal orientation determines which
slip paths are obstructed by fixed connectivity. Changing from a major-diagonal mesh to a
minor-diagonal mesh can avoid the particular collapse illustrated by ABCD, but does not remove
the locking mechanism: the minor-diagonal mesh simulation in Figure 3.1 still traps a dislocation
under sequential loading. Avoiding locking as slip accumulates therefore requires updating the
connectivity during loading, rather than selecting a different initial diagonal orientation.

sequence ABCD collapses a pair of elements in the current configuration while their reference
triangles remain nondegenerate, giving det F = 0, and cannot be completed while preserving a
two-dimensional lattice representation in those elements. Reversing the horizontal displacement
from B to E allows the vertical displacement through F to G without generating degenerate
elements.


2     Mesh reconnection
To avoid the topological locking illustrated above, the connectivity must adapt as the local
neighborhood changes. We first formulate a geometric condition for deciding when reconnection is
needed. We then describe how to carry out the update through local edge flips and compare this
method with global Delaunay triangulation. Throughout, the nodal positions and the number of
nodes are held fixed during a topology update.

2.1     Condition for reconnection
The neighborhood condition introduced in Section 1.2 requires each mesh edge to join neighboring
nodes. We seek a local test of this condition using the current triangle geometry. This requires
distinguishing the actual mesh edges from the lattice basis used to describe the material state.
We first define a metric of the mesh edges and then use it to identify triangles that are candidates
for reconnection.

2.1.1    Distinction between mesh and lattice geometry

In Chapter 2, the lattice metric G = AT A is constructed from a lattice basis A = (a1 ; a2 ). Here,
                                                                                            (e)
we construct an analogous metric from the actual edges of the current mesh triangle. Let a1 and
 (e)
a2 denote the vectors along the two shortest edges of a nondegenerate element e, directed from
their common vertex, opposite a longest edge. This is the canonical choice of edge pair; either
ordering is allowed. It does not define a unique matrix: exchanging the two edges permutes its
entries, and equal-length ties may permit different base vertices. The neighborhood criterion
                                                                (e) (e)
below is unchanged by these admissible choices. With A(e) = (a1 , a2 ), we define the geometric

                                                67

===== PDF page 79 of 215 =====

Chapter 3. Reconnecting in the MTM


metric
                                         G (e) = A(e)T A(e) .                               (3.1)

Both the lattice metric G and the geometric metric G (e) are Gram matrices, but they describe
different vector pairs: lattice-basis vectors and current mesh-edge vectors, respectively. They
coincide when these pairs coincide. Reconnection changes the mesh edges, while the lattice state
must be accounted for separately. Since the reconnection criterion concerns the current triangle
geometry, we formulate it in terms of G (e) .

2.1.2    C versus G as reconnection criteria

Figure 3.4 illustrates why the metric G used in the reconnection criterion must be distinguished
from the right Cauchy–Green tensor. The tensor C = FT F is a kinematic object: it measures
the affine deformation of material line elements with respect to the fixed reference coordinates.
By contrast, the element Gram matrix G (e) is constructed from the current edges selected by
the mesh connectivity. The matrix G (e) may coincide numerically with the Cartesian component
matrix of C in simple reference configurations. Their definitions nevertheless refer to different
vector pairs. Under the same affine simple shear, the trajectory of C is independent of the chosen
triangulation, whereas the trajectory of G (e) depends on whether the element uses the major or
the minor diagonal. The geometric metric therefore detects the geometry of the selected mesh
edges that the affine deformation gradient alone does not encode. This is why the edge-flip
criterion is formulated in terms of G (e) rather than only in terms of C.

2.1.3    Neighborhood reconnection criterion

The neighborhood condition requires every triangle edge to join nodes with intersecting closed
Voronoi cells, including vertex contacts. We seek a local metric test for this condition.
    Figure 3.5 shows the full space of positive-definite Gram matrices G of arbitrary ordered
incident-edge pairs, up to an orthogonal transformation and uniform scaling. Let (xP , yP )
denote the coordinates obtained by applying the normalization and projection of Section 2.7 in
Chapter 2 to G. To center the figure on the equilateral metric, we apply the same normalization
and projection to

To see why, the three vertex scalar products are G12 , G11 − G12 and G22 − G12 . The domain
inequalities make all three nonnegative, so the triangle is non-obtuse. For a primitive lattice
triangle, this is equivalent to all three edges joining Voronoi neighbors, as proved in Appendix B.8.
    Exchanging the two edges leaves the triangle and its neighborhood relations unchanged. The
condition therefore also holds in DE . For the canonical pair of two shortest edges, the neighborhood
                                                                                 (e)          (e)  (e)
criterion is G (e) ∈ DE . For this pair, the opposite edge is longest, so 2G12 ≤ min(G11 , G22 )
already holds. Membership in DT and DE is therefore equivalent for the canonical pair. If
the supplied edges are not necessarily the two shortest, membership in DT gives the same
condition without selecting a particular pair first. For an irregular mesh, this is a local lattice
approximation; exact Voronoi adjacency also depends on surrounding nodes.
    We therefore use G (e) ∈/ DT to flag an element as a candidate for reconnection. This identifies
a triangle whose edge geometry fails the local criterion; deciding which edge to change also
requires considering the adjacent elements. We now describe that local operation.

2.2     Local reconnection by edge flipping
An edge flip is a local reconnection operation applied to two triangular elements that share
an edge. Together, the two triangles contain two further vertices that are not joined by the
shared edge. As illustrated in Figure 3.6, if their union is a convex quadrilateral, the shared
diagonal can be removed and replaced by the other diagonal. The operation changes only the
local connectivity: it neither moves nodes nor changes their number.
     Each triangular element has at most three edge-sharing neighbors. We call two adjacent
elements twins when their shared edge is a longest edge of each. This geometric relation is
mutual, but tied longest edges need not select a unique twin. The algorithm only flips pairs for
which both triangles lie outside DT . Such triangles are obtuse and have a unique longest edge,
so each reconnection candidate has at most one twin. A triangle with tied longest edges is acute
and cannot participate in a flip; no tie-breaking rule is needed for flipping (Appendix B.8.3). For
a candidate, we denote its twin, when it exists, by etwin . Figure 3.6(b) shows a twin pair before
a flip. In panel (c), the longest edges are (1, 3) and (2, 4), so the two children do not form a twin
pair.
     An edge flip also requires an assignment of the two parent element labels to the two children.
For example, Figure 3.6 lets (124) in panel (c) inherit the color of (123) in panel (b), but the
opposite assignment is geometrically possible. Total energy is independent of this assignment,
whereas changes compared label by label need not be. Section 4.4 uses the stored element labels
for that diagnostic and states its limitation. Transport of physical history variables is addressed
in Chapter 4.

2.2.1    Edge flip algorithm

Algorithm 2 combines the geometric criterion of Section 2.1.3 with the twin relation defined
above. A shared edge is flipped when its two elements are twins and both have current geometric
metrics outside DT . The shared edge must be an interior edge, and the pair must form a convex

Figure 3.6: Illustration of an edge flip. (a) shows the initial state of the mesh. The dashed
line shows an alternative major-diagonal meshing. The rhombus formed by the green and blue
elements has the minor diagonal (2, 3) and two unconnected vertices, 1 and 4. (b) shows a
deformed mesh. In (c), the shared edge has been replaced by the major diagonal (1, 4). The
colors illustrate one parent-to-child label assignment; swapping the two child labels would leave
the geometry unchanged.


quadrilateral so that both new triangles have strictly positive area. After each flip, the element
metrics and adjacency are updated before continuing the sweep. Sweeps are repeated until no
further flips occur. This algorithm specifies the connectivity update at fixed nodal positions.
The reference state assigned to the new elements is addressed in Section 3, and the coupling to
mechanical relaxation in Section 4.1.

Algorithm 2 Edge flipping
 1: repeat
 2:    changed ← false
 3:    for each element e in the mesh do
 4:        if G (e) ∈/ DT then
 5:            Identify etwin , if it exists
 6:            if a twin exists and G (etwin ) ∈
                                               / DT then
 7:                 if the shared edge is interior and the pair forms a convex quadrilateral then
 8:                     Flip the common edge and update metrics and adjacency
 9:                     changed ← true
10: until changed = false




2.3   Global reconnection by Delaunay triangulation
An alternative to selecting individual edge flips is to reconstruct the triangulation of the com-
plete current point set. Delaunay triangulation provides a geometric criterion for this global
update [141].
    A triangulation is Delaunay when the open circumcircle of every triangle contains no other
mesh vertex. For a convex quadrilateral, a diagonal is locally non-Delaunay if the opposite vertex
lies inside the circumcircle of one of its adjacent triangles. As illustrated in Figure 3.7, flipping
that diagonal gives the locally Delaunay configuration. When all four vertices are cocircular,
both diagonals are admissible.
    For a fixed set of vertices, an edge flip is the smallest possible change to a conforming
triangular mesh. A convex quadrilateral admits exactly two triangulations, distinguished only by
their diagonal. A sequence of such local operations can transform one triangulation into another;
in particular, the standard construction of a Delaunay triangulation may be expressed as repeated

                                                 72

===== PDF page 84 of 215 =====

3. Update of the reference state after reconnection


                                         P3                                      P3
                    P4                                   P4




                  P1                          P2        P1                            P2


                          (a) Delaunay                        (b) Non-Delaunay

Figure 3.7: The two triangulations of the same convex quadrilateral. In (a), P3 lies outside the
dashed circumcircle of triangle P1 P2 P4 , so the green diagonal is locally Delaunay. (b) After
flipping the diagonal, P4 lies inside the circumcircle of triangle P1 P2 P3 , so the red diagonal is
locally non-Delaunay.


flips of locally non-Delaunay edges [152]. Edge flipping and global Delaunay reconstruction
therefore use the same elementary topological change, but organize it differently.
    Appendix B.9 proves that every Delaunay triangle of a two-dimensional Bravais lattice is
non-obtuse, placing its edge-pair metric in DT . Choosing the vertex opposite a longest side gives
a pair in DE ; ordering its two edges by length then places the metric in DL . The corresponding
edge pair is therefore a Lagrange-reduced basis, and the third edge is their difference. For a
lattice arrangement of nodes, the Delaunay triangles therefore satisfy the local neighborhood
condition used by the edge-flipping criterion.
    The local and global methods are not identical algorithms. Delaunay triangulation chooses
a complete triangulation of the current nodal positions, including regions that are already
acceptable for the MTM. The edge-flip method approaches the same local geometric structure
through local changes and may stop before reaching the global Delaunay triangulation. It is
therefore a local Delaunay-like update, without a guarantee that the final triangulation is globally
Delaunay.
    This manuscript gives preference to edge flipping. A global reconstruction replaces the
triangulation of the complete current point set in one step. The local formulation instead exposes
each candidate change, allowing it to be tested against the neighborhood criterion, rejected when
necessary, and associated directly with its two parent elements. This control is particularly useful
when history variables are introduced in Chapter 4. It can also avoid rebuilding unaffected parts
of the mesh, although the performance advantage depends on the data structure and the number
of active regions. The more important advantage here is conceptual and algorithmic simplicity:
the entire topology update is reduced to one repeatable local operation.


3    Update of the reference state after reconnection
The geometric criterion and edge-flip algorithm determine the new mesh connections. To evaluate
energy and stress on the reconnected mesh, we must also specify the reference triangle used to
reconstruct each element’s deformation gradient.

Figure 3.8: Degenerate reference element. The following sequence of deformations and edge
flips leaves the mesh with an element with a degenerate reference configuration. Configuration 1
shows a mesh in its reference configuration, with four labeled and colored nodes i, j, k, l. Note
that the nodes i, k, l are collinear. Configurations 2–7 show a sequence of shears and edge flips.
In configuration 7, the nodes i, k, l form an element. Using their initial material positions as the
reference configuration gives the reference element zero area. In contrast, element ijl, illustrated
by dashed lines, now has a reference area twice as large as its current area.


3.1   Degenerate elements from naive node reference positions
A tempting way to construct the reference geometry of a reconnected element is to use the original
node positions of its new three-node set. This is problematic because nodes that become neighbors
in the current configuration could be collinear in the original mesh. Figure 3.8 illustrates this
through three slips and three edge flips. In the last mesh configuration, the newly formed element
ikl has collinear reference vertices. Since the reference triangle is degenerate, its Jacobian cannot
be inverted, so the deformation gradient cannot be constructed from these reference vertices.
The reference area of ikl is zero, while that of the neighboring element ijl is twice the original
single-element reference area.
    We avoid this problem by not constructing element references from the original positions of
newly connected nodes. Instead, every element before and after reconnection is interpreted as
a deformation of one canonical lattice reference triangle. The assigned gradient now describes
the current triangle relative to this canonical reference. It need not equal I even in the initial
mesh, because the ordered element edges may differ from the canonical edges by a lattice-basis
transformation. Changing connectivity at fixed nodes can also change that gradient. We call this
a loss of deformation history: the canonical-reference gradient alone does not retain the lattice
representation followed before reconnection. Chapter 4 introduces the transport of this history.




                                                  74

===== PDF page 86 of 215 =====

3. Update of the reference state after reconnection


3.2     Canonical reference element
To avoid the degenerate reference Jacobians, we introduce a canonical reference element. For the
square lattice, one possible choice is

                          X0 = (0, 0),        X1 = (1, 0),      X2 = (0, 1),                   (3.9)

where we implicitly use the length units discussed in Chapter 2, Section 1.1. Let

                         a = Xa − X0 ,
                        d(0)                     da = xa − x0 ,         a = 1, 2,            (3.10)

and current Cartesian bases are collected in the matrices D(0) and D, respectively:

                                   (0)

                                         a )I ,        Dia = (da )i ,                        (3.11)

The current vertices are stored counter-clockwise so that the element has positive orientation.
The local ordering also selects which two current edges are mapped to the two edges of the
canonical triangle.
    Equation (3.13) makes precise the shorthand F = D(D(0) )−1 . Throughout the remainder of
the thesis, the fixed Cartesian bases are understood and the symbol F is used for both the tensor
and its matrix representation, as explained in Appendix B.3.

3.2.1    Mechanical invariance under reference relabeling

The canonical triangle is a single representative, not a preferred physical lattice orientation. In
this subsection, we consider one element and suppress its element label. Subscripts can and orig
identify quantities evaluated using the canonical and original reference triangles, respectively. If
the original reference mesh is assembled from lattice-equivalent copies of the canonical triangle,
their reference-edge matrices satisfy

in Chapter 2.

Thus, whenever the original reference mesh is constructed from lattice-equivalent copies of the
canonical triangle, replacing those copies by the single canonical representative changes the
matrix representing F, but not the element energy, Cauchy stress or its contributions to the
nodal forces.

3.3     Affine and non-affine twin-element response after edge flips
An edge flip preserves the four nodal positions but changes the triangles from which the local
lattice state is reconstructed. With the canonical reference introduced above, the deformation
gradient assigned to an element with counter-clockwise vertices (1), (2), (3) is
                                                                                  
                                                          (2)   (1)    (3)   (1)
                                                     x − x1           x1 − x 1 
                       F = D(D(0) )−1 ,        D=    1
                                                          (2)   (1)    (3)   (1)       .            (3.22)
                                                         x2 − x 2     x2 − x 2

This definition uses only current nodal positions and the non-degenerate canonical reference. It
forgoes the original material configuration entirely: the original positions of newly connected
nodes may give a singular reference Jacobian. The price we pay is that F is no longer tied to that
original configuration: changing connectivity can change the lattice deformation it represents.
This section investigates how this change in connectivity affects the element deformation gradient
F, its energy, and its contributions to the nodal forces. We distinguish affine and non-affine
element pairs.
    We call an element pair affine if its four current nodes form a parallelogram.1 Otherwise, we
call the pair non-affine. An affine edge flip changes the diagonal of such a parallelogram while
keeping its four nodes fixed.
    In the following, we first show that an affine edge flip can change the represented lattice well
through an integer shear but preserves energy, stress and the summed force contribution at each
node. Second, we show that a non-affine edge flip can change the elastic states represented by
the triangles, so energy and stress need not be preserved. Figures 3.9 and 3.10 illustrate affine
and non-affine edge flips, respectively.

3.3.1    Affine edge flips

Figure 3.9 starts from a square with minor diagonal kj. Panel (b) applies one unit of simple
shear to the right, and panel (c) flips the edge kj to il with fixed nodal positions. The imposed
shear moves all four nodes by a common affine map; the flip then changes only the triangles used
to reconstruct F.
    For the example illustrated in Figure 3.9, the imposed shear matrix is

For the affine pair, both triangles therefore carry the same Cauchy stress before and after the
flip.
     However, for the nodal forces, the situation is different. Since the reference configuration is
the same, there is no canceling term from ∇X N (p) . Furthermore, unlike reference relabeling,
an edge flip changes which nodes belong to each element. In Fig. 3.9(b), node k receives a
force contribution from both triangles, klj and kji. After the flip, it belongs only to triangle
kli. At node l, the opposite happens: one contribution becomes two. Although the individual
contributions change, their sum from the pair at each node is preserved for an affine flip.
     To see why, consider one triangle in the current configuration and suppress its element label.
Its contribution at node p can be written as
                                                                 Z
                   f (p) = −V (0) P∇X N (p) = −V σ∇x N (p) = −             N (p) σn ds,      (3.30)

where V = JV (0) is its current area, Ω is the current triangle and n its outward unit normal. The
last equality follows from the divergence theorem, since the constant stress satisfies ∇x · σ = 0
within the triangle. Along an edge meeting p, the linear shape function decreases from one at p
to zero at the other endpoint; it vanishes on the opposite edge. Each edge meeting p therefore
contributes half its length times −σn.
    Now add the contributions from the two triangles. On their shared diagonal, the shape
functions agree and the outward normals are opposite, so the diagonal contributions cancel. Only
                                                                     (p)      (p)
the two outer edges meeting p remain. If their current lengths are ℓ1 and ℓ2 , and their outward
                    (p)       (p)
unit normals are n1 and n2 , the force contribution from the pair is

                                 (p)     1  (p) (p) (p) (p)

                                         2

The flip changes neither these outer edges nor the uniform stress. Thus, at node k, the single
contribution after the flip equals the sum of the two contributions before it. The same reasoning
applies at each of the other three nodes. Contributions from elements outside the pair are
unchanged, so the total nodal forces are unchanged as well.

Figure 3.10: Non-affine deformation and reconnection. (a) Reference square of unit side length,
with nodal positions Xa and diagonal il. (b) Node l is displaced to the right while i, j, k remain

to (c) can produce energy and stress jumps. In contrast, the affine edge flip in Fig. 3.9(b) and
(c) only changes the represented lattice well to an equivalent one, preserving energy and stress.


3.3.2   Non-affine edge flips

Take the square in Fig. 3.10(a) as the reference configuration for this example, with nodal
positions Xa . Panel (b) shows the current configuration: nodes i, j, k remain at their reference
positions, while l is displaced to the right. This deformation is non-affine: an affine map that
fixes three non-collinear points cannot move the fourth independently. The element gradients are
still evaluated using the canonical reference triangle defined above.
     Before the flip, in panel (b), both triangles ijl and ikl contain l, so both deformation gradients
depend on its displacement. After the flip, in panel (c), triangle ijk contains only the unchanged
nodes and represents an undeformed square-lattice state, up to lattice relabeling. Triangle kjl
retains the effect of the displacement of l. Thus, changing the diagonal changes the elastic
deformation represented by the elements, even though the nodal positions are identical in panels
(b) and (c).
     Unlike an affine edge flip, this change cannot in general be described by an integer shear
between lattice-equivalent wells. Lattice symmetry therefore does not guarantee that energy and
stress are preserved. The total energy can increase or decrease, and the resulting change in nodal
forces can require further mechanical relaxation.
     Section 4.4 measures these energy and stress changes in the simulations. Before presenting
those results, we describe how reconnection is combined with mechanical relaxation.


4    Algorithm and numerical results
The numerical tests address three questions: whether reconnection removes topological locking,
how it affects element conditioning, and what energy and stress changes the topology updates
introduce. We first describe the reconnection–relaxation cycle. We then examine sequential
dislocation crossings, element conditioning, and reconnection discontinuities before considering
collective plastic flow at large strain.



                                                  80

===== PDF page 92 of 215 =====

4. Algorithm and numerical results


4.1     ReMTM algorithm
Algorithm 2 provides the geometric topology update. We now combine it with mechanical
relaxation, accounting for the possible energy changes discussed in Section 3.3. At each imposed
loading step, mechanical relaxation and reconnection are performed as separate operations.
Relaxation changes nodal positions at fixed connectivity; reconnection changes connectivity at
fixed nodal positions. Because the latter can change the assigned elastic state, a trial reconnection
is followed by a new relaxation before deciding whether to accept it. Throughout this procedure
the loading and number of lattice sites remain fixed.

4.1.1    Reconnection–relaxation cycle

Relaxation after a topology update can produce further large local deformations and make other
mesh connections obsolete. Several reconnection–relaxation cycles may therefore be needed
within one loading step to allow dislocations to move without becoming locked again by the mesh.
Algorithm 3 embeds the reconnection update in a relaxation cycle at fixed external loading. Let
W (u, K) denote the total energy for nodal displacements u and topology K. Starting from a
relaxed state, we construct a trial topology at the same nodal positions and relax its energy.
Here Relax(u, K) denotes the numerical minimization initialized at u, over the displacements
admissible at the current imposed loading and fixed topology K. It returns an approximate local
minimum, not a guaranteed global minimizer. We define the net change between the trial and
accepted relaxed states as

                             ∆Eterm = W (uk+1 , Kk+1 ) − W (uk , Kk ).                        (3.32)

The implemented rule accepts a trial only when ∆Eterm < 0. The cycle repeats until no edge
is flipped or a trial gives ∆Eterm ≥ 0. In the latter case, the previously accepted topology and
nodal positions are restored. The comparison uses the computed energies directly, without a
separate acceptance tolerance; near equality, the decision can depend on minimization accuracy
and floating-point precision.

4.2     Resolving topological locking: sequential dislocation-crossing test
We first return to the sequential loading test that motivated reconnection in Section 1 and
Fig. 3.1. It tests whether a previous slip obstructs a later crossing. The boundary loading first
drives a horizontal dislocation through the sample and then drives a vertical one through the
previously slipped region. This is a boundary-controlled test, with dislocations entering and
leaving through free surfaces.
    Figure 3.11 complements the mesh snapshots with the energy response. Fixed connectivity
retains the distorted band created by the first slip and obstructs the subsequent crossing,
producing an abrupt energy increase. With reconnection, both passages remain accessible and
the energy returns toward the defect-free level after completed slip. The agreement between
the defect trajectories and energy response identifies removal of topological locking, beyond an


                                                 81

===== PDF page 93 of 215 =====

Chapter 3. Reconnecting in the MTM


Algorithm 3 Reconnection–relaxation cycle at fixed external loading
 1: k ← 0
 2: u0 ← Relax(u0 , K0 )
 3: while true do
 4:    Copy Kk into Kk+1
 5:    Copy uk into uk+1
 6:    Apply edge flipping (Algorithm 2) to Kk+1                ▷ Reconnect the whole mesh
 7:    if no edge was flipped then
 8:        return (uk , Kk )                                                     ▷ Exit loop
 9:    else
10:        uk+1 ← Relax(uk , Kk+1 )
11:        ∆Eterm = W (uk+1 , Kk+1 ) − W (uk , Kk )
12:        if ∆Eterm ≥ 0 then                                 ▷ Reject a non-improving trial
13:           return (uk , Kk )                     ▷ Discard new connectivity and exit loop
14:        else
15:           k ←k+1                                 ▷ Continue reconnect and minimize loop


improvement in triangle shape alone. Collective periodic shear is considered separately below.

4.3   Element conditioning under simple shear
Reconnection also improves element conditioning under accumulated shear. With fixed connec-
tivity, increasingly distorted current triangles lead to a poorly conditioned Hessian of the element
energy with respect to nodal displacements. In homogeneous simple-shear tests, edge flipping
suppresses this additional deterioration: the condition-number curves from successive shear
intervals coincide when plotted against shear modulo one. Conditioning still varies within each
period, reflecting the changing elastic state. Appendix A.2 presents these tests and distinguishes
the geometric and constitutive contributions to element conditioning.
    A test of mesh effects on a single-dislocation core is given in Appendix A.1.

4.4   Reconnection discontinuities
The preceding tests examine the removal of topological locking and the improvement in element
conditioning. We now assess the changes in energy and stress introduced by reconnection itself.
Section 3.3 distinguishes integer shear jumps between equivalent wells from changes in elastic
state under non-affine reconnection. Here we measure the latter in the simulations.
    Figure 3.12 illustrates this distinction for the energy of the whole mesh. A topology update
produces an instantaneous jump ∆Erec , after which minimization lowers the energy on the new
topology. Each topology update is a complete call to Algorithm 2, which may contain many edge
flips. Acceptance of the new mesh connectivity depends on the net change between relaxed states,
not on the instantaneous jump. The final rejected trial has a higher relaxed energy, indicated by
∆Eterm > 0, and the previous state is restored.
    In the relaxation shown in Figure 3.12, the ∆Erec jumps are of a similar magnitude to the net
energy decrease ∆Erelax . These jumps raise the concern that reconnection may perturb the local

elastic states and trigger rearrangements that would otherwise have required further loading. To
assess their magnitude locally, we measure the net energy change ∆E (e) = E (e),+ − E (e),− for
each retained element label e. Here, − and + denote the states immediately before and after the
whole edge-flip reconnection algorithm (Algorithm 2), at fixed nodal positions and before the next
minimization.2 The correspondence is the stored parent-to-child label assignment, not a unique
matching of material regions. Swapping the two child labels can change individual differences and
their histogram, although it leaves the sum of the two energies unchanged. These signed changes

the topology update. The mean absolute energy and shear-stress changes are about ten times
smaller than their respective benchmark scales. This comparison characterizes the size of the
changes, but does not determine whether they trigger a plastic rearrangement. A system can
be arbitrarily close to an instability, so even a small change may trigger an event that would
otherwise occur at a later strain step.
    Nevertheless, reconnection is intended to restore motions that fixed connectivity obstructs.
An instantaneous energy increase is therefore not by itself evidence that the update should be
rejected: acceptance is decided after relaxation. Reconnecting the fixed-connectivity endpoint in
Figure 3.11, without moving the nodes or minimizing, raises its total energy from approximately
8.07 to 11.61. The blue curve instead follows reconnection and relaxation throughout loading and
reaches a near-zero final energy. These are distinct loading histories, illustrating the difference

4.5                     Simulation results: grain-structure evolution under 500% shear
Having examined sequential slip and reconnection discontinuities, we now consider collective
plastic flow under continued simple shear with periodic boundaries. We first compare the spatial
organization of the deformed samples and then their energy and stress as loading proceeds.
    Figure 3.14 shows two 200 × 200 samples, both at γ = 0.7. They are loaded using the same
parameters, the only difference being that (a) uses a fixed mesh, and (b) uses edge flipping to
reconnect its mesh. Two differences immediately stand out: with reconnection, the energy is
lower and concentrated cleanly in lines, reminiscent of grain boundaries. Without reconnection,
high-energy elements are more numerous, and although some lines could be identified as the
same grain-boundary-like borders, they are not as clearly defined.

4.5.1                                     Grain-like organization at large strain

We described the lines of high-energy elements shown in Figure 3.14(b) as being reminiscent of
grain boundaries. To examine this interpretation, we compare a geometric proxy for local lattice

                                  0.025         0.050   0.075   0.100   0.125   0.150       0.175       0.200

Figure 3.14: Element-energy fields with and without reconnection. Two 200 × 200 samples are
loaded with identical loading parameters, but one uses edge flipping, and the other has a static
mesh topology. The two plots show the mesh of each sample at a load of γ = 0.7. The black
dashed lines outline a cell of the periodic tiling under the imposed macroscopic shear. (a) The
mesh with no reconnection has a higher density of high-energy white/red elements compared to
the mesh with reconnection. (b) The mesh with reconnection has large regions of low-energy
elements, separated by jagged lines of high-energy elements.


orientation across the domains. Figure 3.15 compares the domains at γ = 0.7 with and without
reconnection. The color is a geometric proxy for lattice orientation: the direction of the longest
triangle edge, shifted by π/4 and taken modulo π/2. For an undistorted square-lattice triangle,
the longest edge is a lattice diagonal, so this shift recovers a lattice-axis direction modulo square
symmetry.3
    For a locally square arrangement of nodes, edges satisfying the neighborhood criterion follow
the axial or diagonal directions, providing the geometric basis for this proxy. It is not a pure
rotation measure: elastic distortion can change both edge directions and which edge is the longest.
Moreover, mesh connections without reconnection need not follow local lattice directions. For this
reason, the fixed-connectivity sample in panel (a) is Delaunay-triangulated only for visualization;
its simulated nodal positions are retained. The displayed triangulation must not be mistaken for
the connectivity used during loading.
    Both samples exhibit domains, but their boundaries are more clearly organized with recon-
nection. The emergence of such grain-like structures is a major achievement of the reconnected
MTM: it suggests a qualitative connection with the grain refinement observed experimentally
under severe plastic deformation [153]. This organization arises spontaneously from the collective
plastic dynamics, without prescribing grains or rules for grain-boundary formation. Here, the
description grain-like reflects the use of a geometric proxy for lattice orientation. Chapter 4
provides a complementary orientation measurement from the elastic part of the deformation
gradient and revisits the comparison in Fig. 4.17.
  3
      The π/4 shift makes the colors comparable to the orientation plot in Chapter 4, Fig. 4.17.

Figure 3.15: Mesh orientation analysis. The figure shows two samples loaded to γ = 0.7
without (a) and with (b) reconnection. The color is the geometric orientation proxy (θℓmax −
π/4) mod (π/2), where θℓmax is the angle of an element’s longest edge with the x-axis. In (a),
Delaunay triangulation is applied only during post-processing of the fixed-connectivity simulation;
it changes the displayed edges, while retaining the simulated nodal positions. Grain-like domains
are more clearly defined with reconnection. The insets show selected regions, with white circles
representing nodes.


4.5.2   Steady state and energy evolution at large strain

Under continued loading, a system may approach a statistically steady flow regime called a steady
state. Individual plastic events continue to occur, but the statistics of suitable observables can
become approximately unchanged over successive loading intervals [154]. Sampling events after
their distribution has stabilized helps ensure that the pooled data describe one regime of plastic
flow [133]. If the event-size distribution is still evolving, pooling events across that interval can
obscure the interpretation of a fitted power law.
    Over the simulated loading intervals, reconnection changes the energy trend from continued
accumulation to fluctuations about an approximately stable baseline. Figure 3.16 extends the
energy comparison for a 200 × 200 sample. The fixed-connectivity calculation is shown up to
γ = 1, over which its energy continues to rise. The reconnected calculation reaches γ = 5 and
fluctuates around an approximately stationary baseline. The plotted interval supports removal
of sustained mesh-induced energy accumulation; it does not establish a mathematical divergence
for the fixed mesh or exact stationarity of the reconnected one.
    Figure 3.17 shows that the dip in energy near γ = 4 coincides with a marked reduction of the
grain-like boundary structure. Whether this growth and shrinkage is recurrent, how it depends
on system size, and whether periodic self-interaction contributes remain open questions. The
snapshots alone do not establish a reproducible cycle.

===== PDF page 101 of 215 =====

Chapter 3. Reconnecting in the MTM


Conclusion
In this chapter, we investigated how the fixed Lagrangian connectivity of the MTM limits large-
strain plastic flow. The same mesh both fixes the mesoscopic length scale and specifies which
nodes are used together to reconstruct element deformation gradients. Keeping this topology
fixed is therefore acceptable only as long as the mesh connections remain compatible with the
evolving node geometry.
    The chapter began by illustrating the constraint that fixed connectivity can impose on
dislocation glide. Previous lattice-invariant slip can then be stored as numerical memory and act
as an artificial obstacle, which we identified as topological locking in Section 1.
    To identify when the connectivity should change, we expressed the neighborhood condition in
terms of the current mesh-edge metric. We then introduced local edge flips and an algorithm that
selects twin pairs using this criterion. The comparison with Delaunay triangulation established
their geometric connection for Bravais-lattice node arrangements.
    A second result concerns the reference configuration after reconnection. Using the original
material positions of newly connected nodes can lead to degenerate reference Jacobians. We
therefore assign a canonical lattice reference triangle to every element. With this choice, lattice-
equivalent changes of representation leave the energy, Cauchy stress and nodal forces unchanged
under affine deformation. Non-affine fields can still generate interpolation jumps, but these are
treated inside the reconnection–relaxation cycle.
    The resulting method removes mesh-induced locking, improves the relevant conditioning
measures, and allows continued plastic flow with an approximately stable energy baseline over
the tested loading interval. This energy trend alone does not establish stationarity of all flow
statistics. At large strain, the reconnected simulations develop grain-boundary-like structures
in the two-dimensional periodic setting. The next step is to keep track of what reconnection
removes from the deformation history. This is the purpose of Chapter 4, which introduces a
history-corrected description of deformation and the transport of plastic history through edge
flips.

    The multiwell energy landscape of the MTM describes elastic distortion and plastic rear-
rangement within the same deformation-gradient field. This is one of the strengths of the model:
plastic mechanisms emerge through transitions between lattice-equivalent energy wells rather
than being prescribed through a separate flow rule. It also creates an interpretive difficulty. The
total deformation gradient identifies both the elastic state within a well and the lattice well in
which that state is represented, without separating the two.
    This chapter introduces an integer-valued multiplicative decomposition F = Fe Fp to dis-
tinguish these roles. The elastic factor Fe places the state in a selected fundamental elastic

                                                    91

===== PDF page 103 of 215 =====

Chapter 4. Elastoplastic Decomposition and Plastic-History Transport


well, while the plastic factor Fp records the associated lattice transformation. This follows the
kinematic structure used in finite-strain crystal plasticity, adapted here to the discrete lattice
symmetry of the MTM. The resulting variables allow elastic states, lattice rotations, plastic
deformations and plastic-event increments to be analyzed separately.
    To construct the decomposition, we introduce a plastic reduction that composes orientation-
preserving integer basis changes. We distinguish this operation from Lagrange reduction, whose
role is to evaluate the lattice-invariant energy and which need not retain the accumulated plastic
transformation. We then use the decomposition for a second purpose. After mesh reconnection,
only the accumulated plastic history is transported to the new elements, while their elastic
distortion is obtained from the new element geometry. The final part of the chapter uses these
quantities to analyze reconnected simulations.


1     Integer-valued multiplicative elastoplastic decomposition
At finite strain, continuum crystal-plasticity models commonly separate the deformation gradient
multiplicatively into elastic and plastic factors; see Section 3.3 in Chapter 1 and Refs. [9, 104]. The
elastic factor describes recoverable lattice stretching and lattice rotation, whereas the plastic factor
records the deformation accumulated through crystallographic slip. The decomposition therefore
supplies internal variables that distinguish the current elastic response from the irreversible
deformation history.
    The same distinction is useful in the MTM even though the model does not need an
explicit plastic variable to generate plastic flow. Transitions between the equivalent wells of its
lattice-invariant energy already represent plastic rearrangements. Introducing an elastoplastic
decomposition does not alter that energy or the resulting mechanical equilibrium; it provides a
way to identify and analyze the elastic and plastic information already contained in the multi-well
state.
    The total deformation gradient alone does not uniquely determine its elastic and plastic
factors [155, 156]. Here, we examine decompositions related by square-lattice symmetry. We
select a representative without an additional compensating material rotation of the two factors,
using the shortest-path argument developed in Section 2.2.1.

1.1    Multiplicative decomposition
An elastic–plastic (elastoplastic) decomposition separates the elastic deformation within a local
energy well from the integer lattice transformation relating that well to the fundamental well.
We identify the transformation from the fundamental well to the current well with the plastic
deformation and write
                                           F = Fe Fp ,                                      (4.1)

where Fp is the plastic deformation and Fe describes the elastic deformation represented in the
fundamental well. Elastic recovery F−1

well. The transformation relating this minimum to the fundamental minimum characterizes its

1.3   Ambiguities of the metric-based elastoplastic decomposition
There is a source of ambiguity related to the quadrants of the fundamental domain. As mentioned,
the reduction target De does not uniquely determine Mp .
    We can use a transformation in GL(2, Z) to reduce C into the fundamental elastic well De .
Lagrange reduction maps C to a reduced metric C   e in the fundamental domain D , one quadrant

of the fundamental well. Using the two flip operations from the Lagrange reduction M(1) and
M(2) , we can navigate to the other quadrants of the elastic well, but we still do not know which
quadrant is the correct one.

us to the same quadrant, effectively turning once around the center of the disk, changes the sign

                          Mp M(1) M(2) M(1) M(2) = Mp M2R = −Mp ,                           (4.8)

is a 90◦ rotation.
    At first sight, this may seem inconsistent with the statement that the metric representation
removes rotations. The simple explanation is that the two rotations act on different sides of the
deformation gradient. A superposed spatial rotation acts on the deformed vectors and therefore
multiplies F from the left, whereas a reduction transformation acts on the reference vectors
supplied to F and therefore multiplies it from the right. Writing R for a spatial rotation, we
obtain

                           F′ = RFM,                                                       (4.10)
                          C′ = F′T F′ = MT FT RT RFM = MT CM.                              (4.11)

Thus, C removes the superposed spatial rotation R, but it does not in general remove the
material transformation M.
   More precisely, as discussed in Section 1.2.1, F is a two-point tensor: it maps reference
vectors expressed in the fixed Cartesian basis {Ea } to current vectors expressed in the fixed
Cartesian basis {ea }. The spatial rotation R therefore acts on current vectors, whereas the
material transformation M acts on reference vectors. The transformed deformation gradient is
the composition
                                       F′ = R ◦ F ◦ M.                                  (4.12)

Here, M is the material map used to construct a lattice-equivalent representative; the fixed
reference basis remains unchanged, so this is not a passive change of coordinates. The right
Cauchy–Green tensor is the material bilinear form defined, for reference vectors U and V, by

                              C(U, V) := U · CV = (FU) · (FV).                             (4.13)

It consequently transforms according to

                               C′ (U, V) = (RFMU) · (RFMV)                                 (4.14)
                                          = C(MU, MV),                                     (4.15)

whose matrix representation in the fixed reference basis is C′ = MT CM. The preceding left–right
multiplication rule is therefore the matrix representation of the two-point tensor composition,
rather than an independent matrix-ordering convention.
   Although MR has the matrix of a 90◦ rotation, it acts on the material argument of F, not

                                                 95

===== PDF page 107 of 215 =====

Chapter 4. Elastoplastic Decomposition and Plastic-History Transport


as a superposed rotation of the current configuration. It can therefore change C and move
its representation to the diagonally opposite quadrant. On the other hand, M2R = −I, and
bilinearity gives
                                      (−I)T C(−I) = C.                               (4.16)

Hence M and −M define the same point in metric space, even though they retain different
rotational information in the deformation-gradient decomposition.
    The corresponding change of decomposition can be written explicitly. Replacing Mp by
Mp = Mp MR gives
c
                         b e = Fe M ,
                         F                b p = MT Fp ,
                                          F               F
                                                          b eFb p = F.             (4.17)
                                   R              R

The rotation acts on the material side of the elastic factor and is compensated in the plastic
factor, leaving the total deformation unchanged. This is a discrete square-lattice instance of
the rotational freedom discussed in [155]. Here, choosing no additional rotation means selecting
among these symmetry-related factorizations; it does not require the physical lattice rotation
carried by Fe to vanish.
    There is one more clarification before moving on: the Poincaré disk does not show the sign
of det F, since det C = (det F)2 . The Lagrange-reduction transformations M(1) and M(2) have
determinant −1, so right multiplication by either reverses the sign of det F while leaving det C
unchanged. For the unit-area states illustrated in Figure 4.3, both det F = 1 and det F = −1
therefore give det C = 1. To retain this orientation information, the figure uses two copies of
the same metric disk, labeled by the sign of det F. A transformation with negative determinant
switches between the copies. The figure also shows two symmetry-related deformation-gradient
states for each quadrant and determinant sign.
    With these clarifications, we are prepared to tackle the question of how to construct a plastic
reduction algorithm. There are two key aspects to remember from this section. First, Lagrange
reduction can use transformations with negative determinant. For the orientation-preserving
total deformation considered here, det F > 0, a reduction with det Mp = −1 gives det Fe < 0
and det Fp < 0. Both factors would reverse orientation, so we exclude such reductions from
the admissible decomposition. Second, a reduction to the same point in the Poincaré disk can
lead to two different elastoplastic decompositions; this ambiguity must be addressed before the
reduction algorithm is fully specified.

1.4     Naive stress-sign selection criterion
To develop intuition, we first consider a one-dimensional analog of the two-dimensional Poincaré-
disk energy landscape, then examine a stress-sign selection rule and its limitations. We use a
deliberately simple energy and reduction rule; fully developed one-dimensional MTM-like models
are given in [133, 157, 158].

1.4.1    1D Lagrange-like reduction

We define Φ0simple (C) = −C 3 , a cubic polynomial in the scalar C. We introduce a simple
reduction RLb that maps any point outside the domain DLb = [−1, 0] back into DLb through
three steps. Change the sign of C such that C ≥ 0. Repeatedly subtract 2 until −1 ≤ C ≤ 1.
If C > 0, change the sign of C. We can then define the globally periodic energy landscape
Φsimple (C) = Φ0simple (RLb (C)) as shown in Figure 4.4. This construction parallels the use of DL ,
RL , and Φ in Chapter 2: reduction extends an energy defined on a fundamental domain to a
globally periodic landscape.
    Although this reduction defines a globally periodic energy landscape, the reduced point loses
the information identifying the half-well from which it originated. We would like to construct
a reduction algorithm Rp̂ that preserves which half of the local energy well the original point
comes from. In other words, point c should be reduced to b, not to a. In this 1D example, one
solution is to modify the reduction algorithm such that we only use shifts, and no flips: If C > 1,
subtract 2. If C < −1, add 2. Repeat until −1 ≤ C ≤ 1. This is essentially the 1D analog of the
plastic reduction algorithm we will later present. This is not, however, the only option.
    An alternative method for constructing a plastic reduction Rp,2 is to look at the slope ∂Φ/∂C.
We can see that this slope distinguishes point a from point b in Figure 4.4. By comparing the
slope at c with those at the candidate points a and b in the fundamental well, we can choose
the option which preserves the slope. This approach allows us to reuse the previous reduction
algorithm. The alternative plastic reduction Rp,2 can be described as follows: First reduce C
using RLb . If the reduced and unreduced points have the same slope, we are done. If they do not,
we flip the sign of Ce to end up in the correct half of the energy well.
    The alternative plastic reduction algorithm Rp,2 has the advantage of reusing the existing
reduction algorithm RLb , and only adding a small extra step at the end. That makes it attractive
as a first attempt, and it works well in the 1D example. It turns out, however, that in the 2D
Poincaré disk, this approach does not work.

Figure 4.5 shows one of several attempts at making this gradient-based approach work. Here we
calculate the second Piola–Kirchhoff stress S at every point in the disk. We then color the disk

    The reason why this particular approach fails is not fully understood, but two aspects stand
out as important factors. Firstly, the Poincaré disk represents the two-dimensional surface
det C = 1 within the three-dimensional space of symmetric positive-definite tensors C. Although
an energy well is clearly visible on this surface (see Fig. 2.10), the second Piola–Kirchhoff stress
is obtained by differentiating the energy in the full metric space. Its components therefore do
not directly describe the slope of the energy as displayed on the disk.

why the chosen stress signs do not distinguish the desired sectors. For now, we conclude only
that this particular sign-based construction does not provide a physical plastic reduction.
    In this section, we have shown how the Lagrange reduction algorithm navigates the Poincaré
disk, and how a reduction transformation M gives a multiplicative elastoplastic decomposition of
the deformation gradient. We also presented two approaches to plastic reduction in a 1D analog,
Rp̂ and Rp,2 , and examined why the particular gradient-based construction does not extend
successfully to two dimensions. In the next section, we show that an approach analogous to Rp̂
gives a tiling compatible with the GL(2, Z) symmetries and defines an elastoplastic decomposition.
We compare alternative admissible reductions using the shortest-path criterion, which favors the
fewest elementary basis changes needed to reach the fundamental elastic well. We then show
how the additional steps in the longer paths considered correspond to compensating material
rotations, motivating our preference for a decomposition without such additional rotations.

2    Plastic reduction algorithm
Perchikov and Truskinovsky [135] proposed a plastic-reduction algorithm motivated by the
evolving metric crossing boundaries of the fundamental elastic domain. For a given metric
C, the authors first assume that the plastic reduction matrix Mp , and hence the elastically
reduced metric Ce = MT    p CMp , is known. By tracking which boundary is crossed by Ce , they
determine how to update Mp so that the recomputed Ce lies inside the fundamental elastic
domain. Knowing the initial value of Mp poses no difficulty in simulations initialized with every
element in this domain, for which one can take Mp = I. The authors also give an iterative
algorithm that constructs a multi-step reduction directly from C, without information about
previous states. This construction is physically motivated, although the choice of reduction
path is not examined in detail. Here, we systematically explore alternative reduction paths and
provide two further motivations for the same algorithm: shortest-path reduction and greedy
minimization of the sum of squared basis-vector lengths. We compare the resulting elastoplastic
decomposition with the other admissible decompositions and discuss why the path selected by
the algorithm should be preferred.
    Lagrange reduction is a geometric lattice-reduction procedure. The target of plastic reduction
also depends on the chosen lattice symmetry: the fundamental elastic domains of square and
triangular crystals differ. We restrict the discussion to square-lattice potentials; a formulation
covering both square and triangular symmetries is outside the scope of this thesis.




                                                101

===== PDF page 113 of 215 =====

Chapter 4. Elastoplastic Decomposition and Plastic-History Transport


2.1    Local integer-shear rule
The one-dimensional plastic reduction Rp̂ uses shifts rather than reflections. In two dimensions, we
replace the determinant-−1 operations of Lagrange reduction by the four orientation-preserving in-
teger shears M(12) (1), M(12) (−1), M(21) (1), and M(21) (−1). We call these operations elementary
basis changes. The remaining question is which operation to choose at each step.
    Following [135], the idea of the plastic reduction algorithm is to reduce the metric using
the four elementary basis changes. Each boundary of the fundamental elastic domain De is
associated with one of these basis changes, as illustrated in Figure 4.6. If a point crosses one
of these boundaries, it is reduced by applying the basis change that would bring it back to the
fundamental well. This boundary-crossing motivation applies directly to the neighboring wells,
but relies on knowing which boundary was crossed.
    We could obtain the same reduction algorithm as in [135] by a different motivation, which is the
same motivation used by Lagrange reduction: minimization of the sum of the basis-vector lengths.
Lagrange reduction seeks a lattice basis whose vector lengths attain the successive minima 1 ,
thereby minimizing their sum over all bases of the same lattice. This length-minimization
property does not require a unique choice of basis, and additional selection criteria are needed
for uniqueness of the reduced basis [141]. The primary reduction objective is the minimization of
the sum of the lengths of the basis vectors; the remaining considerations are simply to provide
uniqueness.2 For the local comparison below, we use the sum of squared basis-vector lengths,
C11 + C22 . The same choice minimizes the sum of lengths: the two candidates share the same
replacement vector and differ only in which old vector is retained. We refer to the squared
objective as the basis-length objective.
    Recall first that we use a unit square reference lattice basis. The current lattice basis vectors
aa = FEa therefore satisfy Gab = aa · ab = EaI CIJ EbJ . Since the reference basis is aligned
with the Cartesian axes, the arrays Gab and CIJ are numerically identical. Thus, C11 and C22
are their squared lengths, and C12 is their mutual dot product. This allows us to describe the
basis-length objective directly through the components of C.
    We now show that when greedily optimizing for the largest reduction in the basis-length
objective, we are always left with one elementary change of basis, up to symmetry points and
when C11 = C22 . Repeatedly choosing this remaining option results in the algorithm given
in [135]. Consider the effect of the four elementary basis changes on the diagonal components of
C. Under C′ = MT CM, applying M(12) (k), with k = ±1, gives

                                ′                  ′
                               C11 = C11 ,        C22 = C22 + C11 + 2kC12 .

Applying M(21) (k) instead gives

                                ′                                   ′
                               C11 = C11 + C22 + 2kC12 ,           C22 = C22 .
   1
     The ith successive minimum is the smallest radius of a ball centered at the origin that contains i linearly
independent lattice vectors.
   2
     This motivation holds for two and three dimensions, but remarkably, in higher dimensions, successive minima
do not necessarily form bases. [141]

Figure 4.6: Plastic reduction illustration. The four boundaries of the fundamental reduction

In either case, one squared length remains unchanged, while the other becomes C11 + C22 + 2kC12 .
    We first choose between addition and subtraction. The smaller new squared length is obtained
by choosing k with the opposite sign to C12 : we subtract when C12 > 0 and add when C12 < 0.
The new squared length is then
                                       C11 + C22 − 2|C12 |.

   It remains to decide which vector to replace. Suppose C11 ≤ C22 . Applying M(12) (k) leaves
the shorter vector unchanged and gives the total squared length

                                               C11 + C11 + C22 − 2|C12 |.

Applying M(21) (k) instead leaves the longer vector unchanged and gives

                                               C22 + C11 + C22 − 2|C12 |.

The first expression is no larger than the second, so replacing the longer vector gives the smallest
sum of squared lengths. If C22 < C11 , the same argument applies with the indices exchanged.
    Finally, outside the fundamental elastic well, 2|C12 | > min(C11 , C22 ). The new squared
length is therefore smaller than max(C11 , C22 ), so the selected operation strictly decreases the
basis-length objective.
    To summarize the plastic reduction algorithm Rp : if C11 ≤ C22 , we apply

                                                     M(12) (− sign C12 ) .                                                 (4.19)

Otherwise apply
                                                     M(21) (− sign C12 ) .                                                 (4.20)

Repeat this step until 2|C12 | ≤ min(C11 , C22 ). At equality, retain the current representative. It
remains now to show that this reduction is preferable over all other alternatives.




                                                                   103

Chapter 4. Elastoplastic Decomposition and Plastic-History Transport


2.2   Search using SL(2, Z) generators
Two lattice bases generate the same lattice if and only if A = BM for some M ∈ GL(2, Z), as
discussed in Chapter 2. Thus every lattice-preserving candidate reduction belongs to GL(2, Z). For
det F > 0, requiring both factors to preserve orientation restricts the admissible transformations
to SL(2, Z).
    By using a set of generators of SL(2, Z) and their inverses (the elementary basis changes):
M (1), M(12) (−1), M(21) (1) and M(21) (−1), we can explore all valid plastic reduction possi-
   (12)

bilities by using breadth-first search. From any starting point C, we sequentially add one of
the elementary basis changes until we reach the fundamental elastic well. This is illustrated
in Figure 4.7. In Figure 4.7a, we see an initial C marked by a blue marker. The gray vectors
show the destinations obtained when each elementary basis change is applied. The number next
to each vector indicates the number of steps taken to get from the marker to the end of the
vector. This shows a breadth-first search of depth one. Figure 4.7b extends the search, showing
all possible locations that can be reached from the initial marker using two elementary basis
changes. Here, one path of vectors is colored in green, as this path successfully reduced the
marker into the fundamental elastic well. Searching further, Figure 4.7c shows that after three
steps, the diagonally opposite quadrant can be reached in two different ways. These paths are
shown in red. The gray vectors that do not reach the fundamental elastic well are hidden for
clarity. Finally, Figure 4.7d shows that after four steps, there is a second path which also reduces
to the first quadrant, also shown in green.
    Recall that each path of vectors represents a sequence of elementary basis changes, that can
be combined to form a transformation M ∈ SL(2, Z). Every M ∈ SL(2, Z) that reduces the
initial C into the fundamental elastic domain can be considered as a candidate plastic reduction,
but we currently lack a way to clearly prefer one over the other. In this example, one quadrant
is reached before the others, but the diagonally opposite quadrant is reached in the same number
of steps following two different paths. Furthermore, these two paths produce different reduction
transformations that result in different elastoplastic decompositions. It is therefore not obvious
whether or not we could potentially use the quadrant that is reached first as a qualifier to
uniquely select a plastic reduction candidate. This is investigated in Figure 4.8. Each pixel in
the two disks represents an initial starting point for a breadth-first search. After one depth has
been completed, the next depth is only computed if no paths lead to the fundamental elastic
well. Figure 4.8a shows how many steps are needed to reach the fundamental elastic well. There
are white regions along the borders of the disk, indicating C that did not reach the fundamental
elastic well within the maximum search depth of 12. The color of each pixel in Figure 4.8b
represents how many paths from that pixel reached the fundamental elastic well, called the
multiplicity. At resolved sampled points away from well boundaries, the shortest path found has
multiplicity one. Some boundary states have multiple shortest paths. This is numerical evidence
within the sampled grid and search depth, not a proof of global uniqueness.

2.2.1            Preference of the shortest path

The shortest-path criterion provides a natural selection principle: among admissible reductions, it
favors those requiring the fewest elementary lattice-basis changes. This preference is analogous in
spirit to the variational construction of Ortiz and Repetto, in which minimizing-work deformation
paths define an incremental energy potential [159]. The following comparison clarifies what the
additional steps represent: in the example considered, the longer reduction paths are equivalent
to adding a compensating material rotation to the elastic and plastic factors.
    Consider some initial deformation gradient F, and its corresponding point C = FT F in
the Poincaré disk shown in Figure 4.9. When limited to SL(2, Z) transformations, only two
quadrants can be reached, each with two separate paths and associated decompositions, giving
                                          ′            ′
four reduction options in total: M1 , M1 , M4 and M4 . We notice that these decompositions are
rotations3 of each other. We already saw these exact reduction options in Figure 4.3, also labeled
        ′             ′
M1 , M1 , M4 and M4 . There however, we were not taking different paths using elementary basis
transformations, but rather directly applying MR rotations. This suggests that taking one of
these longer paths is equivalent to performing a rotation. Indeed, consider the red and blue paths
                        ′
associated with the M1 and M4 reduction transformations in Figure 4.9. The blue path consists
of a single M(12) (1) transformation, and the red path consists of first a M(21) (1) followed by a
M(12) (−1) transformation. If we compose the two paths, by reversing the blue path, and then
following the red, we recover a rotation:

                                                                                                                                     0 −1
                                                                                                                                               !
                                    ′
                                M−1
                                 4 M1 = M
                                         (12)
                                              (−1)M(21) (1)M(12) (−1) =                                                                              = MT
                                                                                                                                                        R.                                      (4.21)
                                                                                                                                     1 0
   The alternative reduction paths therefore produce decompositions related by the compensating
material rotations in Eq. (4.17). In the comparison shown, the longer paths add such a rotation
      3
   These rotations are powers of MR . They act on the material side of Fe and are compensated in Fp , leaving F
unchanged, as in Eq. (4.17). They are distinct from a superposed spatial rotation of the current configuration.

                         (a)                                          (b)

Figure 4.8: Multiplicity of shortest path to fundamental elastic well. (a) The figure shows a
501 × 501 grid over the Poincaré disk, where each pixel inside the unit circle represents a C.
Each valid C is used as a starting point for a breadth-first search, exploring one depth at a

path finds the fundamental elastic well. Each pixel is colored by how many steps were required

show a multiplicity of 2, as they correspond to points on well boundaries. The remaining purple
regions near the border represent metrics unresolved within the maximum search depth. The
resolved sampled points support uniqueness away from well boundaries; the finite search does
not establish global uniqueness.


relative to the shortest path. We choose the shortest-path representative to avoid introducing
this additional rotation into the elastic and plastic factors. This is a selection principle for the
decomposition, while physical lattice rotation remains part of Fe . We implement this choice by
following the shortest path in a breadth-first search, or by greedily minimizing the total basis
length, as discussed in Section 2.1. A practical implementation is given in the following section.
    This criterion selects a decomposition from the current state, but does not guarantee recovery
of the actual deformation history. As Ortiz and Repetto emphasize, actual deformation histories
need not be minimizing paths [159].

2.3   Practical implementation
An algorithm for the plastic reduction Rp is described in Algorithm 4. At each step it selects one
elementary basis change and checks whether the metric has reached the fundamental reduction
domain. As in Lagrange reduction, the full transformation is obtained by composing the applied
basis changes, Mp = M1 M2 · · · Mn , where Mi is the transformation applied at step i.
