 7:            Mi ← M(12) (1)                                     ▷ Add basis vector 1 to basis vector 2.
 8:        else
 9:            Mi ← M(12) (−1)                        ▷ Subtract basis vector 1 from basis vector 2.
10:    else
11:        if C12 > 0 then
12:            Mi ← M(21) (−1)                        ▷ Subtract basis vector 2 from basis vector 1.
13:        else
14:            Mi ← M(21) (1)                                     ▷ Add basis vector 2 to basis vector 1.
15:    Mp ← Mp Mi                                                          ▷ Accumulate the reduction.
16:    C ← MT   i CMi                                                                    ▷ Transform C.
17: return Mp , C                                                              ▷ The final metric is Ce .

Chapter 4. Elastoplastic Decomposition and Plastic-History Transport


3     Plastic-history transport through reconnection
As discussed in Chapter 3, the canonical reference element allows the mesh to reconnect without
producing degenerate reference elements. In the current implementation, however, reconnection
changes the deformation gradient of the represented lattice, causing a loss of deformation history.
We therefore introduce a deformation-gradient-like quantity T. Unlike F, T is invariant under an
affine edge flip when compatible elastic representatives are selected on both sides. It is allowed
to change when a non-affine flip changes the represented elastic state.
    History transport also requires assigning each new element to an old donor. We first define
this inheritance convention, then assess its consequences after introducing T.

3.1    Element inheritance across an edge flip
How do we transport element properties in an edge flip? The left side of Figure 4.10 shows a
green element (G) and a blue element (B) before an edge flip. The right side shows two options
for the new left (L) and right (R) elements after the flip. For illustration purposes, shared nodes
are drawn as separate overlapping nodes. For an edge flip, there are only two inheritance options.
The left element can inherit the plastic history of G, giving Lg and Rb illustrated in the top
right, or it can inherit the history of B, giving Lb and Rg illustrated in the bottom right. In this
section, we explain how we choose between these options.
    Consider just the element G and its three green nodes. Compare the position of these nodes
to the positions of the nodes in element Lg : two of the nodes are stationary, while the third has
“moved”. This movement is illustrated by a dashed circle indicating the node’s pre-flip position
and an arrow indicating its new position. The figure shows this move for the other element and
inheritance option as well. None of the nodes actually move during the edge flip, but by splitting
and coloring the nodes as we have done in the figure, it appears as though they move. Let the
total length of the two apparent node movements that occur during the flip be the flip-distance.
We use the inheritance option that maximizes the flip-distance as a modeling convention. Equal
distances are resolved by the fixed ordering of the two candidate assignments.
    This is a nontrivial modeling choice, and its consequences should be examined more systemati-
cally in future work. Here, we take it as a convention. The reason we prefer the maximizing option
over the minimizing will be shown later in Section 3.4. There we examine how the deformation
gradient is transported using the two selection criteria. As we will see, the maximizing criterion
more closely reproduces the fixed-connectivity benchmark than the minimizing criterion in the
example considered.

3.2    Adjusting the rotation of the canonical reference element
Consider Figure 3.9. It shows a twin element pair being sheared by an integer horizontal simple
shear S. Observe that neither element in the reference configuration aligns with the canonical
reference element. Thus, even in the reference configuration, there may be some rotational
component R in the deformation gradient. This extra rotation is reminiscent, but distinct, from
the rotation discussed in Section 2.2.1 earlier in this chapter. There, we explained that although

                                                108

===== PDF page 120 of 215 =====

3. Plastic-history transport through reconnection




                                                             Rb
                                                        Lg


                                     B
                                      G                       Rg
                                                        Lb


Figure 4.10: Choice of inheritance. On the left side, the figure shows two elements B and G that
are about to edge-flip. Overlapping nodes represent one real node, but for illustration purposes,
we have given each element its own set of nodes. Edges connecting overlapping nodes are also
only illustrative duplicates, and represent only one shared edge. After an edge flip, the resulting
elements can inherit properties from the old elements in two different ways. In the top right, the
left element Lg inherits from G, and Rb from B. The bottom-right pair of elements shows the
other alternative, where the left element Lb inherits from B and Rg from G. Arrows and dashed
circles illustrate an interpretation in which the reconnection has apparently moved the green
and blue nodes. The distance through which these nodes appear to move depends on which
inheritance option is chosen.


multiple reduction paths lead to the fundamental domain, their resulting decompositions leave
the elastic and plastic components with different rotational components as a result of redundant
basis transformations. We concluded that although an elastoplastic decomposition with such
an additional material rotation may be appropriate for some histories, the reduction path with
fewer basis changes is more likely to be representative of the deformation history. The canonical
reference element is an additional source of rotations to the deformation gradient. In this section,
we show how we remove this rotation as well.
    Respecting the square symmetry of our crystal, the canonical reference element has four
candidate orientations. After each reconnection, we choose the one that minimizes the absolute
polar-rotation angle of F. This is illustrated in Figure 4.11. There we see a deformed current
element drawn in orange, and the candidate canonical reference element in four different orien-
tations. We compute the deformation gradient F from each candidate and obtain its angle θ
from the polar decomposition F = RU. In this example, the orientation depicted in (c) has the
smallest |θ| and is selected.

3.2.1   Implementation detail: avoiding polar decomposition calculation

Instead of computing a polar decomposition, the code tests both assignments of the two cur-
rent edges to the reference edges. For each candidate orientation, it retains the assignment
that preserves orientation, then chooses the largest sum of corresponding edge dot products.
Appendix B.10 shows that this selects the same candidate as minimizing |θ|.

3.3       Reconnection-aware deformation gradient
An edge flip changes the elements used to describe the crystal without moving any node. The
mesh is not moved and the sample is not loaded, yet the deformation gradient changes. Figure 3.9
illustrates the problem. For the blue triangle, the canonical-reference gradients in the three
panels are
                       F(a) = S,     F(b) = S 2 ,    F(c) = S = F(b) S −1 ,               (4.22)

where the superscripts identify the panels and S = 10 11 . Although the nodal configuration is
                                                                                                                    

unchanged by the edge flip, the canonical-reference gradient loses one represented unit of shear.
We therefore introduce a history variable that retains the accumulated plastic part while allowing
the elastic part to remain consistent with the new element geometry.

3.3.1          Two rules for deformation transport

We write the history-corrected deformation as T = Te Tp , where Tp records the accumulated
plastic history. For an initially undeformed state, we initialize T = Tp = I. Across reconnections,
we compare the two transport rules illustrated in Figure 4.12.
    Let − denote the donor element immediately before a flip and + the inheriting element
immediately afterwards, according to the inheritance choice in Section 3.1. We compare

                                                                                      −                                    −
                                                     Rule I:                     p = Tp ,
                                                                                T+                                T+ = F+
                                                                                                                        e Tp ,                                                         (4.23)
                                                    Rule II:                    T+ = T− .                                                                                              (4.24)

Rule I combines the inherited plastic history with the elastic factor of the new element. Rule II
carries the complete incoming deformation history to the new element.

3.3.2   Preference of Rule I over Rule II

For an affine flip, the gradients satisfy F+ = F− Q with Q ∈ SL(2, Z), as shown in Section 3.3.1.
The flip therefore preserves the elastic lattice state, although the plastic reduction may select a
different square-symmetry representative. When the same elastic representative is selected on
                                −
both sides of the flip, F+e = Fe , and Rule I gives

                                           −    − −     −
                                  T+ = F+
                                        e Tp = Fe Tp = T .                                    (4.25)

Under this condition, the two transport rules agree.
     For a non-affine flip, there is a more basic obstacle to preserving the previous deformation
field. In Figure 4.13, node i of a unit square is displaced 0.2 units to the left. For this example,
consider only the green element. The green pre-flip triangle (l, i, k) is sheared, whereas the
green post-flip triangle (l, k, j) is an undistorted unit right triangle. These elements form a
donor–recipient pair under the chosen inheritance rule. With the reference orientations accounted
for, their elastic factors are
                                            1 0.2

is T = Fe . The two rules consequently give

Rule II retains the donor’s elastic shear in the transported description of an undistorted new
triangle. Rule I instead preserves the plastic history while allowing the elastic factor to reflect
the new geometry. We choose Rule I to keep this direct connection between element geometry
and the elastic state of the represented lattice. Further investigation is needed to characterize
fully the trade-offs between these two transport rules.

3.4    Comparison of element inheritance
Consider the loading protocol introduced in Figure 3.1 in Chapter 3, which nucleates dislocations
from the sides of a sample. We will reuse this protocol to explore the effects of element inheritance.
In the top row of Figure 4.14, we show the components of the deformation gradient in the reference
configuration (a), after one horizontal dislocation (b), and after two vertical dislocations (c). Each
panel shows four copies of the same mesh, colored by the four components of F. Reconnection is
disabled, and the initial mesh is chosen so that the first horizontal dislocation does not produce
topological locking.
    The top row of Figure 4.14 serves as a benchmark for what our reconnection-aware deformation
gradient T should look like. Let us now compare this with T, using either the minimizing or
maximizing transport distance discussed in Section 3.1 earlier in this chapter.
    The middle and bottom rows of Figure 4.14 use the maximizing and minimizing flip-distance
criteria, respectively. In this benchmark, the minimizing rule keeps inherited values in more com-
pact bands, whereas the maximizing rule distributes them more broadly. The cohesion/repulsion
interpretation is a hypothesis based on this one loading protocol. We use the maximizing rule
because it most closely reproduces the fixed-connectivity benchmark in this test.

3.5    Loss of spatial detail under reconnection
Figure 4.15 shows two pre-flip elements with different constant gradients. The new element
(i, j, k), outlined in black, overlaps parts of both and must represent these two local deformation
states with a single gradient. A complete discussion of this phenomenon is outside the scope of
this thesis, but we mention two ideas.
     Instead of one-to-one inheritance, the new deformation gradient could be an area-weighted
average of the two old gradients, with weights given by the overlap area between each old element
and the new element. In Figure 4.15, the green gradient would receive greater weight than the
blue gradient when constructing the gradient in element ijk.
     A second possibility is to use higher-order elements and shape functions. The current linear
elements carry a constant deformation gradient; higher-order interpolation could represent some
of the spatial variation lost when a new element spans regions previously represented by two
elements.

4     Diagnostics in reconnected simulations
The elastoplastic decomposition, inheritance rule, and reconnection-aware deformation gradient
T enable analyses that were unavailable without history transport. This section presents several
such diagnostics and outlines directions for further study.

4.1   Exploration of the strain-energy landscape
The Poincaré disk provides a useful representation of the strain states explored by a mesh [14,
16, 127, 128, 134, 136]. The reconnection-aware deformation gradient T now allows this analysis
to retain the accumulated plastic history when the connectivity changes. We compare the
distributions across lattice-equivalent wells, then use plastic reduction to compare the elastic
distortions within a common well.
    The top row of Figure 4.16 shows the final states of ten 100 × 100 simulations for each
connectivity protocol, loaded in positive horizontal simple shear to γ ≃ 1. The reduced comparison
and the reconnected statistics in Table 4.1 use the nine-run subset excluding seed 5. Each disk
is divided into a 300 × 300 grid of bins, colored by the number of elements in each bin. The
reconnected samples explore the disk more broadly, whereas the fixed-connectivity samples leave
substantial regions sparsely populated, especially in its lower half. Both distributions favor the
upper-left quadrant, consistent with the direction traced by positive horizontal simple shear in
the disk. The detailed origin of the regions avoided by the fixed-connectivity samples remains to
be explained. Quantitatively, we can illustrate the preference by counting how many elements end
up in each quadrant of the disk. We treat each sample separately, and report mean distributions
and standard deviations over the samples in Table 4.1.
    The distributions also differ within the occupied wells. High-density regions identify the
well centers, but the paths between neighboring wells are wider and more densely populated
without reconnection. With reconnection, elements are more concentrated around the local
minima. To compare this elastic distortion directly, the bottom row of Figure 4.16 maps the
states into the fundamental elastic well De using Ce = FT   e Fe . The reduced comparison contains

    The reconnected distribution in Figure 4.16d is more concentrated near the origin, in the
lower-energy part of the deviatoric landscape. It also has a cross-shaped region of low population
density through the origin. The fixed-connectivity distribution in Figure 4.16c extends further
into higher-energy regions and lacks the cross structure.
    A preference for the left side of the well also persists after reduction in the fixed-connectivity
samples. The fraction of elements on the left (xP < 0) is (63.9 ± 1.8)% without reconnection,
compared with (51.1 ± 14.6)% with reconnection. These values give the mean and standard
deviation across the ten fixed-connectivity and nine reconnected samples, respectively. All ten
fixed-connectivity samples have more elements on the left than on the right. The reconnected
samples are roughly balanced on average, but their left–right distributions vary substantially
between runs. This suggests that with reconnection, there is little preference for which quadrant
of a local energy well an element sits in.

4.3     Yield surface and plastic events in reduced state space
In classical rate-independent plasticity, the yield surface bounds the elastic domain in stress space.
Plastic flow can occur only on the current yield surface, where the stress remains during continued
plastic loading. The surface may evolve with plastic deformation, while elastic unloading can
move the stress into its interior [160].
    The MTM has no explicit need to define a yield surface, since plastic flow does not need to be
activated; it occurs naturally due to its non-convex periodic energy landscape. That landscape
also gives rise to a yield-like boundary for a single element, where its current elastic response first
loses strong ellipticity. Intuitively, the energy becomes flat to second order along a particular
deformation direction δF. If an applied load holds the element in equilibrium at this boundary, a
perturbation in that direction has no quadratic restoring stiffness and may cause the element to
leave its current well for a neighboring one. More precisely, at the boundary there exist a nonzero
incremental-displacement vector p in the current configuration and a unit reference direction n(0)
along which that displacement varies. A small deformation-gradient step δF = ε p ⊗ n(0) then
has zero second variation of the energy. For this particular n(0) , p is a zero-eigenvalue eigenvector
of the material acoustic tensor; see Appendix B.6 and [14, 128, 134]. We have illustrated this
loss of strong ellipticity boundary in the Poincaré disk in Figure 4.18.

4.3.1    Density distribution of pre-event elastic states

Reconnection permits sustained plastic flow with an approximately stationary energy baseline
(see Section 4.5.2 in Chapter 3). Sampling after the initial yield transition excludes its transient
response and focuses the distribution on elastic states maintained during this approximately


                                                 118

===== PDF page 130 of 215 =====

4. Diagnostics in reconnected simulations


steady flow. With plastic-history transport, we can compare pre-event elastic states, and later
visualize the event dynamics in strain space.
    We selected 110 irreversible events from a 200 × 200 sample over the loading interval γ ∈ [1, 2].
Figure 4.19 shows the resulting counts per bin in two representations of these same observations.
In panel (a), relaxed meshes just before each event are mapped into the fundamental elastic
well by using only the elastic component Ce = FT   e Fe of each element. A 200 × 200 pixel grid is
placed over the region of the Poincaré disk shown, and the color represents how many elements
were mapped to each pixel. Since the metric is invariant to rotations, a spatial rotation changes
neither Ce nor the eigenvalues that determine loss of strong ellipticity. Elements that differ only
by this rotation therefore have the same reduced disk coordinates and can be compared with the
same loss-of-strong-ellipticity boundary (LEB).
    Panel (b) instead shows the laboratory-frame Cauchy-stress components, with (σ22 − σ11 )/2
horizontally and σ12 vertically. The dashed black curve is the stress image of the unit-area LEB,
                                                                  √
calculated using the rotation-free representative Fe = Ue = Ce . It therefore applies to an
element whose elastic polar rotation is zero relative to the laboratory axes. For an element with
rotation Re , the corresponding Cauchy-stress tensor is rotated by Re ; its boundary appears at a
different orientation in this deviatoric stress plane, rotated through twice the elastic rotation
angle. Unlike the disk plot, one drawn curve in panel (b) is therefore not a common LEB for
all the plotted elements. A point outside the LEB drawn in (b) need not lie outside its own
orientation-dependent LEB boundary. This rotational dependence explains why the plot has
circular features, despite the LEB being drawn as a horizontally elongated ellipse-like boundary.

4.3.2   Change in elastic-state density across plastic events

So far, we have examined pre-event states. The post-event distributions look similar when plotted
separately (not shown), so we examine the changes in element counts in Figure 4.20. Both maps
show net depletion farther from the origin and a net increase closer to it. The depleted region lies
mainly above the x-axis and, in the Poincaré disk, is stronger on the left. This redistribution is
consistent with relaxation opposing the imposed positive horizontal shear. The count differences
describe net redistribution; matched element transitions are examined in the following subsection.

4.3.3   Directional flow of strain during plastic events

We now take the analysis one step further by considering the directional dynamics during
                    (−)        (+)
the events. Let Tn and Tn denote the deformation matrices before and after the event,
respectively. Since several events are pooled, each index n identifies an element–event pair, and
the event refers to its associated event. This avoids introducing a separate event index.
    To investigate strain motion relative to the fundamental elastic well, we consider the displace-
ment vector vn connecting the before- and after-event positions in Poincaré disk coordinates.
Independently reducing the two states to obtain their elastic components can introduce a large
apparent displacement when an element changes wells: nearby strain states can then be mapped
to opposite sides of the fundamental elastic well. To avoid this, we express both states using the

purple density. Black low-density regions separate the central column from the offshoots, with
intermittent white pixels indicating a complete absence of elements. (b) The same pre-event
states are shown in laboratory-frame Cauchy-stress coordinates. The LEB is calculated for unit
area and zero elastic polar rotation, so it is an orientation-specific reference. The plot shows
a high density zone slightly above the origin, and an orange ring centered at the origin with a
radius equal to the shortest distance between the origin and the LEB.

deviation across the plotted bins is 29◦ , increasing to 41◦ when restricted to bins inside the LEB.
These substantial dispersions indicate that the predominantly radial maximal-flow axes describe
a collective tendency, while individual element displacements can deviate considerably from these
axes.

    Figure 4.21(b) shows the average projected magnitude. It is small near the center but
otherwise featureless. Figure 4.21(d) shows the magnitude dispersion. It too has few identifiable
features.

4.3.4   Perspective toward a mean-field model

These results come from a single 200 × 200 sample and serve as a proof of concept rather than
an ensemble characterization of plastic avalanches. The reduced-domain maps suggest a possible
mean-field description in which separate loading and relaxation kernels evolve a continuous strain
distribution. Establishing such a model would require larger ensembles, an assessment of how
the kernels depend on event size, and validation against avalanche statistics.

4. Diagnostics in reconnected simulations


Conclusion
In this chapter, we addressed the loss of deformation-history information introduced by mesh
reconnection. Chapter 3 showed that a canonical reference triangle avoids degenerate reference
configurations after an edge flip, but this choice also changes the representative deformation
gradient. The present chapter therefore constructed a deformation-gradient-like quantity that
keeps the plastic history associated with reconnection.
    The construction relies on an integer-valued multiplicative decomposition F = Fe Fp . The
plastic-reduction algorithm reduces C = FT F to the fundamental square-lattice domain by
composing elementary basis changes. The inverse of the accumulated transformation gives the
plastic deformation, while the reduced factor describes the elastic state. This separates the role
of lattice-basis changes from the elastic distortion carried by the current element.
    We then used this decomposition to define history transport through edge flips. The donor’s
accumulated plastic quantity Tp is inherited unchanged across a flip. With compatible elastic
representatives, T is also invariant under affine flips; it is allowed to change when a non-affine
flip produces a different elastic state. This avoids carrying obsolete elastic distortion through
non-affine reconnections, as would occur if the full deformation were transported instead. The
chapter also defined the element-inheritance rule and the rotation choice for the canonical
reference element.
    The final sections used these variables to examine plastic events. We distinguished changes in
plastic deformation from mechanical yield criteria and compared pre-event elastic-state densities
in the reduced Poincaré representation and in laboratory-frame stress space, measured the
corresponding pre/post count changes, and analyzed matched displacement directions across
events. The observed flow has a clear radial component, while several features near the center
and edges of the well remain open questions rather than firm identifications.
    With reconnection and plastic-history transport in place, the model can now be used to study
collective plastic fluctuations without losing the accumulated plastic history. Chapter 5 uses this
framework to measure energy and stress drops, extract avalanche statistics and test how these
statistics depend on numerical choices such as minimization and reconnection.

    This chapter focuses on plastic avalanches in athermal quasistatic simulations and on the
extraction of reliable energy-drop statistics from the raw numerical data. We use the simulation
protocol described in Section 5 of Chapter 2. The systems are prepared from randomized
prestrained samples, loaded by affine strain increments under periodic boundary conditions, and
relaxed to mechanical equilibrium after each increment. The avalanche observables correspond
to sudden drops of the total energy along this quasistatic loading path. The first section defines
these observables and explains how events are classified.
    A substantial part of the chapter is devoted to the processing of these energy drops. We
use a loading–unloading protocol to separate reversible drops from irreversible events before
selecting the data used for the statistical analysis. The measured energy drops are corrected
using a second-order stress approximation, and the validity of this correction is tested.


                                                     126

===== PDF page 138 of 215 =====

1. Avalanche observables and event classification


    The avalanche distributions are heavy-tailed and exhibit approximate truncated-power-law
regimes. We fit these regimes using the procedure introduced by Clauset et al. [68]: a lower cutoff
is selected, the tail parameters are fitted by maximum likelihood, and the Kolmogorov–Smirnov
distance and bootstrap p-values assess statistical plausibility. The fitted parameters measure the
sensitivity of the observed statistics to changes in the numerical protocol.
    The chapter ends by applying this analysis to two numerical choices that are central to MTM
simulations. We first study the dependence of the avalanche statistics on the minimization algo-
rithm. The final section then investigates the sensitivity of the observed avalanche distributions
to mesh reconnection.


1     Avalanche observables and event classification
1.1   Avalanche detection and definition
Dislocation avalanches produce sudden drops in energy and stress. These drops are visible in
Figure 5.1. The total energy of the sample E is shown in blue. The inset shows a zoom of the
energy in the region γ = 0.8 to 0.81.
    Several points must be considered when calculating the energy drops ∆E shown in orange.
Figure 5.2 illustrates three definitions. Each full black marker represents an equilibrium state
at strain γn . In the limit ∆γ → 0, the energy follows the preceding elastic branch until the
instability strain γ̌n . The energy difference between that pre-instability state and the subsequent
equilibrium state defines the limiting drop ∆ET .
    Since we are limited to finite strain steps, we need to approximate ∆ET , and that is where
the three different approaches can be introduced. The most direct estimate is the difference
between the energies of two successive equilibrium configurations, a commonly used measure of
energy drops in quasistatic simulations [64, 135, 161], even though it underestimates the true
energy drop. We refer to this as the inter-strain energy drop ∆EI = En−1 − En .
    Alternatively, we can measure the energy decrease from the affinely loaded configuration to
the final relaxed equilibrium, denoted by ∆ER = Enaff − En . This measure includes not only the
energy released by an avalanche, but also the non-affine elastic relaxation required to restore
equilibrium after affine loading. It therefore contains an elastic contribution even in steps that
do not trigger an avalanche. This measure is used both to measure power-law statistics [15] and
to separate plastic and elastic events [162, 163].
    A third approach estimates the energy that would have been reached by elastic loading
before subtracting the measured final energy. This accounts for the loading work omitted by
∆EI , while avoiding the use of the affinely loaded energy as the baseline in ∆ER . The resulting
stress-corrected energy drop is denoted by ∆ES . Stress-based estimates of this kind have been
used in amorphous solids [163, 164].
    The elastic-energy increment is estimated by expanding the energy in the strain increment
∆γ. At first order, the correction uses the shear stress before loading to estimate the work done
during the step. Retaining the second-order term accounts for the variation of stress along the
chosen elastic loading path and requires the corresponding tangent shear modulus. Here, we

                                                127

use a second-order estimate along the affine loading path. This improves the estimate of the
affinely loaded energy, but does not remove the contribution from subsequent non-affine elastic
relaxation. For the loading increments used in our avalanche analysis, we find little difference
between the drops obtained with the first- and second-order estimates.
    The following discussion focuses on energy drops. An analogous construction can be used to
estimate stress drops by extrapolating the elastic stress response before comparing it with the
stress after relaxation.

1.2   Elastic-energy increment
To assess the accuracy of the elastic-energy estimate, we first consider a pristine crystal subjected
to purely affine simple shear. The deformation is homogeneous, so its elastic response can be
examined using a single element. We restrict the test to the elastic branch before the loss of
ellipticity. The energy increment estimation is therefore not affected by avalanches and non-affine
elastic relaxation.
     The second-order Taylor estimate of the element energy after a shear increment ∆γ is

Figure 5.2: Schematic illustration of the different energy drops measured between two successive
strain steps under quasistatic loading. The quantity ∆EI measures the energy difference between
two consecutive equilibrium states. The quantity ∆ET denotes the theoretical drop that would
be obtained from ∆EI in the infinitesimal-strain-step limit, ∆γ → 0. The relaxation drop
∆ER measures the energy decrease from the affinely loaded state to the relaxed equilibrium
state, and therefore includes the non-affine elastic relaxation. The corrected drop ∆ES uses the
pre-increment shear stress σ12 and a tangent-stiffness estimate to extrapolate the affine loading
energy. It corrects the loading work omitted from ∆EI , but does not remove the subsequent
non-affine elastic relaxation.

second-order term accounts for the change in stress during the increment, through the tangent

as a constant reference-state modulus. The estimate therefore incorporates the local nonlinear
elastic response, although it remains truncated at second order in ∆γ.

resolving elastic energy changes. Note, however, that the single-element test does not assess
additional errors associated with non-affine relaxation in a heterogeneous sample.

1.3   Elasticity, plasticity, and the reversibility protocol
Collective plastic rearrangements can produce avalanches spanning a broad range of sizes, whose
distribution may exhibit a power-law regime [55, 56]. We investigate this behavior in the MTM
through the statistics of the stress-corrected energy drops ∆ES . Section 1.4 explains why ∆ES
is chosen as the avalanche-size measure rather than ∆EI or ∆ER .
    In the following sections, we extract a power-law exponent from the avalanche statistics. For
a candidate lower cutoff ∆ES,min , energy drops smaller than this value are excluded from the fit,
while drops at or above it are used to estimate the exponent. We denote the cutoff selected by
minimizing the Kolmogorov–Smirnov distance by ∆ES,min     KS , as described in Section 2.2.

    This cutoff raises an important question. Is ∆ES,min  KS   only a statistical fitting parameter,
or does it separate two physically distinct classes of events? Consider the probability density
function shown in Figure 5.4. There is a pronounced change near ∆ES /V0 = 10−9 . What
distinguishes the energy drops retained in the power-law regime from the smaller drops that
are discarded? The purpose of the following analysis is to clarify this point by comparing the
energy-drop magnitude with the reversibility of the corresponding event.
    We use irreversibility as an operational criterion for plasticity: reversible steps are classified
as non-plastic and irreversible steps as plastic. This is not a general thermodynamic equivalence,
because reversible anelastic mechanisms may exist. Reversibility is tested using the protocol in
Figure 5.5.
    The protocol consists of four steps. Starting from a relaxed configuration (state 0), an affine
forward loading increment is first applied, bringing the system to state 1. This loading step is


                                                    131

===== PDF page 143 of 215 =====

Chapter 5. Plastic avalanches


followed by a static relaxation, leading to state 2. The system is then sheared backward by the
opposite affine increment, giving state 3, and relaxed again to state 4. States 0 and 4 therefore
have the same macroscopic strain. For a reversible cycle, one expects state 4 to coincide with
state 0, up to the numerical relaxation tolerance. In contrast, an irreversible cycle leaves a finite
difference between these two states.
    We quantify the displacement difference between states 0 and 4, after removing the mean
nodal translation, by the scalar closure measure
                                                                            #1/2
                            1 X  (p,4)
                            "
                                                                2
                     drev =      u      − ū(4) − u(p,0) − ū(0)                   ,            (5.5)
                            N p

where N counts the independent nodes, p labels a node, the second superscript label identifies
the cycle state, and barred variables denote nodal averages:

                                      1 X (p,0)               1 X (p,4)
                            ū(0) =       u     ,   ū(4) =       u     .                       (5.6)
                                      N p                     N p

The displacements must use a common periodic-image convention before the mean translation is
removed; wrapped coordinates compared independently would produce artificial closure errors.
The stored nodal displacement vectors are compared directly in the implementation.
    For each parameter set, we apply Otsu’s method to log10 drev to obtain a threshold d∗rev . Events
with drev ≤ d∗rev are classified as reversible; the remaining events are classified as irreversible.
The threshold is estimated from the pooled observations for that parameter set, rather than
imposed as a universal displacement scale.
    Using this protocol and the scalar closure measure drev , we separate energy drops into two
populations, as illustrated in Figure 5.6. The figure further shows that ∆ER separates the
populations without requiring the closure measure. We therefore use it as a proxy in simulations
without a reversal cycle; its motivation is given in Section 1.4.
    Representative spatial fields for the two classes are shown in Figures 5.7 and 5.8. The
reversible example in Figure 5.7 shows small local energy variations distributed throughout the
sample during forward relaxation, as seen in panel (a). The enlarged view in panel (b) reveals
dipolar patterns around dislocation cores, consistent with non-affine elastic relaxation of the
heterogeneous configuration. During backward relaxation, these energy variations are reversed:
panels (c,d) show approximately the opposite pattern to panels (a,b). The loading–unloading
cycle therefore restores the initial configuration within numerical tolerance, as confirmed by the
small residual displacement in panel (f). Reversibility does not, however, require every element
to remain in the same elastic well.
    The irreversible example in Figure 5.8 has a different spatial structure. The forward energy
variation in panel (a) is localized, rather than distributed throughout the sample. Within the
enlarged region in panel (b), the elements outlined in black change elastic wells, identifying
the local rearrangements associated with the avalanche. The backward relaxation in panels

1. Avalanche observables and event classification


    Without reusing information from the forward relaxation, the reversibility protocol requires an
additional backward loading and relaxation step and therefore nearly doubles the computational
cost. It enables clean separation of the reversible and irreversible population, but we also see that
with our loading parameters, a similar result can be achieved using ∆ER . In the next section,
we share the energy-based explanation developed in Ref. [166].

1.4    Classification procedure
As Figure 5.6 demonstrates, the closure measure drev provides a direct way of separating reversible
and irreversible events. This is not identical to the distinction between elastic and plastic. Some
authors distinguish anelasticity from both elasticity and plasticity, defining it as deformation that
is mechanically reversible but dissipative [167]. In this thesis, however, we adopt an operational
definition and use irreversibility as the defining criterion for plasticity. Reversible events are
therefore classified as non-plastic, regardless of whether they involve anelastic mechanisms.
    While the reversibility protocol is a powerful tool for analysis, it is computationally costly
because it requires roughly twice as much computation. Most simulations are therefore run
without it. Instead, we rely on a less direct criterion to separate reversible from irreversible events:
∆ER . This energy-based criterion, introduced in Ref. [166] and further developed in Ref. [162],
can be presented as follows: let En−1 denote the energy of the equilibrium configuration before
the strain increment, Enaff the energy immediately after affine loading, and En the energy after
minimization. As introduced in the beginning of the chapter, in Section 1.1, the relaxation-energy
drop is

                                         ∆ER = Enaff − En .                                       (5.7)

This measure has a particular scaling when it remains on a smooth elastic branch. For such a
smooth step, the energies before and after relaxation can be expanded as


                                                           V 0 µB
                           Enaff = En−1 + V0 ⟨σ12 ⟩n−1 ∆γ +       (∆γ)2 ,                         (5.8)
                                                              2
                                                           V0 µ
                             En = En−1 + V0 ⟨σ12 ⟩n−1 ∆γ +      (∆γ)2 ,                           (5.9)
                                                            2

up to higher-order terms. Here µB is the affine, or Born, shear modulus, whereas µ is the relaxed
shear modulus, which includes the reduction in stiffness produced by non-affine relaxation.
Subtracting Equations (5.8) and (5.9) eliminates both the initial energy and the work term
proportional to the initial stress:

                                              V0
                                     ∆ER =       (µB − µ)(∆γ)2 .                                 (5.10)
                                              2
    Following [166], we therefore define

                                                 ∆ER
                                           κ=             ,                                      (5.11)
                                                Nel (∆γ)2


                                                  137

===== PDF page 149 of 215 =====

Chapter 5. Plastic avalanches


    where Nel is the reference element count used to normalize the energy-drop analysis. It
is distinct from the node count N in the closure measure. Introducing the element density
ρ = Nel /V0 , the elastic prediction becomes

                                                µB − µ
                                           κ=          .                                      (5.12)
                                                  2ρ
   Provided that the relaxed modulus is nonnegative, this also gives the elastic upper bound

                                                      µB
                                           κ ≤ κe =      .                                    (5.13)
                                                      2ρ
    This derivation should be understood as an elastic reference response. If the strain increment
triggers a rearrangement that is not described by the smooth continuation of the initial equilibrium
branch, the expansion for En no longer accounts for the complete relaxation. The resulting ∆ER ,
and hence κ, can then be much larger than the elastic background. Thus, the criterion does not
classify an event from the absolute size of its energy drop; it tests whether the affine-to-relaxed
energy loss has the quadratic scaling expected for an ordinary elastic increment.
    This also explains why ∆ER and ∆ES serve different purposes in the following analysis. The
stress-corrected quantity ∆ES is constructed to estimate the energy released by the event and
is therefore used as the avalanche-size observable. In contrast, ∆ER measures the mismatch
between affine loading and the final relaxed configuration. In our simulations, this mismatch
separates the two reversibility populations more clearly than the magnitude of ∆ES , even though
reversible and irreversible events may have overlapping values of ∆ES .
    The derivation alone does not prove that a large value of κ implies irreversibility, particularly
because the MTM permits reversible transitions between elastic wells. We therefore treat κ
as an operational proxy whose association with reversibility is established using the subset of
simulations for which the reversal protocol was performed.
    Figure 5.9 demonstrates the operational classifier. We approximate the event-dependent Born
modulus by the reference spatial tangent a1212 (0), defined in Appendix B.6, giving the fixed
threshold
                                             a1212 (0)
                                       e =
                                      κ(0)             = 1.52.                                 (5.14)
                                                2ρ
Here Nel = 2L2 and V0 = L2 , so ρ = Nel /V0 = 2 for the periodic reference grids used in this
analysis, and the numerical value uses the simulation energy and length units. This reference
approximation is distinct from the event-dependent elastic bound in Eq. (5.13). Its association
with reversibility is assessed empirically, rather than guaranteed by that bound. The threshold
separates the two populations in Figure 5.9a, although their ∆ES distributions in Figure 5.9b
overlap substantially.

1.5         Selection and validation of numerical parameters
Most simulations were performed using a strain increment ∆γ = 10−5 and a relaxation criterion
ϵx = 10−6 .1 These values are small enough to give a good separation of reversible and irreversible
events, yet not so small that computation times become unmanageable.
    Figure 5.10 shows the reversibility/irreversibility split for several simulation parameters. In
Figure 5.10a, we see that when the load increment ∆γ is large (10−4 ), ∆ER no longer separates
reversible and irreversible steps.
    Likewise, Figure 5.10b shows that as ϵx increases, ∆ER is unable to separate the reversible
and irreversible populations.
    We see that ϵx mostly influences the drev scale of the reversible population while ∆γ influences
the ∆ER level of the reversible population. This is expected, as ϵx determines the simulation
stopping criterion in terms of displacement. The lower ϵx is, the closer the mesh will be to the
true local minimum. Since both S (0) and S (4) approach the same local minimum, the two states
converge, and drev decreases. The increase of the reversible relaxation-energy drop with ∆γ
can be understood from the mismatch between affine loading and mechanical equilibrium. In a
heterogeneous configuration containing dislocations, uniform shear generally produces unbalanced
nodal forces. Additional non-affine displacements are therefore required to restore equilibrium,
even when the system remains on the same elastic branch. For sufficiently small increments, these
displacements are linear in ∆γ, while the energy excess above the relaxed minimum is quadratic
in displacement [166]. Consequently, Equation (5.10) predicts ∆ER /V0 ≃ (µB − µ)(∆γ)2 /2.
   1
    As defined in Section 4.4 of Chapter 2, ϵx is the threshold on the Euclidean norm of the accepted minimization
displacement step, ∥∆u∥2 .

Figure 5.10: Energy-drop ∆ER /V0 as a function of the reversibility displacement closure drev , for
different choices of (a) the strain increment ∆γ and (b) the relaxation displacement threshold
ϵx . In panel (a), ϵx = 10−6 is held fixed while ∆γ is varied. In panel (b), ∆γ = 10−5 is held
fixed while ϵx is varied. For each parameter set, four 100 × 100 samples are used. The event
populations are separated by Otsu thresholds applied to log10 drev [165], shown as vertical dashed
lines. In panel (b), small isolated populations at anomalously small closure and energy drop
for ϵx = 10−4 and 10−5 are marked by crosses and excluded from the population split. Their
numerical origin and the sensitivity of the classification to this exclusion remain to be established.
For sufficiently small relaxation thresholds, ϵx < 10−5 , a clear gap separates reversible from
irreversible events.


Increasing the strain increment thus raises the reversible elastic background in ∆ER , helping to
explain the poorer separation from small irreversible events in Figure 5.10a. A larger relaxation
drop need not indicate a larger irreversible event; it can also reflect a larger mismatch between
the affinely loaded configuration and the relaxed equilibrium.


2              Statistical analysis of avalanche distributions
Statistical analysis of heavy-tailed distributions, such as power laws, can be challenging. Several
studies provide guidance on how best to analyze and present such data [68, 168, 169]. The
inferred exponent can be sensitive to post-processing even when it is expected to be insensitive
to microscopic details [71]. For example, the acoustic-energy exponent for ice was initially
estimated as 1.5–1.6 using least-squares fitting, whereas a later maximum-likelihood analysis
gave 1.40 ± 0.03 [39, 55].
    Power laws are informative because they may reveal scale-free collective mechanisms in the
mesoscopic regime of crystal plasticity [37].

2.1                Power-law fitting and cutoff effects
A power-law distribution of the form p(∆ES ) ∝ ∆ES−α with exponent α > 1 assigns increasingly
large probability density to small events as ∆ES → 0. It is therefore not normalizable unless
a lower cutoff ∆ES,min > 0 is specified. At the same time, compared with rapidly decaying

                                                                         140

===== PDF page 152 of 215 =====

2. Statistical analysis of avalanche distributions


distributions such as a Gaussian, a power law gives much larger weight to rare, large events.
When fitting a power law to physical data, one must therefore define the range over which the
power-law description is assumed to hold. In practice, this requires choosing at least a lower cutoff
∆ES,min and, when relevant, accounting for an upper cutoff ∆ES,max or a smooth truncation of
the tail.
    This choice is not always straightforward. Real data rarely display a sharp transition between
the non-universal small-event regime and the scaling regime. As a result, changing the fitting
range can lead to continuously varying estimates of the exponent. In this thesis, we follow the
statistical procedure proposed by Clauset et al. [68] to select the lower cutoff and estimate the
power-law parameters.
    Here, we fit a truncation parameter λ to describe the exponential decay of the tail, rather
than imposing a sharp upper cutoff ∆ES,max . We thus use the following truncated power-law
model:

                                                1
                                   p(∆ES ) =      ∆ES−α e−λ∆ES ,                               (5.15)
                                                Z
where Z is a normalization constant
                           Z ∞
                      Z=              x−α e−λx dx = λα−1 Γ(1 − α, λ∆ES,min ) ,                 (5.16)
                            ∆ES,min


using Γ(a, z) as the upper incomplete gamma function. For a pure tail on [∆ES,min , ∞), normal-
ization at infinity requires α > 1. In the exponentially truncated model, ∆ES,min > 0 and λ > 0
make the density normalizable for any finite α, including the values below one obtained in some
of the fits here.

2.2   Fitting procedure
To determine the exponent α, the exponential cutoff λ, and the selected lower cutoff ∆ES,min
                                                                                       KS ,

we scan candidate thresholds ∆ES,min using the following procedure:

   1. During the AQS loading protocol, the relaxation energy drop ∆ER is recorded after each
      affine strain increment.

   2. Each event is classified using the normalized relaxation drop

                                                    ∆ER
                                              κ=             .
                                                   Nel (∆γ)2

      The threshold
                                                       a1212 (0)
                                               e =
                                              κ(0)
                                                          2ρ
                                                                                 (0)
      is used to separate reversible and irreversible events. Events with κ < κe are classified as
                                           (0)
      reversible, while events with κ ≥ κe are classified as irreversible. The analytical bound is
      closed; this empirical classifier assigns exact threshold ties to the irreversible class as its
      operational boundary convention.

                                                 141

===== PDF page 153 of 215 =====

Chapter 5. Plastic avalanches


  3. The stress-corrected energy drop ∆ES is computed from the energy–strain signal using the
     second-order estimate introduced in Eq. (5.4).

  4. This classification separates the probability distribution of ∆ES into reversible and ir-
     reversible contributions. Only positive irreversible values of ∆ES are retained for the
     avalanche-statistics analysis.

  5. For each candidate lower cutoff ∆ES,min , the events satisfying

                                           ∆ES ≥ ∆ES,min

     are fitted with a truncated power-law distribution

                       p(∆ES ) = C∆ES−α̂ exp(−λ̂∆ES ),         ∆ES ≥ ∆ES,min ,

     where C is the normalization constant and the parameters α̂ and λ̂ are fitted by maximum
     likelihood, as described in Section 2.2.1.

  6. The empirical complementary cumulative distribution function is then compared with the
     fitted model. For each candidate cutoff, we compute the Kolmogorov–Smirnov distance D
     between the empirical distribution and the fitted distribution. See Section 2.2.2 for details.

  7. The selected cutoff ∆ES,min
                             KS  is the candidate value that minimizes the Kolmogorov–Smirnov
     distance over all tested candidates.

  8. A final maximum-likelihood fit is performed using the selected cutoff ∆ES,min
                                                                             KS , yielding α̂

     and λ̂.

  9. The quality of the fit is assessed by bootstrap resampling; see Section 2.3 for details.
     The resulting p-value is used to test whether the fitted truncated power-law model is a
     statistically plausible description of the retained avalanche data.

    We begin with raw data from a simulation. As discussed in Section 1.1, we correct the values
of ∆EI using a second-order approximation for the energy. This gives ∆ES , which is the set of
values used for the truncated power-law analysis.
    Before fitting ∆ES , we first separate reversible and irreversible events. For this purpose,
we use the relaxation-energy drop ∆ER to calculate κ. Figure 5.9a shows the PDF of κ for
                                                 (0)
ten 250 × 250 samples. We use the threshold κe = a1212 (0)/(2ρ) to distinguish reversible and
irreversible events.
    Figure 5.9b shows the corresponding PDFs of ∆ES where the events have now been split into
reversible and irreversible. Not all positive ∆ER events are positive when measured as ∆ES , and
fewer still are irreversible. From 270 000 ∆ER > 0 events, we have 85 000 ∆ES > 0. Removing
reversible events leaves only 4500 irreversible ∆ES > 0 events.
    For a candidate cutoff, let xi = ∆ES,i be the ith positive irreversible drop. The retained
indexed sample is
                         Xmin = {xi }i∈Imin ,   Imin = {i : xi ≥ ∆ES,min }.               (5.17)

Repeated values are retained through their distinct indices.

Rather than multiplying the likelihood of every event, which quickly produces extremely small
numbers, we maximize the log-likelihood,

                              ℓ(α, λ) =            ln p(xi | α, λ, ∆ES,min ),                       (5.18)
                                           X

                                          i∈Imin

                                  (α̂, λ̂) = arg max ℓ(α, λ).                     (5.19)

2.3                p-value test
Following Clauset et al. [68], we assess goodness of fit by comparing the observed KS distance
with distances from synthetic datasets generated under the fitted model. The bootstrap p-value
estimates the probability of obtaining a KS distance at least as large as the observed one under
this procedure.
    The p-value is calculated using a semiparametric2 bootstrap following Clauset et al. [68],
adapted here to a truncated power-law tail. We first fit the observed data above the selected
cutoff ∆ES,min
           KS , obtaining (α̂, λ̂) and the KS distance D
                                                         obs = D(∆ES,min ). We then generate Np
                                                                      KS

synthetic datasets, each containing the same number of observations as the full set of positive
irreversible energy drops. For each observation, with probability equal to the observed fraction
of drops above the original cutoff, we draw a new value from p(x | α̂, λ̂, ∆ES,min
                                                                               KS ); otherwise, we

sample with replacement from the empirical subcutoff observations, assigning equal probability
to each indexed observation. Thus, the distribution below the cutoff is represented empirically,
while the tail follows the fitted model. For each synthetic dataset, we reselect the lower cutoff by
        2
     A nonparametric bootstrap resamples observed values with replacement; a parametric bootstrap draws values
from a fitted model. A semiparametric bootstrap combines the two approaches. We sample model values in the
                                                      KS
tail, and observed values below the selected cutoff ∆ES,min .



                                                                           144

===== PDF page 156 of 215 =====

2. Statistical analysis of avalanche distributions


minimizing the KS distance over that replicate’s distinct observed values, subject to the same
minimum tail size nmin , refit (α̂s , λ̂s ), and calculate the corresponding KS distance Ds . The
reported bootstrap uncertainties on the fitted parameters are the standard deviations of their
estimates across these synthetic datasets. The p-value is defined as the fraction of synthetic
datasets with a KS distance at least as large as Dobs :

                                               Np
                                            1 X
                                  pboot =          1(Ds ≥ Dobs ),                            (5.24)
                                            Np s=1

where 1 is one when its argument is true and zero otherwise. Following the conventional threshold
used by Clauset et al. [68], pboot ≤ 0.1 is evidence against the fitted model. A larger value means
that this test does not reject the model; it does not establish that the model is true.
    This event-wise bootstrap treats pooled drops as independent observations. Serial correlations
within a loading trajectory and variability between independent samples are therefore absent from
its uncertainty estimate. The reported parameter spreads are conditional on that assumption; a
sample-level or block bootstrap is needed to assess its effect.
    The number of synthetic samples controls the statistical uncertainty of the bootstrap p-value.
Since the p-value is estimated as a fraction, its standard error is bounded by

                                                  1
                                            σp ≤ p .
                                                2 Np

Thus, to ensure the bound σp ≤ ε, it is sufficient to use

                                                  1
                                                       
                                            Np ≥
                                                 4ε2

synthetic samples [68]. For example, ε = 0.01 gives Np ≥ 2500.
    Figure 5.12b shows diagnostic bootstrap p-values over a cutoff scan using Np = 100 synthetic
datasets per cutoff. The selected-cutoff test also uses 100 replicates, giving a worst-case Monte
Carlo standard-error bound of 0.05. The diagnostic scan tests fixed candidate cutoffs; the final
selected-cutoff test must repeat cutoff selection in every replicate as described above.
    The slight increase of the p-value at large ∆ES,min should not be interpreted as strong evidence
that the model becomes more physically accurate in this range. When ∆ES,min is increased, fewer
events are retained in the fit. The empirical distribution is then noisier, the Kolmogorov–Smirnov
test has less statistical power, and the two parameters of the truncated power law, α and λ,
have more freedom to adapt to the remaining tail. As a result, deviations from the fitted curve
are harder to detect. The apparent improvement of the p-value at large ∆ES,min therefore
mainly reflects the loss of statistical constraints, rather than a clear improvement of the scaling
description.
    The low p-values reject the fitted single truncated-power-law model as a statistically plausible
generator under the reported bootstrap procedure. They do not, by themselves, rule out critical
dynamics. Possible explanations include corrections to scaling, mixtures of event populations,
residual dependence, finite-size effects, and a misspecified tail model. Distinguishing these

                         Figure 5.13 gives a summary of the obtained results, and demonstrates the significance of
                     using ∆ES over ∆EI and ∆ER . Notice that when using ∆EI , which underestimates energy
                     drop size, the distribution is skewed toward smaller drops, resulting in a curved PDF. When
                     using ∆ER , we obtain a qualitatively straight line, apparently showing a power-law regime for
                     six decades. However, when looking at the exponent determined by setting a cutoff, we see a
                     continuous slope, with only a small plateau, which is easy to miss in lower quality data. This
                     is in contrast to the clear plateau observed when using ∆ES , where the PDF and the scans
                     of α(∆ES,min ) and D(∆ES,min ) show a pronounced candidate crossover. Its uncertainty and
                     physical interpretation remain limited by the bootstrap goodness-of-fit results.
                         Using ∆ES , we find an exponent of α̂ = 1.03 ± 0.02 for irreversible post-yield events. This is
                     close to values near one found in two-dimensional plasticity models [135, 173], but smaller than
                     those reported in earlier scalar models and acoustic-emission experiments [13, 39]. Differences in
                     the observable and loading protocol limit a direct comparison.

2.4   System-size scaling
Single-system-size power-law fits can be misleading because the measured range may be affected
by finite-size cutoffs and other corrections to scaling. Finite-size scaling across several system
sizes therefore provides a more robust way to estimate the exponent and to test whether the
observed distributions are compatible with avalanche criticality [168].
    We assume the finite-size scaling form

                                                             ∆ES
                                                                      
                               pL (∆ES ) = AL ∆ES−α f               ,                         (5.25)
                                                            ∆Ec (L)

where AL accounts for normalization and finite-lower-cutoff corrections, pL is the avalanche-size
distribution for a system of linear size L, α is the avalanche exponent, f is a cutoff function, and
∆Ec (L) is the system-size-dependent cutoff. In the truncated power-law fits used above, the
cutoff is controlled by the parameter λ,

                                 p(∆ES ) ∝ ∆ES−α exp(−λ∆ES ),                                 (5.26)

so that the characteristic cutoff scale is proportional to

                                          ∆Ec (L) ∼ λ−1 .                                     (5.27)

We define the cutoff-scaling exponent Dλ by

                                λ(L) ∝ L−Dλ ,      ∆Ec (L) ∝ LDλ .                            (5.28)

    Figure 5.14(a) shows the fitted value of λ as a function of the system size. A log–log fit
over L ∈ {50, 100, 150, 200, 250} gives the descriptive slope Dλ = 1.175. We treat this value as
provisional because its uncertainty and stability under omission of individual sizes have not been
established. The same scaling can be tested directly by collapsing the probability distributions.
Using the cutoff scaling suggested by Eq. (5.27), the distributions are rescaled according to

                                   pL (∆ES )∆ESα    ∆ES
                                                              
                                                 =f                ,                          (5.29)
                                        AL          LDλ

with α = 1.04. The plotted comparison omits an amplitude correction, so interpreting it as a
collapse also assumes that AL is approximately size independent over the displayed range. The
comparison is shown in Fig. 5.14(b). The collapse is satisfactory over the main scaling range,
consistent with approximate scaling over that range. Deviations remain visible at small energy
drops, where event classification and non-universal corrections are expected to matter, and near
the largest events, where statistics are limited.
    Overall, the distributions are consistent with approximate avalanche scaling, with a PDF
exponent close to α ≃ 1.04 and an apparent energy-cutoff exponent Dλ ≃ 1.2. A cutoff exponent
close to unity is not unusual in two-dimensional plasticity: Salman and Truskinovsky report an
energy-cutoff exponent of 1.2 ± 0.1 in their scalar crystal-plasticity automaton, while Salman

Figure 5.14: System-size scaling analysis. (a) Inverse avalanche cutoff λ, obtained from the
truncated power-law fit, as a function of the system size L. Blue triangles show the measured
values, while the orange line shows a power-law fit, λ ∝ L−Dλ , over L ∈ {50, 100, 150, 200, 250},
with descriptive slope Dλ = 1.175. Its uncertainty and robustness to the fitted size range remain

et al. obtain a post-yield energy-cutoff exponent near unity in the tensorial model [13, 15].
Independently, Zaiser and Nikitas find that the cutoff in accumulated plastic slip grows linearly
with system size at fixed hardening rate and machine stiffness, consistent with system-spanning
slip lines or bands [72]. Although their observable differs from the energy drops studied here,
these results support the plausibility of predominantly band-like activity. An energy-cutoff
exponent does not, however, determine the spatial geometry of an avalanche by itself. The
present estimate remains provisional because of the limited system-size range and uncertainties in
the distribution tails. Its stability under omission of the largest system size must be established
before assigning significance to the precise fitted value of Dλ .


3      Effect of the relaxation algorithm
3.1      Relaxation algorithms
Non-convex energy landscapes can contain multiple local minima and competing equilibrium
branches. Consequently, the evolution selected under quasi-static loading may depend not only
on the initial state and loading protocol, but also on the numerical procedure used to traverse
the energy landscape. Terzi et al. demonstrated this sensitivity in a variational damage model,
showing that quasi-Newton minimization can delay bifurcations and thereby alter the selected
evolution path [174].
     Since we use our model to investigate critical behavior and its dependence on model parameters,
it is therefore important to establish whether the choice of minimization algorithm also influences


                                                              149

===== PDF page 161 of 215 =====

Chapter 5. Plastic avalanches


the measured avalanche statistics. Figure 5.17 first illustrates the underlying issue in a simple
energy landscape: starting from the same initial condition, three minimization algorithms
converge to three different local minima.
    We have studied three different minimization algorithms: the limited-memory Broyden–
Fletcher–Goldfarb–Shanno algorithm (L-BFGS), conjugate gradient (CG), and the Fast Inertial
Relaxation Engine (FIRE). As L-BFGS performed best in this comparison, it has been used for
all simulations for which the minimization algorithm is not specified.

3.1.1     BFGS and its limited-memory variant

The Broyden–Fletcher–Goldfarb–Shanno (BFGS) method is a quasi-Newton method that builds
an approximation to the inverse Hessian from successive force and position changes. We denote
this inverse-Hessian approximation by Hk . Given

                    sk = xk+1 − xk ,        yk = ∇Π(xk+1 ) − ∇Π(xk ) = fk − fk+1 ,                 (5.30)

the curvature information from the step is incorporated through the BFGS update. The inverse-
Hessian form is convenient because it gives the search direction directly as pk = Hk fk . In the
limited-memory BFGS algorithm (L-BFGS), only a fixed number of the most recent correction
pairs (sk , yk ) are stored, and a two-loop recursion applies the corresponding inverse-Hessian
approximation without forming the full matrix. Algorithm 5 summarizes the full BFGS update;
the simulations use the L-BFGS two-loop recursion [175, 176]. The common-event comparison
uses seven stored correction pairs.

Algorithm 5 BFGS
 1: Given an initial guess x0 and a convergence tolerance ϵ > 0, compute f0 = −∇Π(x0 ), set
      H0 = I, and set k = 0.
 2: while ∥fk ∥ > ϵ do
 3:     pk = Hk fk                                                          ▷ Compute search direction
 4:     αk = LMT (Π, xk , pk )                                                           ▷ Line search
 5:     xk+1 = xk + αk pk                                                                ▷ Take a step
 6:     fk+1 = −∇Π(xk+1 )                                                     ▷ Compute internal force
 7:     sk = xk+1 − xk                                                                  ▷ Change in x
 8:     yk = fk − fk+1                                                     ▷ Change in energy gradient
 9:     if yTk sk > 0 then
10:         ρk = (yTk sk )−1                                                         ▷ Curvature factor
11:         Hk+1 = (I − ρk sk yTk )Hk (I − ρk yk sTk ) + ρk sk sTk             ▷ Inverse-Hessian update
12:     else
13:         Apply a damped update or set Hk+1 = Hk                   ▷ Safeguard against loss of positive
    definiteness
14:     Set k ← k + 1                                                                        ▷ Next step




                                                     150

===== PDF page 162 of 215 =====

3. Effect of the relaxation algorithm


3.1.2   Nonlinear conjugate gradient

The nonlinear conjugate-gradient (CG) method [177] improves on the steepest-descent method
by searching in conjugate directions. fk denotes the force in the descent direction,

                                          fk = −∇Π(xk ).                                      (5.31)

Let LMT denote the More–Thuente line-search method [178] that we use to find a step size αk
satisfying the Wolfe conditions [179]. Algorithm 6 summarizes the hybrid Dai–Yuan/Hestenes–
Stiefel update used by the default ALGLIB solver in the current implementation. The force
convention gives yk = fk − fk+1 , the change in the energy gradient. An unsuccessful line search
restarts the direction; a periodic restart occurs every ndof + 3 iterations, where ndof is the number
of free displacement components.

Algorithm 6 Hybrid nonlinear conjugate gradient
 1: Given an initial guess x0 and a convergence tolerance ϵ > 0, compute f0 = −∇Π(x0 ), set the
    initial search direction p0 = f0 , and set k = 0.
 2: while ∥fk ∥ > ϵ do
 3:     αk = LMT (Π, xk , pk )                                                       ▷ Line search
 4:     xk+1 = xk + αk pk                                                            ▷ Take a step
 5:     fk+1 = −∇Π(xk+1 )                                                ▷ Compute internal force
 6:     yk ← fk − fk+1
 7:     if the line search satisfies the Wolfe conditions and no restart is requested then
                     ∥fk+1 ∥22
 8:         βDY ←
                      pTk yk
                        T
                    −fk+1 yk
 9:         βHS ←
                      pTk yk
10:         βk ← max{0, min(βDY , βHS )}
11:     else
12:         βk ← 0                                      ▷ Restart after an unsuccessful line search
13:     if a periodic restart is due then
14:         βk ← 0
15:     pk+1 ← fk+1 + βk pk
16:     Set k ← k + 1                                                                  ▷ Next step


     While very similar to steepest descent, it is the addition of βk pk to the search direction
pk+1 that allows the algorithm to avoid repeatedly searching in the same direction. For linear
                                                       )T fk
CG on a quadratic objective, one can use αk = p(fTk Ap       , where A is a matrix representation
                                                     k     k
of the linear system being solved. This expression applies to a quadratic objective, whereas
our implementation uses a line-search method. One limitation of CG is that it relies only on
first-order information. For non-quadratic problems, information about higher-order terms, such
as the Hessian, can result in faster convergence.

Chapter 5. Plastic avalanches


3.1.3   Fast Inertial Relaxation Engine

While CG and BFGS are line-search-based algorithms, the Fast Inertial Relaxation Engine (FIRE)
algorithm uses an MD-like integration scheme with adaptive velocity and time-step updates [180].
Algorithm 7 summarizes the normal iteration of the FIRE 2.0 solver, including its integration
order and half-step uphill correction [181, 182].
    The FIRE algorithm uses the internal force from the energy landscape to drive an MD-like
trajectory. It has two main modifications: (1) if the algorithm detects uphill motion, it stops the
motion, reduces the time step, and resets the velocity; and (2) at each step, the velocity v is
mixed with the internal force f according to

                                     v ← (1 − α)v + αbf ∥v∥,                                (5.32)

where the hat denotes a normalized vector and α is a steering parameter. Here, f (t) = −∇Π(x(t)),
so the power P = (f (t))T v(t) is positive for downhill motion because dΠ/dt = −P < 0. In our
implementation, we use velocity Verlet [183] as the MD integrator.
    For the common-event comparison, the configured time steps are ∆t0 = 0.01, ∆tmin = 10−8 ,
and ∆tmax = 0.03. The steering parameters are αstart = 0.1, finc = 1.1, fdec = 0.5, fα = 0.99,
and Nmin = 5. Unit masses are used for the fictitious dynamics, and the uncapped version applies
no componentwise displacement limit. These are numerical relaxation parameters, not physical
inertial constants.

Algorithm 7 FIRE 2.0: normal iteration with velocity Verlet
 1: Set x = x0 , v = 0, ∆t = ∆t0 , α = αstart , N+ = 0, and k = 0.
 2: Compute f = −∇Π(x).
 3: while ∥f ∥2 > ϵ do
 4:    (x, v, f ) ← VV∆t (x, v, f )                       ▷ Integrate and evaluate the new force
 5:    k ←k+1
 6:    if ∥f ∥2 ≤ ϵ then
 7:        return x
 8:    P ← f Tv
 9:    v ← (1 − α)v + αbf ∥v∥2
10:    if P > 0 then
11:        N+ ← N+ + 1
12:        if N+ > Nmin then
13:             ∆t ← min(finc ∆t, ∆tmax )
14:             α ← fα α
15:    else
16:        N+ ← 0
17:        x ← x − 21 ∆t v                                           ▷ Uphill position correction
18:        if k > Nmin then
19:             ∆t ← max(fdec ∆t, ∆tmin )
20:             α ← αstart
21:        v←0
22:        Retain f until the next velocity-Verlet force evaluation.
23: return x

3.2         Minimization paths and convergence efficiency
Figure 5.15 shows a simpler example in which the three algorithms all start close to a local
minimum but take different paths. As discussed in more detail below, L-BFGS and CG are
line-search algorithms, whereas FIRE is an inertial gradient-descent algorithm that follows a
smoother inertial trajectory. Figure 5.16 shows the norm of the energy gradient as a function of
the number of function evaluations. FIRE uses the most function evaluations, CG uses fewer
but exhibits some oscillatory behavior, and L-BFGS uses the fewest function evaluations. After
some initial probing and calibration, L-BFGS is also able to take a relatively direct path. Note
that these observations are made from a single minimization of a simple two-dimensional energy
landscape. To get a more general understanding of their behavior, we need to look at a more
realistic dataset.
    Figure 5.18 compares relaxation paths for 200 post-yield increments from ten L = 100
samples. Each minimizer is restarted from the same pre-relaxation configuration for increments
