Figure 5.17: Illustration of the path dependence of minimization algorithms on the six-hump
camel energy landscape [184]. Starting from the same initial condition, located near the upper-
right corner, L-BFGS (a), CG (b), and FIRE (c) converge to different local minima. This
initial condition was deliberately chosen to highlight the sensitivity of the relaxation path to the
minimization algorithm. Regions of large energy are deliberately not displayed, because they
would saturate the color scale and obscure the relaxation paths near the local minima.


first identified in L-BFGS runs. This conditions the comparison on L-BFGS-detected events;
common initial conditions do not guarantee a common final minimum. All three methods use the
force-norm stopping condition ∥f ∥2 < 10−5 , avoiding an ambiguous comparison of displacement-
based criteria; see Section 4.4 of Chapter 2. One function call is a combined energy-and-gradient
evaluation, including evaluations made inside line searches.
    For event j and method a, let Ej,a (k) be the running minimum of the energy after k
evaluations, and define
                                                                  
                           ∆Ej,max = max Ej,a (0) − min Ej,a (k) ,                               (5.33)
                                       a                 k

                                                         Ej,a (0) − Ej,a (k)
                                                                           !
                            rj,a (k) = max 10−12 , 1 −                       .                   (5.34)
                                                              ∆Ej,max

Thus the denominator is the largest completed energy release among the three methods for
the same event, not a maximum over different events. A method ending at a higher energy
can retain a nonzero residual in this representation. The evaluation count is divided by the
total L-BFGS count for that event, so L-BFGS ends at x = 1. To aggregate different-length
trajectories, the plotting procedure interpolates x and log10 r on a common 500-point grid of
fractional completion. The thick curves are geometric averages, and the horizontal bands span
one standard deviation in log x about its mean. The floor 10−12 keeps the logarithms finite; it is
a plotting convention, not a convergence tolerance. The displayed range is x ≤ 3.
    For these selected increments, L-BFGS uses the fewest evaluations on average. The geometric
mean evaluation-count ratios shown in the figure are 1.30 for FIRE and 1.84 for CG, relative to
L-BFGS. CG has greater event-to-event variability; one outlying event requires approximately
ten times the L-BFGS count and lies outside the displayed range.

3.3                  Comparison of avalanche statistics
Critical behavior is expected to be universal: the critical exponents should not depend on
microscopic or algorithmic details, provided these details do not change the universality class. It
is therefore useful to test the sensitivity of the avalanche statistics to the minimization algorithm.
This test is not a proof of universality, but it provides an important numerical consistency check:
if the measured avalanche distributions depended strongly on the relaxation procedure, the
extracted exponents could not be interpreted as robust properties of the model.
    Figure 5.19(a) compares the post-yield irreversible energy-drop distributions obtained with
FIRE, CG, and L-BFGS for systems of size L = 200. The three probability distributions are very
close over most of the measured range. Independently selected fits nevertheless yield different
exponents: approximately 0.93 for CG, 0.92 for L-BFGS, and 0.56 for FIRE. The selected lower
cutoffs also differ, so these fits sample different parts of the distributions and do not isolate an
algorithmic effect. A common-cutoff comparison, with uncertainty estimates and goodness-of-fit
tests, is needed before concluding that the fitted statistics are weakly affected by the relaxation
algorithm.
    Panel (b) shows the cutoff-dependent scans of D and α. Their broadly similar shapes provide a
qualitative comparison, but do not establish agreement of the independently selected parameters.




                                                                       156

===== PDF page 168 of 215 =====

4. Effect of mesh reconnection


4    Effect of mesh reconnection
Mesh reconnection modifies the plastic dynamics primarily by removing mesh-induced locking. In
non-reconnected simulations, the fixed mesh connectivity becomes progressively more distorted as
plastic strain accumulates. The energy then tends to increase systematically, and the system does
not reach a well-defined large-strain quasi-stationary regime. With reconnection, by contrast, the
mesh can adapt to the evolving deformation. The system reaches a quasi-stationary intermittent
regime, in which grain-boundary structures grow, shrink, and reorganize.
     Reconnection also changes the mechanisms available during plastic flow. When mesh locking
is present, dislocation glide can be artificially impeded by the mesh topology. With reconnection,
dislocations can propagate over larger distances and trigger further plastic activity. This can
produce larger collective events. The question addressed in this section is therefore whether
these additional mechanisms modify the avalanche statistics, and how they affect the observed
heavy-tailed energy-drop distributions.
     A direct comparison is limited by the fact that non-reconnected simulations become increas-
ingly difficult to minimize at large strain. The comparison is therefore restricted to a common
accessible strain range ending at γ = 1.
     Figure 5.20 compares post-yield corrected energy-drop distributions at L = 200, with and
without mesh reconnection. The empirical distributions and fitted intermediate ranges are similar
within the available statistics. The independently fitted exponents are α̂ = 0.93 ± 0.03 with
reconnection and 0.99 ± 0.03 without it. These parameter spreads alone do not establish a
common critical regime.
     The selected lower cutoff is larger without reconnection than with reconnection. This difference
may reflect a change in the small-event population or sensitivity of the KS-selected cutoff; the
present comparison does not distinguish those explanations. The upper cutoff also differs: the
fitted exponential parameter is (3.52 ± 0.28) × 10−2 with reconnection and (4.12 ± 0.33) × 10−2
without it, consistent with larger accessible events in the reconnected simulations. Suppression
of long-range dislocation motion by mesh locking is a possible interpretation, rather than a
mechanism established by these PDFs alone.
     At this system size and over the accessible strain interval, reconnection mainly changes the
fitted cutoff regions. Additional sizes and protocol-specific goodness-of-fit tests are required to
establish the robustness of a critical scaling regime.

===== PDF page 169 of 215 =====

range; differences are visible near the lower and upper cutoffs. The lower selected cutoff is larger
without reconnection. The displayed parameter uncertainties are bootstrap standard deviations.


Conclusion
In this last chapter, we used the MTM to study plastic avalanches under athermal quasistatic
loading. The simulations were performed with periodic boundary conditions, affine strain
increments, and relaxation to mechanical equilibrium after each loading step. Independent
samples of different sizes were generated by randomizing the initial relaxation guess of prestrained
configurations.
    Plastic fluctuations were analyzed through energy-drop statistics. We distinguished the
inter-strain energy drop ∆EI , the relaxation energy drop ∆ER , and the stress-corrected energy
drop ∆ES . The relaxation energy drop ∆ER was found to be useful for separating ordinary
elastic relaxation from plastic activity, whereas the second-order stress-corrected quantity ∆ES
was better suited for the statistical analysis of avalanche sizes.
    To identify plastic events, we introduced a reversibility test based on a loading–unloading
protocol. The displacement-closure measure drev separates reversible from irreversible events,
whereas the magnitude of ∆ES alone does not. Irreversibility was therefore used as the operational
definition of plasticity. For the larger simulations, where the reversibility protocol was not applied
systematically, the quantity κ = ∆ER /[Nel (∆γ)2 ] was used as a practical proxy to separate
ordinary elastic relaxation from irreversible events.
    The statistical analysis was restricted to positive irreversible ∆ES events. These events
were fitted with a truncated power-law density, p(∆ES ) ∝ ∆ES−α exp(−λ∆ES ), where the fitted


                                                     158

===== PDF page 170 of 215 =====

4. Effect of mesh reconnection


exponent α and the exponential cutoff λ were estimated by maximum likelihood. Following
the procedure of Clauset et al. [68], the lower cutoff ∆ES,min
                                                            KS    was selected by minimizing the
Kolmogorov–Smirnov distance, and the quality of the fit was assessed using bootstrap p-values.
    The resulting distributions show qualitative power-law behavior over an intermediate range.
However, the reported bootstrap tests reject the fitted single truncated-power-law model at the
adopted p = 0.1 criterion. Possible explanations include finite-size corrections, the small number
of independent samples, residual event dependence, classification effects, and a misspecified
scaling function. Larger systems and ensembles, alternative tail models, and dependence-aware
uncertainty estimates are needed to distinguish them. For the larger L-BFGS datasets used in
the finite-size analysis, the fitted exponent is approximately α ≃ 1.05, and the apparent energy
cutoff grows with system size. The precise cutoff-scaling exponent remains provisional.
    Finally, we tested the robustness of the avalanche statistics with respect to two numerical
choices. We first compared three relaxation algorithms with different numerical philosophies:
FIRE, which is inertia-based, and CG and L-BFGS, which are line-search methods. In terms
of performance, L-BFGS was the most efficient minimizer, while FIRE and CG required more
function calls. The distributions are visually similar over much of the measured range, but their
independently selected exponents are not uniformly similar: FIRE has a substantially smaller
selected exponent and a different selected cutoff.
    We then compared simulations with and without mesh reconnection. Reconnection removes
the artificial energy growth caused by mesh locking and enables larger collective events, grain-
boundary motion, and large-deformation plastic flow. Within the explored sizes and strain
intervals, the heavy-tailed energy-drop distributions are qualitatively similar across the tested
relaxation algorithms and reconnection protocols. The differing selected cutoffs and exponents,
together with the goodness-of-fit results, prevent a stronger claim of universal critical robustness
at present.




                                               159

===== PDF page 171 of 215 =====

General conclusions


   In this thesis, we have highlighted five main results concerning the numerical modelling of
crystal plasticity using the Mesoscopic Tensorial Model:
   • A mesh-reconnection method that addresses large mesh distortions and topological locking
      in the fixed-connectivity MTM mesh.

  • The emergence, at large deformation, of a quasi-stationary intermittent regime marked by
    spontaneous grain-like organization.

  • A unique multiplicative elastoplastic decomposition, defined by the shortest admissible
    path as a sequence of integer plastic shears.

  • A consistent method for transporting plastic history during mesh reconnection.

  • The robustness of the model’s critical avalanche statistics with respect to the static
    relaxation algorithm and mesh reconnection.

    The MTM provides a promising mesoscale framework for crystal plasticity. It combines two
natural ingredients as introduced in Chapter 2: a lattice-invariant energy density respecting
crystal symmetries and a finite-element kinematics based on piecewise affine deformation maps.
These ingredients place finite rotations, geometrically nonlinear elasticity, compatibility, long-
range elastic redistribution and plastic rearrangements within the same variational framework.
Plastic events then arise from transitions between lattice-equivalent wells in the non-convex
energy landscape, rather than from explicit dislocation lines or prescribed reaction rules [14,
127, 134]. Crack and damage mechanisms were not treated in the simulations of this thesis,
although related MTM work has used the same type of framework to study coupled crack and
dislocation evolution [16]. The standard MTM formulation nevertheless remained limited by
large-deformation mesh distortion, topological locking and the need to transport plastic history
consistently when moving beyond a purely Lagrangian description.
    We introduced in Chapter 3 a reconnection method as a way to remove topological locking in
the fixed-connectivity MTM mesh. The mesh connectivity determines which groups of three nodes
are used to reconstruct the local deformation gradient and evaluate the element energy. Local edge
flipping updates these groups without moving, adding, or removing nodes. The neighborhood
reconnection criterion is related to the geometric conditions of Delaunay triangulation.


                                               160

===== PDF page 172 of 215 =====

General conclusions


    To make such reconnections well defined, each element is assigned a canonical reference
triangle, which avoids degenerate reference Jacobians and makes affine reconnections mechanically
neutral up to lattice-equivalent relabelling. The resulting method removes mesh-induced locking
and allows continued intermittent plastic flow, with the stored energy fluctuating about an
approximately stable baseline over large strain. In large-strain simulations, it also gives access to
an emergent grain-boundary-like organization, not prescribed by the model. This observation
suggests a qualitative link with grain refinement under severe plastic deformation [153], but
remains limited to an idealized two-dimensional periodic setting and should not be interpreted
as a general recrystallization mechanism.
    The associated plastic-history problem is then treated separately in Chapter 4 where we
developed the plastic-reduction framework needed to recover physical information after canonical-
reference reconnection. The method uses an integer-valued multiplicative decomposition F =
Fe Fp , in which Fe gives the elastic state and Fp stores the lattice-basis changes identified as
plastic deformation. Unlike Lagrange reduction, plastic reduction must retain the accumulated
integer transformation associated with the material point. It is therefore path-dependent, even
when different paths lead to the same reduced metric. For the square lattice considered in this
thesis, we select the plastic deformation using the shortest admissible sequence of integer shears
in SL(2, Z). This construction yields a unique selected plastic deformation.
    In this construction, Tp is the integer plastic transformation accumulated as the plastic path
evolves. We store Tp directly, updating it with each plastic increment between reconnections.
During a non-affine edge flip, the elastic component is allowed to relax in the new element
geometry, while the accumulated plastic history is transported by keeping Tp unchanged. Thus,
mesh reconnection modifies the local representation of the element without resetting its plastic
memory. Together with a local inheritance rule for assigning old histories to new elements, this
gives a consistent history-transport procedure. The same reduced representation also separates
changes of lattice representation from loss of strong ellipticity and from relaxed collective plastic
events. Thus the reduction boundary should not be interpreted as a mechanical yield surface,
even though the framework is useful for comparing post-event elastic states and incremental
plastic flows.
    Chapter 5 used athermal quasistatic simulations with periodic boundary conditions to study
plastic avalanches in the MTM. The main avalanche observable was the stress-corrected energy
drop ∆ES . A second energy measure, ∆ER , was used to separate ordinary relaxation from
irreversible events. This separation was complemented by a reversibility test based on the closure
measure of the displacement ∆rev u. In practice, events were classified with κ = ∆ER /[N (∆γ)2 ],
and the statistical analysis was then restricted to irreversible events with positive ∆ES .
    The resulting avalanche statistics are compatible with a critical phenomenology, characterized
by power-law distributions of the type already observed in the MTM [15]. The fitting procedure
selected ∆ES,min
              KS   by minimizing the Kolmogorov–Smirnov distance and estimated the parameters
of a truncated power law by maximum likelihood [68].
    The comparison between L-BFGS, CG and FIRE showed that the minimization algorithm
affects efficiency and relaxation paths, but only weakly changes the avalanche exponents within


                                                161

===== PDF page 173 of 215 =====

General conclusions


the tests performed. Reconnection had a stronger effect on the mechanical state: it removed the
artificial energy growth associated with mesh locking and enabled larger collective events and
grain-boundary dynamics. Nevertheless, the fitted avalanche exponent remained comparatively
stable. Within the explored system sizes and fitting ranges, this suggests that the measured
critical statistics are robust with respect to algorithmic details, such as the choice of relaxation
procedure, and to the plastic-flow mechanisms introduced by mesh reconnection.


Perspectives
As a direct continuation of this thesis work, several research directions can be identified that
would consolidate the modelling of plastic deformation at the mesoscopic scale. These directions
require the development and implementation of new methods to address the questions that
remain open.
    A first perspective is to study systematically the interaction between crystal symmetry,
mesh topology and finite-element interpolation. The MTM construction used in this thesis
was restricted to a square lattice, but the same questions should be addressed for other crystal
symmetries, in particular hexagonal crystals. Different element families, including square or
quadrilateral elements, should therefore be compared in terms of mesh locking, reconnection
rules and plastic-history transport. Such a study would clarify when similarities or mismatches
between lattice and mesh symmetries produce numerical artifacts like preferential directions or
asymmetric dislocation cores. The longer-term goal is to simulate several lattice symmetries
with several mesh types while controlling the limitations introduced by each lattice–mesh pairing.
It would also be relevant to extend the present linear-triangular formulation to enriched or
nonlinear shape functions, and to study the consequences for reconnection and history transport
accordingly.
    The physical length scale associated with an MTM element remains an open issue. Relating
the mesh spacing h to the underlying atomic spacing a is necessary to compare simulated samples
and defect structures with their experimental counterparts at specified physical sizes. A first
calibration route is to map the MTM exactly onto an atomistic system through the Cauchy–Born
assumption, while neglecting non-local effects [185]. This approach gives promising preliminary
agreement with atomistic nano-indentation simulations and dislocation nucleation when the
energy is calibrated directly from an interatomic potential. Its limitation is that one node is still
identified with one atom, so the model is not yet coarse grained in a mesoscopic sense. A change
of scale cannot therefore be obtained by simply reinterpreting the same element as a larger block
of material; without modifying the energy landscape, the mechanical response would remain
unchanged. A rational coarse-graining procedure must instead increase the physical size of an
element while preserving controlled elastic and plastic behavior. This likely requires changing
the periodicity and amplitude of the MTM energy functional to decouple the numerical mesh
scale from the crystallographic slip scale, as first explored by Engrand [16].
    Beyond direct numerical simulation, the MTM also provides a compact language for inter-
preting collective plastic mechanisms. For example, its combination with molecular dynamics in


                                                162

===== PDF page 174 of 215 =====

General conclusions


a square-to-triangular structural transition showed that plastic slip can play a central role in the
transformation pathway [136]. The plastic-reduction framework developed in Chapter 4 strength-
ens this interpretive role by placing elastic states and plastic increments in a common reduced
representation. In this space, the elastoplastic decomposition and the yield-surface analysis
allow one to describe populations of local states, rather than tracking only individual real-space
events. This suggests a possible mean-field description of hardening-regime mesoscopic plasticity
based on the evolution of a state density in the reduced fundamental domain. The aim would
be to replace part of the spatially resolved dynamics by an evolution equation for this density,
while preserving information on changes in plastic deformation and stability boundaries. This
perspective is related to mean-field approaches to amorphous plasticity, but the corresponding
MTM closure remains to be derived [186].
    The extension to three dimensions is a natural next step, but it is not a purely technical
continuation of the present two-dimensional model. In particular, remeshing would no longer
reduce to a local triangular edge flip operation [144, 150, 151]. The dislocation physics also
changes qualitatively. A key example is the dissociation of perfect dislocations in fcc crystals into
Shockley partials separated by a stacking fault, where the equilibrium separation is controlled by
the stacking-fault energy [187–189]. Because the present MTM energy is local, it would assign
no energetic cost to such a stacking fault unless additional non-local terms were introduced.
It would therefore not select a finite partial-dislocation separation, an issue pointed out by
Tejedor [190]. Finally, the three-dimensional setting would require a corresponding generalization
of the elastoplastic decomposition and plastic-history transport developed in Chapter 4.
    Introducing disorder is another stimulating extension of the MTM. The disorder could first be
implemented at the discretization level, for example through variations of element size, or at the
material level through spatial variations of the MTM energy functional, such as elastic stiffness.
This would make it possible to use the same framework to explore a continuous crossover from
crystalline plasticity to amorphous-like mechanics, with complex multicomponent alloys providing
an intermediate regime as disorder is progressively increased [191–194]. The advantage of the
MTM here is that it would retain nonlinear kinematics, plastic advection and history transport,
as well as an explicit constitutive energy, whereas many mesoscopic amorphous models rely on
linear elasticity and cellular-automaton dynamics [195]. This perspective is also well aligned
with efforts to calibrate elastoplastic models quantitatively from atomistic amorphous solids
[196]. In addition, quenched correlated disorder in the energy landscape could provide a useful
representation of memory effects in those driven out-of-equilibrium solids [197, 198].




                                                163

===== PDF page 175 of 215 =====

Appendix A
    Numerical validation and element
              conditioning

    This appendix collects supporting tests for the numerical implementation. We first examine
the effect of retained mesh connectivity on a single-dislocation core, complementing the topological-
locking test in Chapter 3. We then present the element-conditioning calculations summarized in
Section 4.3.
A.1      Effect of mesh connectivity on a single-dislocation core
The isolated edge dislocation shown in Fig. A.1 tests the influence of retained connectivity on
the core structure. The upper half of a square lattice is shifted by nb while preserving the
original connectivity across the glide plane. An anisotropic Stroh displacement field for an
edge dislocation with Burgers vector b and magnitude h = ∥b∥ is then superimposed on this
configuration, using square crystal symmetry [199].
    Nodes within a radius Rfree = 70h of the core are relaxed, whereas the outer nodes remain
fixed to the imposed Stroh solution.
    The integer shift leaves the bulk lattice in an energetically equivalent state, but it stretches the
triangles crossing the glide plane. Comparing the relaxed core profiles of the shifted configurations
with the unshifted case n = 0 isolates the effect of retained connectivity. For the larger retained
shifts, the core broadens, its peak energy decreases, and its profile becomes left–right asymmetric.
Rebuilding the connectivity before relaxation moves the profile toward the n = 0 reference while
preserving the same outer displacement field.

A.2      Element conditioning under simple shear
A.2.1     Element Hessian and linearized equilibrium
For a triangular element e, let u(a) = x(a) − X(a) be the displacement of node a ∈ {1, 2, 3},
with reference position X(a) and current position x(a) . Collect its six Cartesian displacement
components in the vector

                                          (1)   (1)     (2)     (2)          (3)   (3)
                                    q = (u1 , u2 , u1 , u2 , u1 , u2 )T .                                            (A.1)

The element tangent stiffness matrix K is the Hessian of the element energy E (e) with respect to
these degrees of freedom:

                                          ∂ 2 E (e)
                                Kαβ =               ,          α, β ∈ {1, . . . , 6},                                (A.2)
                                          ∂qα ∂qβ




                                                        165

===== PDF page 177 of 215 =====

Appendix A. Numerical validation and element conditioning


with the reference nodes and connectivity held fixed. Thus K is a 6 × 6 matrix representing
the energy curvature in nodal displacement coordinates. Assembling the element matrices and
imposing the boundary conditions gives the system tangent Ksys . A Newton step for mechanical
equilibrium requires solving the linear system

                                           Ksys ∆u = f ,                                      (A.3)

where ∆u contains the free nodal displacement increments and f is the corresponding force
residual, equal to the negative energy gradient. The conditioning of this matrix therefore
matters for solving the linearized equilibrium problem, and its curvature also matters for energy
minimization.
    A distorted triangle can map comparable nodal displacement increments into very different
deformation-gradient increments. This geometric anisotropy contributes to the conditioning of K,
together with the nonlinear elastic response and the current stress. The underlying stored-energy
landscape is non-convex, so positive definiteness of the Hessian cannot be assumed throughout
the shear path.

A.2.2     Loading configurations
To examine how accumulated shear affects element conditioning, we impose homogeneous simple
shear on a periodic mesh of 3×3 square cells, split into 18 T3 elements, with mechanical relaxation
disabled. We compare two loading configurations that produce the same relative deformation
while distributing the geometric distortion differently between the current and reference triangles.
We use S(γ) for a shear of magnitude γ:

                            1 γ
                                 !
                  S(γ) =        ,        F = S(γcur )S(γref )−1 = S(γcur − γref ),            (A.4)
                            0 1

where γcur and γref specify the current and reference shears relative to the same unsheared lattice.
Figure A.2 illustrates the two ways of producing the same relative shear. In panel (a), the
reference triangle is unsheared and the current triangle carries the full shear γcur = 2.5. In panel
(b), the reference triangle carries γref = −2 while the current triangle has only γcur = 0.5. Both
give γcur − γref = 2.5, but the geometric distortion is distributed differently between the two
configurations.
    We extend these examples to two loading families, sampling r ∈ {0.1, 0.2, . . . , 0.9} and
n ∈ {0, 2, 5, 10}. The current-distortion test uses γref = 0 and γcur = n + r; the reference-
distortion test uses γref = −n and γcur = r. Both therefore sample the same relative shears n + r,
placing the integer distortion in different configurations. States separated by integer relative
shear are lattice-equivalent, whereas varying r within one period generally changes the elastic
state. The reference triangles are nondegenerate.

A.2.4      Condition numbers and constitutive curvature
A singular value measures how much a matrix stretches a vector along a principal direction. To
visualize this in two dimensions, apply a nonsingular matrix A to every unit vector: the unit
circle becomes an ellipse. Its longest and shortest semi-axis lengths are the largest and smallest
singular values, smax and smin . They give the greatest and smallest stretch produced by the
matrix. In higher dimensions, the same picture applies to a unit sphere and its image, an ellipsoid.


                                                                  167

===== PDF page 179 of 215 =====

Appendix A. Numerical validation and element conditioning


In an energy-landscape analogy, a Hessian describes curvature, whereas the gradient describes
slope. At a stationary state with a positive-definite Hessian, the smallest singular value gives
the weakest curvature: its direction has the smallest quadratic energy cost for a displacement of
fixed length. At a saddle, singular values retain curvature magnitudes but lose their signs, so the
smallest singular value does not identify the direction of greatest energy decrease.
    The function κ2 denotes the spectral condition number, the ratio of these greatest and
smallest stretches. For a nonsingular matrix A,

                                                                   smax (A)
                                     κ2 (A) = ∥A∥2 ∥A−1 ∥2 =                .                     (A.9)
                                                                   smin (A)

Here ∥ · ∥2 is the operator norm induced by the Euclidean vector norm. A large ratio means
that the matrix responds much more strongly in some directions than in others. If the smallest
singular value is zero, a nonzero vector is mapped to zero: the matrix is singular and its condition
number is infinite.
    Formally, the singular values are defined from the eigenvalues λi of AT A by
                   q
        si (A) =       λi (AT A),       smax (A) = max si (A),          smin (A) = min si (A).   (A.10)
                                                       i                            i

For a symmetric matrix, the singular values are the absolute values of its eigenvalues. For a
symmetric positive-definite matrix, they therefore equal the eigenvalues themselves. In particular,
the symmetric element tangent considered below can have negative eigenvalues, but its singular
values retain only their magnitudes. Its condition number measures directional contrast without
retaining the signs that distinguish positive from negative energy curvature.
    Previous studies distinguish the effects of discretization, element geometry, and material or
operator coefficients on finite-element conditioning [200–203]. Finite-strain tangents also contain
distinct constitutive and initial-stress contributions [204]. To examine these effects, we monitor
geometric, kinematic, constitutive, and full-tangent condition numbers separately. The reference
and current geometric condition numbers are

                                      κX = κ2 (DX ),          κx = κ2 (Dx ).                     (A.11)

The kinematic condition numbers associated with the relative deformation are

                                     κF = κ2 (F),          κC = κ2 (C) = κ2F .                   (A.12)

   Following Appendix B.6, the fourth-order second elasticity tensor C is defined in the work-
conjugate pair (E, S), where E is the Green–Lagrange strain. Let

                                       1
                                    E = (C − I),           ψ(E) = Φ(I + 2E).                     (A.13)
                                       2




                                                       168

===== PDF page 180 of 215 =====

A.2. Element conditioning under simple shear


The second Piola–Kirchhoff stress and the material tangent are then

                                  ∂ψ    ∂Φ                ∂S     ∂2Φ
                           S=        =2    ,         C=      =4      .                     (A.14)
                                  ∂E    ∂C                ∂E    ∂C∂C

In components, the factor of 4 is already contained in C:

                                        δSIJ = CIJKL δEKL .                                (A.15)

   For the constitutive condition number, we consider C as the linear map δE 7→ δS = C : δE,
where the colon denotes contraction over two tensor indices. To compute its singular values,
we measure both symmetric tensor increments with the Frobenius norm, ∥A∥2F = A : A, and
represent them in an orthonormal basis of the three-dimensional space of symmetric tensors.
Using the reference Cartesian vectors e1 , e2 , choose

                                                                  e1 ⊗ e2 + e2 ⊗ e1
                 Q1 = e1 ⊗ e1 ,       Q2 = e2 ⊗ e2 ,       Q3 =          √          .      (A.16)
                                                                           2
                                                                                √
Denote this basis by BM = (Q1 , Q2 , Q3 ). The coordinates of E are (E11 , E22 , 2E12 )T , whose
squared Euclidean norm equals E : E. The same normalization is used for stress. The constitutive
matrix in this common basis is

                          [C]BM µν = Qµ : (C : Qν ),         µ, ν ∈ {1, 2, 3}.             (A.17)
                                  


Here C : Qν is the stress response to the strain increment Qν , and contraction with Qµ extracts
its µth coordinate. Since BM is orthonormal, the ordinary matrix singular values are those of the
constitutive operator with the specified tensor norms, so we define

                                      κcon = κ2 (C) = κ2 ([C]BM ) .                        (A.18)
                                                                                     √
The resulting condition number is independent of the chosen orthonormal basis. The 2 weighting
of the shear coordinate is essential: omitting it would change the relative weighting of shear and
normal increments in the Euclidean coordinate norm. As with the other singular-value condition
numbers, κcon measures directional contrast without distinguishing positive from negative energy
curvature.

A.2.5    Nodal Hessian and removal of translation modes
The element tangent is obtained by differentiating the element energy with respect to the
six current nodal displacement degrees of freedom, holding the reference nodal positions and
connectivity fixed. This is the local variation used during mechanical relaxation. In particular,
δDX = 0 and δF = δDx D−1     X ; differentiating with respect to reference nodes would define a
different Hessian.

                                       Kikab

    The element tangent has two exact null modes corresponding to uniform translations. To
define the tangent with these modes removed, let Z ∈ R6×4 have orthonormal columns spanning
the subspace orthogonal to (1, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0)T and (0, 1, 0, 1, 0, 1)T , so that ZT Z = I4 . The tangent
restricted to the translation-free displacement subspace is

                                                    Kred = ZT KZ.                                 (A.26)


                                                         170

===== PDF page 182 of 215 =====

A.2. Element conditioning under simple shear


The subscript red denotes this restriction of the nodal matrix, not a lattice reduction or a different
elasticity tensor. It distinguishes the 4 × 4 matrix from the full 6 × 6 matrix K, whose translation
modes make its condition number infinite. The reported tangent condition number is

                                          κtan = κ2 (Kred ).                                   (A.27)

    The calculation used for the plotted values obtains all six eigenvalues of K, sorts their absolute
values, and discards the two smallest before forming the ratio of the largest to the smallest
remaining value. It does not explicitly construct Z. For the 144 sampled configurations (2592
element spectra), the recorded comparison with explicit projection gives a maximum relative
condition-number difference of 5.46 × 10−9 . The smallest retained absolute eigenvalue is at
least 1.52 × 108 times the largest discarded one. This separation identifies the two discarded
modes as translations for these data. Near an additional zero mode, explicit projection is needed
rather than removal by magnitude alone. Sorting by absolute value is essential because negative
eigenvalues need not correspond to translations.
    Only translations are removed. A rigid rotation also preserves energy, but each node follows
a circular path. Let q(θ) collect the components of R(θ)xa − Xa , with the reference nodes fixed.
Objectivity gives
                                  d2 E (e) (q(θ))
                             0=                   = q̇T Kq̇ + ∇q E (e) · q̈.                    (A.28)
                                        dθ2
Away from stationarity, the path-curvature term can cancel the Hessian contribution, so an
infinitesimal rotation need not be a Hessian null mode. At a stationary state, objectivity also
makes every rotated state stationary; differentiating that equilibrium condition gives Kq̇ = 0.

A.2.6     Conditioning under current and reference distortion
The nodal Hessian includes both constitutive and finite-stress contributions. It remains an
element measure: its condition number does not determine the conditioning of the assembled,
constrained system or the cost of global minimization. Each plotted value is taken from one
element after checking that all 18 elements, including both triangle orientations, agree within
relative tolerance 10−8 and absolute tolerance 10−10 . Thus the curves show a common element
value rather than an average or a maximum.
    Figure A.3 shows the resulting condition numbers, with fixed connectivity (dashed curves) and
with reconnection by edge flipping (solid curves). The left column shows the current-distortion
test against γcur mod 1: the four sampled shear intervals are superposed so that lattice-equivalent
states share the same horizontal coordinate. The right column shows the reference-distortion
test directly against γcur , with a separate color for each fixed reference shear.
    With fixed connectivity, increasing the imposed integer distortion generally increases the
condition numbers of the geometric, relative-deformation and constitutive matrices. In the
current-distortion test, this also increases κtan : accumulated shear increases the directional
contrast of energy curvature in nodal displacement coordinates, without specifying the signs
of that curvature. With reconnection, the curves for successive shear intervals instead coincide


                                                 171

===== PDF page 183 of 215 =====

Appendix A. Numerical validation and element conditioning


when plotted against γcur mod 1. Reconnection removes the additional conditioning associated
with accumulated integer shear, while preserving the variation within a shear period.
    In the reference-distortion test, κX is constant along each curve because the horizontal axis
varies the current shear while the reference triangle remains fixed. Its level nevertheless increases
with the magnitude of the integer reference shear. The relative-deformation and constitutive
condition numbers also increase between these reference choices, with or without reconnection.
By contrast, κtan follows the same curve for all tested reference shears. These are two different
observations: the flatness of κX follows from the loading protocol, whereas the coincidence of
κtan requires examining how the constitutive tangent and shape-function gradients enter the
nodal Hessian.

(18 T3 elements) without mechanical minimization, sampled at r = 0.1, 0.2, . . . , 0.9. Each curve
reports the common element value after verifying agreement among all 18 elements. Dashed

flipping. Left: distorted current geometry with γref = 0. The horizontal coordinate is γcur mod 1,
and the colors identify the intervals 0 < γcur < 1, 2 < γcur < 3, 5 < γcur < 6, and 10 < γcur < 11.
Right: distorted reference geometry with 0 < γcur < 1. The horizontal coordinate is γcur , and
the colors identify γref = 0, −2, −5, −10. The measures κx and κX quantify the conditioning of
the current and reference geometries, respectively; κC quantifies the conditioning of the right
Cauchy–Green tensor; κcon gives the constitutive conditioning; and κtan gives the condition

Table A.1: Effect of increasing integer shear at a fixed fractional shear, with fixed connectivity.
Comparisons are between shear intervals or reference choices, not along the horizontal axis within
one curve.

   Quantity                       Current-distortion test          Reference-distortion test
   Reference geometry κX          Unchanged                        Increases
   Current gradients ha           Change with triangle distor-     Unchanged
                                  tion
   Spatial tangent a              Unchanged                        Unchanged
   Nodal conditioning κtan        Generally increases              Unchanged


These still differ by the same integer lattice-basis transformation, so lattice invariance gives equal
energy densities for every sufficiently small L. On corresponding twice-differentiable branches,
their energy curvatures with respect to this additional spatial deformation are therefore equal.
These curvatures have components Jaijkl ; since det M(12) (n) = 1, the two states also have the
same J. For the simple-shear family, the matrix identity S(γ + n) = S(γ)M(12) (n) then gives,
wherever the energy is twice differentiable,

                         a S(γ + n) = a S(γ) ,                   0 < γ < 1.                    (A.32)
                                               
                                                       n ∈ Z,

    In particular, S(r + n) = S(r)S(−n)−1 , so the two loading families at matching n, r have the
same relative gradient.
    To relate this result to conditioning, return to Eq. (A.30). The current area JA(0) multiplies
the entire nodal Hessian by a positive scalar and therefore cancels from its singular-value ratio.
With the same spatial tangent at matching fractional shears, any difference in κtan between the
two tests must come from the current shape-function gradients.
    In the reference-distortion test, the current triangles are the same for every integer reference
shear at a fixed γcur . Both ha and a are therefore unchanged, so the complete nodal Hessian and
its translation-free condition number are unchanged.
    In the current-distortion test with fixed connectivity, the current triangles instead become
increasingly sheared. Their gradients ha = D−T    x g b a therefore change even though the spatial
tangent repeats at each shear period. The map from nodal increments to spatial deformation
becomes more anisotropic, producing the observed growth of κtan . Reconnection removes the
accumulated current distortion and hence this additional source of ill-conditioning.
    Table A.1 summarizes the comparison. For this family of lattice-equivalent states, integer
distortion of the reference triangle is less consequential for nodal conditioning than the same
accumulated shear of the current triangle: the former leaves κtan unchanged, whereas the latter
generally increases it with fixed connectivity. This result concerns integer lattice-equivalent
reference changes; a general reference distortion can change the elastic state and therefore the
spatial tangent.

Appendix B
                       Proofs and derivations

    This appendix collects the lattice-invariance, constitutive, and geometric derivations used
in the main text. It begins with lattice-basis changes, rigid-motion invariance, and changes of
material reference, then derives the stress and elasticity tensors. The final sections establish the
geometric results used by the reconnection algorithm.
B.1      Proof: generators of lattice-preserving basis changes

                                                     e = (e1 ; e2 )                                          (B.1)

be a lattice basis in R2 , and let
                                             L(e) = {e n : n ∈ Z2 }.                                         (B.2)

We prove two facts:

   1. the matrices {M(1) , M(12) (1), M(21) (1)} generate GL(2, Z);

   2. two bases generate the same lattice if and only if they are related by an element of GL(2, Z).

If ē = eM with M ∈ GL(2, Z), then L(ē) = L(e).             Indeed,

B.2. Proof: invariance of the energy under rigid translations and rotations


If L(ē) = L(e), then ē = eM for some M ∈ GL(2, Z). Since each column of ē lies in L(e),
each is an integer linear combination of the columns of e, so ē = eM for some M ∈ Mat2×2 (Z).
Similarly, e = ēN for some N ∈ Mat2×2 (Z). Substituting gives e = eMN, hence MN = I and
M ∈ GL(2, Z). Therefore ē = eM with M ∈ GL(2, Z).

Conclusion. The matrices {M(1) , M(12) (1), M(21) (1)} generate GL(2, Z), and two bases gen-
erate the same lattice if and only if they are related by right multiplication by an element of
GL(2, Z).

B.3     Changing the material reference of the right Cauchy–Green
        tensor
This appendix explains how to use the MTM energy for non-square lattice samples while
measuring deformation from the sample’s own reference configuration. With this choice, the
right Cauchy–Green tensor satisfies C(0) = I in the undeformed sample, regardless of its lattice
symmetry. The inner density Φ0 introduced in Section 3 of Chapter 2, however, uses a metric
tensor referred to a normalized square lattice. We therefore construct that tensor, denoted by
Csq , from the sample-relative C before evaluating the energy. A triangular lattice provides the
example.
    The main-text formulation is the square-reference case of the same construction: the reference
map is H = I, so Csq = C. There is consequently no need to distinguish the two metric tensors
or introduce a second reference configuration in Chapter 2.

B.3.1    From the sample reference to the square reference
Let H be a fixed invertible linear map from the normalized square reference to the sample
reference. With origins chosen consistently, X = HXsq relates their material positions. If F
maps sample-reference material vectors to current vectors, the composite map from the square
reference to the current configuration is

                                             Fsq = FH.                                       (B.22)

The corresponding right Cauchy–Green tensors are therefore related by

                             C = FT F,        Csq = FT
                                                     sq Fsq = H CH.
                                                               T
                                                                                             (B.23)

The transpose denotes the Euclidean adjoint. The two tensors describe the same current
configuration relative to different material references; this is a change of reference, not merely a
change of basis for C. In the undeformed sample configuration, C(0) = I, whereas C(0)    sq = H H.
                                                                                                T

    To connect this construction to Chapter 2, let {easq } be the orthonormal basis generating
the normalized square lattice, and let {eI0 } be an orthonormal Cartesian basis of the sample
reference. Choose H so that

                                 Heasq = aa(0) ,         aa(0) = QIa eI0 .

Thus the matrix of H in these bases is the reference lattice coordinate matrix Q:

                         [H]{e 0 }←{e sq } = Q,      [Csq ]e sq = QT [C]e 0 Q.               (B.24)

The tensor H changes the material reference; Q is its matrix in the specified Cartesian bases.
Explicitly, QT [C]e 0 Q also equals the lattice Gram matrix G of Chapter 2. Equivalently,
G = [C]A(0) gives the covariant components of C in the sample reference lattice basis.


                                                   178

===== PDF page 190 of 215 =====

B.4. Proof: GL(2, Z)-invariance of the reduced energy density


   The deformation gradients are two-point tensors. Their matrices use the current Cartesian
basis {ei } for the codomain and the appropriate reference Cartesian basis for the domain. When
these bases are understood, we also use the tensor symbols for their matrix representations.

B.3.2    Triangular sample reference
For the normalized square reference, Q = I and the two reference configurations coincide. For an
area-normalized triangular reference, choose

                                   4       1 1/2
                                  1/4               !
                          Q△ =               √     ,        det Q△ = 1.                      (B.25)
                                   3       0   3/2

Although C(0) = I in the undeformed triangular sample, its square-reference tensor has the
Cartesian matrix
                                               2   1 1/2
                                                            !
                          [Csq ]e = Q△ Q△ = √
                            (0) sq    T
                                                              .                     (B.26)
                                                3 1/2 1

This is the triangular symmetry point in the strain coordinates used by Φ0 ; the identity represents
the square symmetry point. The square-reference tensor retains the geometry of the triangular
sample even when its sample-relative metric tensor is the identity.

B.3.3    Transforming the energy argument
The inner density Φ0 accepts the reduced right Cauchy–Green tensor with the normalized square
lattice reference. To use this same density when C refers to the sample reference, we first
construct Csq = HT CH and then apply Lagrange reduction in the square-reference Cartesian
basis. For the area-preserving reference choices considered here, the sample-reference energy
density is                                                       
                          Φ(C) = Φ0 (RL (Csq )) = Φ0 RL (HT CH) .                       (B.27)

We retain the notation Φ for the energy density, with the chosen reference map H fixed and
understood. For H = I, this is the main-text expression Φ(C) = Φ0 (RL (C)). The Cartesian
matrix convention is understood in RL . For the triangular example, Φ0 is consequently evaluated
at the triangular symmetry point when C = I. Inserting the sample-relative identity directly
would instead evaluate the square symmetry point. The reference change in Eq. (B.25) preserves
area, so the reference area measures also coincide. For a reference change with det H ̸= 1, a
density per unit sample-reference area additionally requires the factor 1/| det H|.


B.4     Proof: GL(2, Z)-invariance of the reduced energy density
Let C be a positive-definite metric tensor and let
                                       n                           o
                              O(C) = MT CM : M ∈ GL(2, Z)                                    (B.28)

   The Lagrange reduction map RL assigns to each metric tensor a reduced representative in
the fundamental domain
                                  n                                         o
                           DL = C ∈ Q+
                                     2 : 0 ≤ 2C12 ≤ C11 ≤ C22 .                          (B.29)

                                      {M(1) , M(12) (1), M(21) (1)}                      (B.30)

                                         RL (C) = MT

                                       RL (MT CM) = RL (C).                              (B.35)

B.6       Derivation of the first and second elasticity tensors and the
          acoustic tensors
This appendix extends the derivation of the first Piola–Kirchhoff stress to the first elasticity tensor
A, the second elasticity tensor C, and the spatial push-forward a of A. The terms “first” and
“second” refer to the associated first and second Piola–Kirchhoff stresses; both elasticity tensors
are fourth-order tensors. Lowercase indices refer to the current configuration and uppercase
indices to the reference configuration. Repeated indices are summed. All expressions are local to
one smooth elastic branch of the Lagrange reduction, so the reduction matrix M        f is treated as
constant. We assume an orientation-preserving deformation, J = det F > 0.

B.6.1    Second elasticity tensor
Introduce the Green–Lagrange strain

                                                1

                                         ∂SIJ    ∂SIJ       ∂2Φ

All derivatives with respect to C are understood as derivatives on the space of symmetric tensors,
with C12 = C21 treated as one independent variable.
      Throughout the stress and elasticity derivations, we use the independent component ordering
(c1 , c2 , c3 ) = (Ce11 , Ce22 , Ce12 ). The reduced Hessian is the symmetric 3 × 3 matrix

                                            ∂ 2 Φ0

B.6.2    First elasticity tensor
The first elasticity tensor is associated with the work-conjugate pair (P, F). It is the constitutive
tangent with respect to the deformation gradient used in the total-Lagrangian linearization and

in the material acoustic tensor:

                                                ∂PiJ     ∂2Φ
                                    AiJkL =          =           .                         (B.69)

In two dimensions, each compound index (i, J) has four possible values. For example, one may
use the ordering

                     1 ↔ (1, 1),      2 ↔ (1, 2),         3 ↔ (2, 1),      4 ↔ (2, 2).     (B.70)

                                     δPiJ = SLJ δFiL + FiR δSRJ .                          (B.71)

                                              1

                                   AiJkL = FiR CRJSL FkS + δik SJL .                       (B.74)

At an undeformed, stress-free state, where F = I and S = 0, the first and second elasticity
tensors A and C have the same numerical components after the current and reference Cartesian
bases are identified. Away from that state, they are different tensors.

B.6.3    Spatial push-forward
The push-forward of A, expressed per unit current volume, is

                                                 1

where J = det F. For comparison, the spatial constitutive tensor obtained by pushing forward
the second elasticity tensor is

                                              1
                                    cijkl =     FiI FjJ FkK FlL CIJKL .                    (B.76)
                                              J

Substitution of Eq. (B.74) into Eq. (B.75) shows that the two spatial tensors are related by

                                         aijkl = cijkl + δik σjl .                         (B.77)

Thus a contains both the spatial constitutive tangent and the Cauchy initial-stress contribution.

B.6.5    Material and spatial acoustic tensors
For a unit vector n(0) in the reference configuration, define the components of the material
acoustic tensor by
                                                       (0) (0)
                                  Qik (n(0) ) = AiJkL nJ nL .                        (B.86)

For a unit vector n in the current configuration, define the components of the spatial acoustic
tensor by
                                      qik (n) = aijkl nj nl .                            (B.87)

   The nonzero vector p specifies an incremental displacement in the current configuration. A
small displacement field δu(X) = εp(n(0) · X) has the rank-one deformation-gradient increment
δF = εp ⊗ n(0) . The strong-ellipticity condition is

                        pi Qik (n(0) )pk > 0    for every nonzero p and unit n(0) ,            (B.88)

or, equivalently,
                           pi qik (n)pk > 0     for every nonzero p and unit n.                (B.89)

In two dimensions, strong ellipticity is equivalent to
                                                                   
                                         min         λmin Q(n(0) ) > 0.                        (B.90)
                                       ∥n(0) ∥2 =1


The corresponding directional minimum of the spatial acoustic tensor gives the equivalent test.
Along a continuous loading path that begins in a strongly elliptic state, the first loss occurs when
this minimum reaches zero. The associated eigenvector supplies the critical polarization p.


B.7      Proof: a noncollinear point triple determines a unique affine
         map
Let a, b, c ∈ R2 be noncollinear source points and let ā, b̄, c̄ ∈ R2 be arbitrary target points. We
seek an affine map
                                         φ(x) = Fx + d                                          (B.91)

that maps each source point to its corresponding target.

Linear part.        Subtracting the condition at a from those at b and c gives

                               F(b − a) = b̄ − ā,             F(c − a) = c̄ − ā.             (B.92)



                                                        187

===== PDF page 199 of 215 =====

Appendix B. Proofs and derivations


Define the edge matrices by their columns,
                              h              i              h             i
                         A= b−a c−a ,                  Ā = b̄ − ā c̄ − ā .                (B.93)

The source points are noncollinear, so A is invertible. Thus

                                    FA = Ā,       F = ĀA−1                                 (B.94)

uniquely determines the linear part.

Translation.    The condition at a then uniquely determines

                                           d = ā − Fa.                                      (B.95)

These formulas also satisfy the two remaining interpolation conditions, so they establish both
existence and uniqueness of the affine map.

Admissibility as a deformation. The map is invertible if and only if the target points are
also noncollinear. It is orientation preserving if and only if the ordered source and target triples
have the same orientation, since
                                                  det Ā
                                         det F =         > 0.                                (B.96)
                                                  det A
Arbitrary target points determine a unique affine map, but need not define an orientation-
preserving deformation.


B.8     Triangle domains and the neighborhood criterion
For two independent edges directed from a common vertex, let G denote their positive-definite
Gram matrix, with entries Gab = aa · ab . We use the three domains introduced in Section 2.1.3 in
Chapter 3:

                                      2 : 0 ≤ 2G12 ≤ G11 ≤ G22 },
                           DL = {G ∈ Q+                                                      (B.97)

                                      2 : 0 ≤ 2G12 ≤ min(G11 , G22 )},
                           DE = {G ∈ Q+                                                      (B.98)
                           DT = {G ∈ Q+
                                      2 : 0 ≤ G12 ≤ min(G11 , G22 )}.                        (B.99)

The domain DL describes a length-ordered reduced pair; DE removes the length ordering; and
DT allows any base vertex of a non-obtuse triangle.

B.8.1    Choice of incident edges
The domain DT tests whether a nondegenerate triangle contains a Lagrange-reduced incident
pair, without selecting that pair in advance. Let a1 and a2 be two edge vectors from a chosen
vertex, with Gram matrix G. The incident edge pairs at the other vertices are (−a1 , a2 − a1 ) and


                                                 188

===== PDF page 200 of 215 =====

B.8. Triangle domains and the neighborhood criterion


(−a2 , a1 − a2 ). Their scalar products are

                 (−a1 ) · (a2 − a1 ) = G11 − G12 ,         (−a2 ) · (a1 − a2 ) = G22 − G12 .

Thus all three angles are non-obtuse precisely when

                                       0 ≤ G12 ≤ min(G11 , G22 ),

that is, G ∈ DT by Eq. (B.99).
     Use the edge labels ℓ1 , ℓ2 , ℓ3 and the three metrics of Eq. (3.7) in Chapter 3. Here ℓi denotes
an edge and ∥ℓi ∥ its length. Relabel the edges, if necessary, so that ∥ℓ1 ∥ ≤ ∥ℓ2 ∥ ≤ ∥ℓ3 ∥. Then
a1 , a2 run along the two shortest edges, directed from their common vertex opposite ℓ3 , and

           A = ∥ℓ1 ∥2 ,       B = ∥ℓ2 ∥2 ,     C = a1 · a2 ,        L = ∥ℓ3 ∥2 = A + B − 2C.

With this edge order and the same choice at equal-length ties, the canonical metric is G (e) = G (e1) .
The metrics G (e2) and G (e3) are based opposite ℓ2 and ℓ1 , respectively. They use (−a1 , a2 − a1 )
and (−a2 , a1 − a2 ), respectively. Each change of base vertex is unimodular and preserves the
edge-generated lattice. Since L ≥ B ≥ A, we have 2C ≤ A ≤ B. For a non-obtuse triangle,
C ≥ 0, so the first matrix belongs to DL . Conversely, a pair in DL has nonnegative vertex scalar
products C, A − C and B − C, hence defines a non-obtuse triangle. This proves the equivalence
between membership in DT and the existence of a length-ordered reduced pair among the triangle
edges.
    Allowing either order of the canonical pair gives DE . The images of G (e2) and G (e3) , allowing
either edge order, define DE1 and DE2 , respectively.
    Explicitly, for a general metric G, set d = G11 + G22 − 2G12 , the squared length of the omitted
edge. These images are

                          DE = {G ∈ DT : d ≥ max(G11 , G22 )},                                 (B.100)
                      DE1 = {G ∈ DT : min(G11 , G22 ) ≤ d ≤ max(G11 , G22 )},                  (B.101)
                      DE2 = {G ∈ DT : d ≤ min(G11 , G22 )}.                                    (B.102)

They classify the omitted side as longest, intermediate, or shortest. Every non-obtuse triangle
representation has one of these possibilities; conversely, choosing its vertex opposite a longest
side recovers a canonical pair in DE . This proves

                                        DT = DE ∪ DE1 ∪ DE2 .

The non-strict inequalities retain all equal-length ties. The regions overlap when the omitted side
ties an incident side; all three contain the equilateral metric. Thus ties may change the labels of
the vertex representations but not the union or the acceptance decision. For the canonical pair,
L ≥ max(A, B) already implies 2C ≤ min(A, B), even for an obtuse triangle. Thus, for this edge



                                                     189

===== PDF page 201 of 215 =====

Appendix B. Proofs and derivations


choice, membership in DE reduces to C ≥ 0:

                                                         (e)
                                   G (e) ∈ DE   ⇐⇒      G12 ≥ 0.                            (B.103)

B.8.2    Equivalence with Voronoi neighborhood on a lattice
Assume now that the incident edges a1 , a2 form a basis of the nodal Bravais lattice Λ. A nonzero
lattice vector w joins sites with intersecting closed Voronoi cells if and only if it is a shortest
vector in its class w + 2Λ [205]. If all three triangle edges are such vectors, comparison with
other vectors in their respective classes gives

                           ∥a2 − a1 ∥2 ≤ ∥a2 + a1 ∥2   =⇒      G12 ≥ 0,
                               ∥a1 ∥2 ≤ ∥a1 − 2a2 ∥2   =⇒      G12 ≤ G22 ,
                               ∥a2 ∥2 ≤ ∥a2 − 2a1 ∥2   =⇒      G12 ≤ G11 .

Hence G ∈ DT is necessary. Conversely, the three vectors a1 , a2 − a1 , and −a2 sum to zero and
any two form a basis of Λ. Their pairwise scalar products are G12 − G11 , G12 − G22 , and −G12 . For
G ∈ DT , all are nonpositive, so these vectors form an obtuse superbase. By the obtuse-superbase
characterization in Ref. [205], its vectors are Voronoi vectors, proving sufficiency. The non-strict
inequalities include contacts at a Voronoi vertex; in particular, both diagonals are admissible in
the rectangular case. The lattice-basis assumption is essential: the edge-generated lattice need
not coincide with the full nodal arrangement.

B.8.3    Ties between longest edges
A nondegenerate triangle with two or more longest edges of equal length is acute. Indeed, equal
longest edges have equal opposite angles, and these are the largest angles of the triangle. If either
were at least π/2, their sum would be at least π, leaving no positive angle at the third vertex.
Thus all three angles are strictly less than π/2. By Eq. (B.99), its edge-pair metric therefore
belongs to DT at every vertex.
    Consequently, a triangle with tied longest edges does not require an edge flip under the
neighborhood criterion. Since the algorithm only flips pairs for which both triangles lie outside
DT , such a triangle cannot participate in a flip. Any ambiguity in its twin relation therefore
does not affect which flips are performed. In particular, every triangle outside DT has a unique
longest edge.


B.9     Lagrange-reduced bases in a Delaunay triangulation
Let Λ ⊂ R2 be a two-dimensional Bravais lattice. We use the weak Delaunay convention:
the open circumdisk of each triangle contains no lattice site, while additional sites may lie on
its circumcircle. Under this convention, every Delaunay triangle of Λ is primitive and non-
obtuse. Appendix B.8 then guarantees that its two shortest edges, ordered by length, form a
Lagrange-reduced basis. We use the domains defined there.

                                                190

===== PDF page 202 of 215 =====

B.9. Lagrange-reduced bases in a Delaunay triangulation


    Consider a Delaunay triangle with vertices P0 , P1 , and P2 . Collect the two edge vectors
incident at P0 in the edge matrix A = (a1 , a2 ), where

                                          −−−→                 −−−→
                                    a 1 = P0 P1 ,         a2 = P0 P2 .                       (B.104)


                                               P2

                                                           a2 − a 1
                                          a2


                                     P0             a1            P1
                                                                            −−−→           −−−→
Figure B.1: Triangle with vertices P0 , P1 , and P2 , and edge vectors a1 = P0 P1 and a2 = P0 P2 .

   The triangle contains no other lattice point in its interior or on the relative interior of an
edge, since any such point would lie inside its circumcircle. An invertible linear map sending a
basis of Λ to the standard basis of Z2 preserves the lattice-point counts and scales areas by 1/SΛ .
Pick’s theorem [206] therefore gives, for interior and boundary counts nint and nbdry ,

                                      S           nbdry
                                         = nint +       − 1,                                 (B.105)
                                      SΛ            2

where SΛ is the area of a fundamental cell. Here, nint = 0 and nbdry = 3, so the triangle has area
SΛ /2. The parallelogram spanned by its two incident edge vectors therefore has area SΛ , thus
these vectors form a basis of Λ.
    To show that the triangle is non-obtuse, translate P0 to the origin and write its circumcircle
as
                                        ∥x∥2 − q·x = 0,                                    (B.106)

for some q ∈ R2 . Since a1 and a2 lie on the circle,

                                 q·a1 = ∥a1 ∥2 ,          q·a2 = ∥a2 ∥2 .                    (B.107)

The fourth vertex a1 + a2 of the corresponding parallelogram is also a lattice point, and its power
with respect to the circumcircle is

                                ∥a1 + a2 ∥2 − q·(a1 + a2 ) = 2a1 ·a2 .                       (B.108)

If the angle at P0 were obtuse, then a1 ·a2 < 0, placing a1 + a2 strictly inside the circumcircle and
contradicting the Delaunay property. Thus the angle at P0 is non-obtuse. Applying the same
argument at P1 and P2 shows that every angle of the triangle is non-obtuse. Equivalently, the
edge-pair metric belongs to DT at every vertex, as derived in Appendix B.8.
    It remains to identify a Lagrange-reduced basis. Relabel the vertices, if necessary, so that P0
is opposite a longest side, and retain the notation in Eq. (B.104). The Gram matrix of the two



                                                    191

===== PDF page 203 of 215 =====

Appendix B. Proofs and derivations


incident edge vectors is
                                                  !                      !
                                        G11 G12           ∥a1 ∥2 a1 ·a2
                           G=A A=
                                T
                                                      =                 .                  (B.109)
                                        G12 G22           a1 ·a2 ∥a2 ∥2

Order these edges by length. Since the triangle is non-obtuse, Appendix B.8 gives

                                             G ∈ DL .                                      (B.110)

This is the ordered Lagrange domain introduced in Eq. (3.5), and precisely Engel’s two-dimensional
Lagrange-reduction condition [141]. Hence a1 is a shortest nonzero vector of Λ and a2 is a shortest
lattice vector linearly independent of a1 . The two edges incident at P0 therefore form a Lagrange-
reduced basis. The argument applies to every triangle of the Delaunay triangulation of Λ.


B.10      Selecting the orientation of the canonical reference element
Use fixed Cartesian axes for the reference and current configurations, and also denote the matrix
of F in these axes by F. Let D contain as columns the two current edges u and v from the
vertex opposite the longest side. Assume that the current triangle is nondegenerate, so det D ̸= 0.
For each of the four square-lattice orientations, D(0) contains the corresponding perpendicular
unit reference edges. We consider both assignments of these edges to u and v, retaining the one
with det D det D(0) > 0. Since D(0) is orthogonal, this guarantees det F > 0 for every retained
candidate.
    The deformation gradient and the dot-product score for each retained assignment are

                                                          (0)      (0)
                      F = D(D(0) )T ,     salign = u · d1 + v · d2 = tr F.                 (B.111)

Write the polar decomposition as F = R(θ)U, where θ ∈ [−π, π] is the principal rotation angle
and U is positive definite. Because U is symmetric,

                                salign = tr(R(θ)U) = tr(U) cos θ.                          (B.112)

Right multiplication by the orthogonal (D(0) )T leaves the singular values of D unchanged. Thus
tr(U) is the same positive number for every candidate, and maximizing salign is equivalent to
minimizing |θ|.
    The minimizing candidate need not be unique in a symmetric configuration. For an exact
tie, the implementation retains the first admissible candidate in its fixed ordering of reference
orientations and edge assignments.

RÉSUMÉ

La plasticité cristalline est gouvernée par le mouvement, l’interaction et l’organisation collective des dislocations. Aux
grandes échelles, ces mécanismes microscopiques sont généralement représentés par des lois constitutives continues.
À l’échelle atomique, ils peuvent être résolus explicitement, mais seulement sur des échelles de longueur et de temps
limitées. Entre ces deux limites se trouve un régime mésoscopique dans lequel la plasticité reste discrète, collective,
intermittente et dépendante de l’histoire. Cette thèse étudie ce régime à l’aide du Modèle Tensoriel Mésoscopique
(MTM), un modèle énergétique construit à partir d’une densité d’énergie invariante par les symétries du réseau cristallin
et implémenté ici dans un cadre éléments finis.
Dans une formulation purement lagrangienne, le maillage peut subir un verrouillage topologique lorsque les éléments
deviennent fortement distordus. Nous développons une procédure de reconnexion, fondée sur des basculements locaux
d’arêtes, afin de maintenir une topologie adaptée à la configuration déformée tout en préservant l’information plastique
pertinente. Cette approche supprime les artefacts associés à un maillage fixe et donne accès, aux grandes déformations
plastiques, à un régime intermittent quasi stationnaire marqué par la formation de grains.
La deuxième contribution de la thèse est une construction unique par plus court chemin permettant de sélectionner la
décomposition élastoplastique à valeurs entières du MTM pour un réseau carré. La branche plastique est sélectionnée
comme la plus courte séquence admissible de cisaillements simples de SL(2, Z) reliant la métrique courante au domaine
élastique fondamental. Nous montrons que cette sélection est unique, sauf sur les frontières entre puits, où apparaît
la dégénérescence attendue entre branches voisines. Cette construction fournit une règle cohérente pour transporter
l’histoire plastique lors des changements de représentation du maillage.
Enfin, ce travail analyse les avalanches plastiques produites sous chargement quasi statique. Les statistiques des chutes
d’énergie sont utilisées pour caractériser leur intermittence et leur comportement critique, avec une attention particulière
portée à l’extraction des exposants critiques. Nous montrons que les statistiques d’avalanches ne sont que faiblement
affectées par le choix de l’algorithme de minimisation et par la reconnexion du maillage. Cette faible sensibilité suggère
un comportement critique robuste.


MOTS CLÉS

plasticité cristalline ; dislocations ; modèle tensoriel mésoscopique ; décomposition élastoplastique ; histoire
plastique ; reconnexion de maillage ; avalanches plastiques ; criticalité.



ABSTRACT

Crystal plasticity is governed by the motion, interaction and collective organization of dislocations. At large scales, these
microscopic mechanisms are usually represented by continuum constitutive laws. At the atomic scale, they can be
resolved explicitly, but only over limited length and time scales. Between these two limits lies a mesoscopic regime in
which plasticity remains discrete, collective, intermittent and history-dependent. This thesis studies this regime using the
Mesoscopic Tensorial Model (MTM), an energy-based model built from a lattice-symmetry-invariant energy density and
implemented here within a finite-element framework.
In a purely Lagrangian formulation, the mesh can undergo topological locking when elements become strongly distorted.
We develop a reconnection procedure, based on local edge flips, to maintain a topology adapted to the deformed configu-
ration while preserving the relevant plastic information. This approach removes artifacts associated with a fixed mesh and
gives access, at large plastic deformation, to a quasi-stationary intermittent regime marked by grain-like organization.
The second contribution of the thesis is a unique shortest-path construction for selecting the integer-valued elastoplastic
decomposition of the square MTM. The plastic branch is selected as the shortest admissible sequence of SL(2, Z) simple
shears connecting the current metric to the fundamental elastic domain. We show that this selection is unique, except on
well boundaries, where the expected degeneracy between neighboring branches appears. This construction provides a
consistent rule for transporting plastic history during changes of mesh representation.
Finally, this work analyzes plastic avalanches produced under quasistatic loading. The statistics of energy drops are
used to characterize their intermittency and critical behavior, with particular attention paid to the extraction of critical
exponents. We show that avalanche statistics are only weakly affected by the choice of minimization algorithm and by
mesh reconnection. This weak sensitivity suggests a robust critical behavior.


KEYWORDS

crystal plasticity; dislocations; Mesoscopic Tensorial Model; elastoplastic decomposition; plastic history;
mesh reconnection; plastic avalanches; criticality.
